[15] BleepingComputer - Five warning signs of ransomware breach before encryption or data theft
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 10:00:10 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/five-warning-signs-of-ransomware-breach-before-encryption-or-data-theft/

[TAM METİN - 1358 kelime]
Ask many IT teams how they would recognize ransomware, and the answer is still “when files start changing extensions.” By then, the intrusion may already include credential theft, privilege escalation, defense evasion, recovery sabotage, lateral movement and data exfiltration.

Encryption is often a late-stage action, not the beginning of the attack. In data-extortion attacks it never happens at all: the operators steal data and threaten to leak it without encrypting anything.

Recent attacks against on-premises SharePoint servers illustrate that sequence. After exploiting internet-facing systems, operators used stolen credentials and legitimate administration tools to move through victim environments before deploying ransomware.

The practical lesson is simple: defenders need to detect the behaviors that precede encryption, not only the encryptor itself.

Acronis Cyber Platform (formerly Acronis Cyber Protect Cloud) natively integrates cybersecurity, data protection and endpoint management through a single agent, console and policy framework.

When the relevant capabilities are licensed and enabled, Acronis Active Protection, EDR, MDR and XDR can help detect, contain and recover from ransomware-related activity before widespread encryption occurs.

Five high-value signals deserve immediate attention:

Defense tampering: Attempts to stop security services, modify protection agents, load vulnerable drivers or clear event logs.

Privilege escalation and lateral movement: Unexpected privileged accounts, remote-execution tools spreading across hosts or unusual server-to-server remote access.

Data exfiltration: Unusual outbound transfers from systems that do not normally upload large volumes of data.

Recovery sabotage: Deletion of shadow copies, backup catalogs or recovery options with tools such as vssadmin.exe, wbadmin.exe or bcdedit.exe.

Pre-encryption file behavior: Rapid file enumeration, repeated read-write-rename activity and encryption-like changes across local or network locations.

How do ransomware gangs typically gain initial access to corporate networks?

Ransomware attacks rarely begin with the ransomware payload itself. Operators first need an initial foothold, and common entry paths include exploitation of vulnerable internet-facing systems, abuse of stolen credentials, and social engineering.

An unpatched externally accessible service can provide a route into the environment, while compromised credentials can allow an attacker to enter through legitimate remote-access or cloud services.

Social engineering adds another path by targeting users and the processes around identity and access.

Once attackers establish access, the initial compromise can become the starting point for credential theft, privilege escalation, defense evasion and lateral movement.

Organizations should reduce unnecessary internet exposure, prioritize patching of high-risk systems, strengthen identity and access controls, and protect email and collaboration channels.

Monitoring endpoints for suspicious post-compromise behavior provides another layer when preventive controls do not stop the initial intrusion.

The article already establishes these pre-encryption stages as important signals defenders should detect.

Acronis Cyber Platform supports this layered approach from one platform.

On the detection and response side, endpoint protection, built on Acronis Active Protection and EDR, identifies and contains malicious activity once an attacker gains a foothold, while email security and XDR extend that detection to the attack surface beyond the endpoint.

On the prevention side, vulnerability assessment and patch management reduce exploitable exposure, security posture management and security policy management close configuration gaps, and data loss prevention limits what an intruder can take.

Together these create a progression from reducing opportunities for initial compromise to detecting and responding to activity after one occurs.

What are the warning signs of ransomware before encryption or data theft?

Encryption happens late in a ransomware attack, and data-extortion attacks may skip it entirely. Earlier warning signs include defense tampering, privilege escalation, data exfiltration, recovery sabotage and pre-encryption file activity.

Learn how to detect each signal, respond quickly and recover.

How does ransomware protection prevent an attack instead of only detecting it?

Stopping ransomware is rarely about a single control. Organizations need to reduce the opportunities attackers have to get in, detect and contain malicious activity when they do, and have a reliable way to recover if systems or data are affected.

That makes prevention, detection and response, and recovery different parts of the same ransomware defense strategy.

Acronis brings those layers together in one platform.

On the prevention side, patch management reduces exploitable exposure, security posture management and unified endpoint and security policy management close configuration gaps before an attacker can use them, data loss prevention limits what can leave the environment, and data protection with immutable backups removes the leverage that recovery sabotage depends on.

On the detection and response side, Active Protection, EDR, XDR and email security identify and contain malicious activity across endpoints, email and the wider environment. Integrated backup and recovery then provide the path to restoring affected systems and data.

That last layer is critical for getting operations back online, but it does not undo data theft, an important distinction in double-extortion attacks.

How do detection tools stop ransomware before it encrypts files?

Ransomware rarely goes straight to encryption. Endpoint detection tools can spot the suspicious activity that comes first, from attempts to disable defenses to lateral movement and unusual file activity. The table below breaks down five signals worth watching, the controls that can address them and how Acronis supports each response.

Acronis implementation

Alert on recovery-tool abuse; stop the malicious process; maintain off-host, immutable recovery points.

Active Protection blocks ransomware behavior and restores affected files. Self-protection helps defend Acronis components and backups. Immutable Acronis Cloud Storage protects eligible recovery points.

Use agent self-protection, exploit prevention, vulnerable-driver controls and off-host logging.

Self-protection helps prevent unauthorized changes to the Acronis agent. EDR/XDR and SIEM integrations add incident visibility, subject to configuration.

Privilege and lateral movement

Monitor privileged-account changes and remote-execution activity; isolate affected endpoints.

EDR/XDR correlates activity across workloads, presents the attack chain and supports endpoint isolation and response actions.

Monitor unusual data flows and apply controls appropriate to  systems and users, based on least-privilege principle.

XDR correlates endpoint activity with supported telemetry sources. Acronis DLP controls sensitive-data transfers on supported endpoints.

Pre-encryption file behavior

Use behavioral detection and automated rollback; protect local data, network folders and servers.

Active Protection uses behavioral and AI/ML-based detection to stop ransomware and restore affected files. Network folder and server-side protection extend coverage where supported.

What should an organization do immediately after detecting ransomware?

The first actions should limit spread, preserve evidence and create a safe path to recovery. Organizations should pre-authorize automated actions for clearly defined, high-severity conditions and test them before an incident.

Isolate the affected workload while preserving the management channel needed for investigation.

Stop malicious processes and quarantine confirmed malicious files.

Protect evidence, including process trees, command lines, network connections and relevant logs.

Identify and disable the compromised account, token or remote-access path.

Patch the exploited vulnerability or close the exposed service after containment.

Restore affected files or workloads from a known-good recovery point, then validate the recovered environment before reconnecting it.

Escalate to incident-response, legal, privacy, insurance and communications stakeholders according to the organization’s response plan.

Acronis EDR and XDR support investigation, isolation and response actions from the incident view. Recovery actions depend on the applicable backup and recovery licenses, available recovery points and configuration.

Acronis MDR by Acronis TRU adds 24/7 expert monitoring, investigation and response; managed remediation and recovery are included in the Advanced service tier within the agreed scope and permissions.

Turn pre-encryption signals into tested controls

The value of an early ransomware warning depends on what happens next. The five signals above can give defenders an opportunity to contain an attack before encryption spreads, but only if the response is already defined and tested.

Whether it is an attempt to delete shadow copies, data exfiltration, unexpected remote execution or a sudden burst of encryption-like file activity, suspicious behavior should trigger a clear containment and recovery plan.

Acronis Cyber Platform can connect prevention, detection, response and recovery through one integrated platform. Capabilities vary by edition, deployment model, configuration and licensing.

[16] BleepingComputer - Critical NGINX ‘NGINX2Root’ bugs let attackers hijack servers
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 09:57:24 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/critical-nginx-nginx2root-bugs-let-attackers-hijack-servers/

[TAM METİN - 702 kelime]
Two critical NGINX vulnerabilities dubbed "nginx2root" allow unauthenticated attackers to execute code remotely on vulnerable web servers, with researchers demonstrating how the flaws can be chained with a Linux kernel bug to gain root access.

The two HTTP/2 vulnerabilities were discovered independently by researchers at Xint, Wiz, Ikotas Labs, Metnir, and Rapid7, while Xint also discovered three additional NGINX RCE flaws.

Censys later reproduced the underlying memory corruption conditions in four of the five flaws through lab testing, but did not achieve remote code execution.

The first of the two "nginx2root" flaws is CVE-2026-123007, a flow-control bug that can corrupt memory in an NGINX worker. It impacts all versions from 1.31.8 down to 1.9.5, released in 2015.

Wiz demonstrated unauthenticated remote code execution on a server hosting ordinary static files over HTTP/2 with TLS, while Xint demoed root access by chaining it with a separate Linux kernel flaw.

Xint says the separate vulnerability affects the Linux kernel's SCTP subsystem and can be used to escape an NGINX Docker container and gain root privileges on the underlying host.

The second flaw is CVE-2026-123633, a use-after-free problem that affects NGINX when it accepts HTTP/2 connections and proxies requests to a backend. It impacts all versions from 1.19.7 through 1.31.8.

When the worker runs low on connection slots, it can close an active connection and free memory that a request still uses. Wiz also demonstrated remote code execution via CVE-2026-123633, but the firm is not publicly releasing its proof-of-concept (PoC) exploits at this time.

The other three flaws the researchers discovered and F5 fixed are CVE-2026-121810, CVE-2026-123634, and CVE-2026-113425, all use-after-free vulnerabilities affecting HTTP/3 reverse proxying, DNS resolution with subrequests, and mail proxy authentication, respectively.

The flaws were fixed in NGINX Open Source 1.30.7 stable and 1.31.9 mainline. NGINX Plus users should update to versions 37.0.8.1 or 37.3.0.1.

F5’s latest security update also addresses five more vulnerabilities not highlighted in the Wiz or Xint reports.

The most notable is CVE-2026-118899, a high-severity issue that allows an unauthenticated attacker to cause a worker to enter an infinite loop while reading a request body over TLS 1.3, resulting in denial of service.

For system administrators, it is recommended to upgrade immediately to the latest NGINX release, or temporarily disable HTTP/2 until an upgrade is possible. However, this mitigation only covers the two "nginx2root" flaws.

Regarding the real exploitability risk of "nginx2root," security researcher Florian Roth says it's worse than Node2Shell and Java2Shell, but doesn’t appear to be an imminent threat for most NGINX deployments.

Commenting on X, Roth said the flaws are exploitable in non-default configurations, warning that claims of widespread exposure based solely on NGINX version numbers are misleading.

Xint previously reported that 98.9% of NGINX hosts disclosing recognizable versions were running software within the affected range, but this does not account for whether HTTP/2 is enabled or security fixes have been backported.

The two HTTP/2 flaws require an unpatched server with HTTP/2 enabled on a listener an attacker can reach, while CVE-2026-123633 also requires NGINX to proxy requests to a backend.

Roth scanned a sample of a few hundred NGINX HTTPS endpoints from Shodan and found that roughly 66% of them support HTTP/2, but confirming actual exposure requires checking the server’s configuration and whether a fix has been backported.

According to Censys' live exposure tracker, approximately 8.8 million NGINX services were potentially exposed to CVE-2026-123007 as of November 19, based on affected version numbers and HTTP/2 support.

The company reports that more than 511,000 previously exposed services have already been patched or mitigated, while another 104 million NGINX services do not disclose their versions, making it difficult to determine whether they are affected.

[17] BleepingComputer - Drupal patches critical RCE flaw, warns exploits may surface quickly
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 08:08:08 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/drupal-patches-critical-rce-flaw-warns-exploits-may-surface-quickly/

[TAM METİN - 473 kelime]
The Drupal Security Team has patched a critical remote code execution vulnerability that can let attackers take over vulnerable sites.

This content management system (CMS) is commonly used by large organizations that manage multi-site installations and massive data structures, including government entities, educational organizations, major research universities, and high-profile enterprise and media organizations.

The security flaw (tracked as CVE-2026-132165) was discovered by Anthropic's OSS Scanner, and anonymous users can exploit it to reinstall the Drupal CMS.

"Drupal core ships with an install.php file for installing Drupal core. Under specific conditions, an attacker can use the installer to attempt to reinstall Drupal. This can lead to a site takeover or remote code execution," the Drupal Security Team warned in a Wednesday advisory. "Some exploit paths are mitigated if the web server cannot write to or change permissions on settings.php."

Admins whose websites use the paid Drupal Steward Web Application Firewall (WAF) service are already protected from known attack vectors, but should still upgrade to Drupal 10.6.22, 11.3.22, or 11.4.13 in case other attack vectors are discovered.

Those who can't immediately upgrade should secure the Drupal installer by blocking access to /core/install.php in the web server configuration or the .htaccess file in the web root.

On Wednesday, Drupal also addressed a moderately critical gadget chain vulnerability (CVE-2026-132166) that attackers with admin privileges can exploit to gain remote code execution on a targeted site if the application deserializes untrusted data due to another security flaw.

"The Drupal Security Team urges you to reserve time for core updates at that time because exploits might be developed within hours or days," the security team said in a Monday advance warning. "The vulnerabilities are currently rated as critical and at least one can be exploited by anonymous users."

In May, Drupal also confirmed that attackers were actively exploiting an SQL injection vulnerability (CVE-2026-9082) in the database abstraction API that could let them escalate privileges, access sensitive information, or achieve remote code execution on unpatched servers.

More recently, Drupal warned that a critical access bypass vulnerability (CVE-2026-118539) in the Commerce Core module is being abused in attacks.

The US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) has flagged 5 Drupal vulnerabilities as exploited in the wild since November 2021, two of which have also been abused in ransomware attacks.

[18] BleepingComputer - Microsoft tests new Edge Lockdown Mode that disables GPU APIs
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 06:46:00 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-tests-new-edge-lockdown-mode-that-disables-gpu-apis/

[TAM METİN - 489 kelime]
​Microsoft is developing a new Edge security feature to reduce the attack surface by disabling the WebGL, WebGL2, and WebGPU APIs.

Inspired by Apple's Lockdown Mode, the new feature shares the same name and builds on Edge Enhanced Security Mode (also known internally as Super Duper Secure Mode), which disables Just-In-Time Compilation (JIT) from the V8 processing pipeline.

The new Edge Lockdown Mode offers optional, extreme protections for users facing highly targeted attacks who are willing to sacrifice website compatibility for stronger defenses.

"Lockdown Mode is designed for users who are at particular risk of being targeted in cybersecurity campaigns. This may include journalists, executives, security researchers, and administrators," said Partner Security Group Manager Gareth Evans.

"Individuals whose threat model justifies accepting reduced compatibility in exchange for stronger protection. It may also apply to devices operating within highly restricted environments."

Evans also warned that websites that use the disabled APIs (especially those with interactive graphics like games or maps) may not work as expected when this security feature is toggled on.

Edge users can test Lockdown Mode by switching to the Canary release channel, enabling the #edge-sdsm-plus flag, restarting the browser, then toggling on Strict mode for "Enhance your security on the web" and blocking WebGL graphics and WebGPU graphics.

After enabling it, users will see a new "Lockdown Enabled" indicator in the address bar when WebGL or WebGPU is blocked on a web page.

​While the current Edge Canary Lockdown Mode blocks websites from using WebGL, WebGL2, and WebGPU APIs to stop attacks that try to escalate privileges and escape the sandbox, Microsoft plans to extend the feature in upcoming releases.

"The initial set of Lockdown Mode mitigations focus is the GPU process, a more privileged component that handles complex graphics workloads across browser code, third-party libraries, and device drivers,"  Evans added.

"Results from this experiment will inform the finished Lockdown Mode experience, including clearer descriptions of restricted capabilities and site-level exceptions that restore trusted sites without weakening protection everywhere else."

According to the company's estimates, this new security feature would have blocked around 60% of privilege escalation attacks targeting Edge users in the wild.

Last year, Microsoft also introduced a new Edge scareware sensor to ensure Defender SmartScreen blocks scam pages faster, and restricted access to Edge's Internet Explorer mode after learning that hackers were leveraging zero-day exploits in the Chakra JavaScript engine to access target devices.

[19] BleepingComputer - Microsoft confirms app installation, launch issues after hotpatch
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 05:35:05 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/microsoft/microsoft-confirms-app-installation-launch-issues-after-hotpatch/

[TAM METİN - 514 kelime]
Microsoft has shared a workaround for installation and launch issues affecting Office and third-party applications after installing this month's Windows 11 hotpatch.

In a service alert seen by BleepingComputer, Microsoft told IT administrators that these problems may appear during installation, launch, or even during normal app use on systems running Windows 11 24H2 and later.

"Devices which installed the November 2026 hotpatch update (the Originating KBs listed above), might experience an error when installing, launching, or running some applications. Resulting from this issue, errors similar to "ERROR_ALLOTTED_SPACE_EXCEEDED (0xC0000099)" are displayed," Microsoft said.

"Current investigation suggests this issue occurs for applications which load the 32-bit Windows Installer DLL, found as msi.dll. This can happen more frequently with 32-bit applications, but can also occur for 64-bit applications under certain conditions."

Microsoft added that this known issue should not impact systems that have installed the standard November 2026 Patch Tuesday security updates, but only those that have installed the hotpatch updates pushed through the Windows Autopatch, the company's cloud-based enterprise service that automates Windows and Microsoft 365 software and security updates.

Until a fix is available, Microsoft has shared a workaround to help IT admins temporarily mitigate the bug on affected systems.

To do that, they have to edit the Windows Registry to increase the HotPatchTableSize using the following procedure:

Open the Windows Registry by opening the Start menu, typing "regedit", and selecting Registry Editor from the results.

Using the folders and address path, navigate to the HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Session Manager\Memory Management folder.

In the right-hand panel, locate the "HotPatchTableSize" registry key, open it, and set the Value to "2000". Click OK.

Close the registry editor and restart the device.

"Before you modify the registry, back it up and make sure that you know how to restore it if a problem occurs. For more information, see How to back up and restore the registry in Windows," Microsoft said.

Microsoft said in March that it would turn on hotpatch security updates by default for all eligible Windows devices managed through the Microsoft Graph API and Microsoft Intune, after the May 2026 Windows security updates are installed.

One month later, it fixed a Windows Autopatch bug that caused driver updates restricted by administrative policies to deploy on some Autopatch-managed Windows devices belonging to customers in the European Union.

Microsoft first announced Windows Autopatch in April 2002, saying it would be available for free to customers with a Windows 10/11 Enterprise E3 license or greater starting in July 2022. The feature reached public preview in early June and has been generally available since July 2022.

[20] BleepingComputer - NVIDIA investigating Gentlemen ransomware breach claims
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 04:23:21 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/nvidia-investigating-gentlemen-ransomware-breach-claims/

[TAM METİN - 424 kelime]
NVIDIA is investigating claims of a security breach after the Gentlemen ransomware group has added the company to its dark web data leak site.

Although the ransomware operation now lists the American multinational tech giant, it has not yet shared any proof of the breach.

On their leak site, the cybercrime gang threatens to release data and has added a countdown that ends in nine days, on November 28.

While the company told BleepingComputer it hasn't found evidence that its systems were compromised, it said it's now looking into a separate incident that may have affected one of its service providers.

"NVIDIA is investigating an incident that may have affected a third-party partner," an NVIDIA spokesperson told BleepingComputer when asked to confirm the cybercrime gang's claims.

"We have found no indication that NVIDIA systems or networks were affected. We continue to monitor the situation."

​NVIDIA confirmed a data breach earlier this year, in May, when it told BleepingComputer that some Armenian users' information (including their names, email addresses, phone numbers, dates of birth, and usernames) had been exposed after a regional GeForce NOW Alliance partner was hacked.

However, the company added that the May breach did not affect its network or NVIDIA-operated services.

The tech giant also disclosed another data breach in March 2022 (claimed by the Lapsus$ extortion gang) that affected more than 71,000 employees after attackers accessed its network a month earlier.

The Gentlemen surfaced last year as a closed ransomware group and then switched to a Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) model, offering affiliates up to a 90% cut of ransom proceeds (compared to the typical 70%–80% other ransomware groups offer).

It operates as a double-extortion group, exfiltrating sensitive data from victims' networks before encrypting their systems and pressuring them with the threat of leaking the stolen information online.

Since it emerged, The Gentlemen has claimed more than 1,100 victims from various sectors, spanning manufacturing, healthcare, transportation, energy, education, financial services, and more.

[21] Security Affairs - European Commission Cyberattack: ARTEX AI Tool Suspected in Prolonged Disruption
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 15:06:40 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203483/hacking/european-commission-cyberattack-artex-ai-tool-suspected-in-prolonged-disruption.html

[TAM METİN - 733 kelime]
European Commission Cyberattack: ARTEX AI Tool Suspected in Prolonged Disruption

A cyberattack disrupted EU Commission services for over ten days. ARTEX is suspected, while investigators assess whether staff data was stolen.

A cyberattack has disrupted internal European Commission systems for more than ten days, hitting payroll, leave requests and medical reimbursements. Politico’s Brussels Playbook reports the Corporate Management Board, the Commission’s top internal crisis body, met urgently over the incident, and tech commissioner Henna Virkkunen briefed the full College of Commissioners on 18 November. An EU official told Politico the Commission believes it has contained most of the attack, while the full extent of any damage remains unclear.

Sysper, the HR platform for payslips, timesheets and leave, crashed briefly again on 18 November after access had been restored earlier in the week. MyPMO, the medical reimbursement system, came back online around the same time. JSIS, the staff health insurance portal, was still down on 19 November according to a Commission employee cited by Euronews, and translation services went offline on 17 November, prompting one official to admit to “swearing a lot.”

Pay still landed on time, according to staff Politico spoke to, even as some couldn’t submit timesheets or request leave. Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier confirmed the response to Politico: “Individual systems were targeted and, in line with our standard practice, we have informed those concerned.”

Some staff still complained the internal communication said little, and an IT reply about the Sysper problem cited a general technical issue without mentioning an attack.

CERT-EU, the cybersecurity team that protects EU institutions, detected the activity and identified ARTEX, an AI-powered penetration-testing tool, as a possible tool used in the attack. An EU official told Politico that some threat actors misuse tools like ARTEX and have targeted the Commission. In a technical note published on 5 November, CERT-EU described ARTEX as a system that uses AI agents. An operator gives it an objective in plain language, and the software breaks it down into smaller tasks that the agents carry out using real hacking tools.

ARTEX does not include its own AI model. Instead, it connects to an external AI model selected by the operator. Its original developer removed the project from its repository in October after reports of misuse. However, independent copies are still available, so removing the original project does not prevent others from using it. So far, there is no public information explaining how the attackers entered the Commission’s systems. The reference to ARTEX therefore points to a suspected tool, not a confirmed attack method or the identity of those responsible.

The biggest unanswered question is whether staff data was stolen. This could include names, salary details, home addresses or health information. Investigators have not yet confirmed whether anyone accessed this data, which explains why the Commission has shared little information with employees.

Taking systems offline or restoring them does not prove that no data was stolen. Getting a service back online only shows that it is working again. It does not mean attackers did not copy information before the system was shut down.

Under EU Regulation 2018/1725, a confirmed personal data breach must normally be reported to the European Data Protection Supervisor within 72 hours. If the breach creates a high risk for individuals, they must also be informed without delay. However, these deadlines only start once a personal data breach has been confirmed.

This is not the Commission’s first security incident this year, although no link between the cases has been established. On January 30, attackers left traces in the system used to manage staff mobile devices. Some names and phone numbers were exposed. The Commission said it contained and cleaned up the incident within nine hours.

A more serious incident happened in March. Attackers compromised a software update for Trivy, a security tool, and used it to enter an AWS cloud environment. They stole around 91.7 GB of compressed data, including personal information and email content. The breach may also have affected at least 29 other EU institutions and bodies.

The timeline shows that the Commission restored its systems before it fully understood what had happened. Services came back online in stages, but key questions remain unanswered: where did the attack start, which systems were affected, and was any data stolen, and who is behind the attack?

[22] Security Affairs - PHP Warns of Critical Flaws Ahead of November Security Release
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 10:22:51 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203472/security/php-warns-of-critical-flaws-ahead-of-november-security-release.html

[TAM METİN - 517 kelime]
PHP Warns of Critical Flaws Ahead of November Security Release

PHP will patch critical security flaws across supported versions, including a session handling bug that could allow remote code execution.

The PHP development team announced a new security release scheduled for November 19, warning that the updates will address vulnerabilities across all supported versions of the widely used server-side programming language. The highest severity rating is CRITICAL, although the project says the affected issues require unusual configurations or non-default setups.

“The PHP security team announces upcoming security releases scheduled for Thursday, 19 November 2026, between 14:00 and 14:30 UTC.” reads the announcement. “The issues rated CRITICAL require either non-default configuration or uncommon setups. Users are strongly encouraged to prepare to upgrade promptly once the releases become available. Technical details and fixed version numbers will be published alongside the releases.”

The announcement places the release within PHP’s regular four-week update cycle.

PHP hasn’t disclosed the technical details, vulnerability identifiers, or fixed version numbers ahead of publication. The maintainers say they’ll release that information alongside the patches, giving administrators a short window to identify affected systems and prepare their upgrades.

One vulnerability expected to be addressed is CVE-2026-115560, a remote code execution flaw involving PHP session handling.

In October, PHP warned of a new code execution vulnerability, tracked as CVE-2026-115560, just two days after releasing emergency patches for the critical PHP2Shell flaws.

The vulnerability affects only a specific PHP configuration that PHP says is unlikely to be used in practice. An application must meet several conditions: it must configure session.serialize_handler as php_binary or php_serialize, change it to php at runtime using ini_set(), and make this change before calling session_start(). Session upload progress must also remain enabled.

In an advisory published on October 23, the project recommended disabling session upload progress until a fix becomes available in a regular release. The vulnerability is separate from PHP2Shell and is not fixed by the October 21 updates for PHP versions 8.2.35, 8.3.36, 8.4.27 and 8.5.12.

The November release also comes less than a month after PHP addressed two other critical vulnerabilities, CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619, collectively known as PHP2Shell. Those flaws let unauthenticated attackers execute code remotely through specially crafted HTTP requests, and they received emergency patches on October 21.

For organizations running PHP-based applications, the immediate priority is to identify the runtime versions in production, review custom session configurations and prepare to deploy the new packages as soon as they’re released. Teams should also check whether their Linux distributions or long-term support providers distribute PHP security fixes independently of the upstream release schedule.

PHP 8.1 and older no longer receive security updates directly from the PHP project, although some Linux distributions and commercial vendors continue to provide backported patches. Organizations still running these versions should verify their vendor’s coverage rather than assume that an upstream release automatically protects their installations.

“Users are strongly encouraged to prepare to upgrade promptly once the releases become available.” concludes the announcement. “Technical details and fixed version numbers will be published alongside the releases.”

[23] Security Affairs - nginx2root:AI Discovers Five Critical nginx Flaws and a Path to Root
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 09:21:19 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203440/hacking/nginx2rootai-discovers-five-critical-nginx-flaws-and-a-path-to-root.html

[TAM METİN - 985 kelime]
nginx2root:AI Discovers Five Critical nginx Flaws and a Path to Root

nginx2root exposes five critical nginx flaws and a Linux kernel bug that can lead to remote code execution and root access.

Xint Research has disclosed nginx2root, five distinct nginx vulnerabilities plus one Linux kernel bug, each with a proven exploit. The worst of the five, now tracked as CVE-2026-123007, lets an unauthenticated attacker run code remotely, and chaining it with the kernel bug reaches root on the host.

“This issue affects NGINX Plus or NGINX Open Source configurations that use HTTP/2. When NGINX processes undisclosed traffic, the flow control window can exceed the allowed threshold, potentially resulting in a heap buffer overflow typically followed by an NGINX worker segmentation fault. Additionally, attackers can execute code on systems with Address Space Layout Randomization (ASLR) disabled or when the attacker can bypass ASLR. (CVE-2026-123007)” reads the advisory. “An attacker could leverage this vulnerability to crash the NGINX worker process, corrupt its memory, or potentially execute code remotely. There is no control plane exposure; this is a data plane issue only.”

Affected nginx versions run from 1.9.5 to 1.31.8, and the fix landed in 1.31.9. According to Zakir Durumeric, Co-Founder and CEO Censys, nearly 10M Internet services are running versions of Nginx vulnerable to the NGINX2Root CVEs.

The bug sits in nginx’s HTTP/2 flow control, and it’s been there since nginx added HTTP/2 in 2015, eleven years ago. An attacker corrupts memory in an nginx worker, leaks memory addresses, and overwrites a pointer to a cleanup function that fires when a connection closes.

“Specifically, the most severe vulnerability is in nginx HTTP/2 flow control. An unauthenticated remote attacker can corrupt memory in an nginx worker, leak memory addresses, and overwrite the pointer to a cleanup function that runs when a connection closes.” reads the advisory. “This can be used to execute commands with worker privileges and receive the output over an existing connection.”

The figures show why this issue matters beyond nginx itself. In a Shodan scan on 10 November, 98.9% of nginx servers with a known version were running a vulnerable release. Xint also found that 88.6% of Fortune 500 companies use nginx. These figures show how widespread the vulnerable versions are, not how many servers can actually be attacked. Some servers may not have HTTP/2 enabled or may already include a fix provided by their software vendor. Still, administrators have a large number of systems to check.

To move from control of an nginx worker to full root access, an attacker needs to exploit a second flaw in the Linux kernel’s SCTP code. Xint tested this attack chain in environments running on AWS, GCP and Azure, including systems reachable through a public IP address. Luxembourg’s cybersecurity response team, CIRCL, also confirmed that exploiting the kernel flaw is necessary to gain root access. No active attacks have been reported so far, and CVE-2026-123007 was not on CISA’s Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog at the time of writing.

The discovery story is worth pausing on. nginx has been fuzzed by the best researchers for two decades, and a pre-auth RCE in it is rare, the last known one dating to 2013.

“nginx runs roughly a third of the web and has been fuzzed and audited by the best researchers in the world for two decades. Pre-auth RCE in nginx almost never happens. The best known one is from 2013. This year every AI lab pointed models at it. Everyone figured the easy bugs were long gone.” continues the advisory. “While testing a new Xint capability ahead of ZeroDay Cloud, a Xint researcher pointed it at a slice of nginx, about 50,000 lines. He picked the target; Xint did the rest. Ninety minutes later it flagged high severity issues with reproduction steps specific enough to become a working exploit in under ten minutes.”

That first find was a human picking the target and an AI doing the digging. What came next was different: Xint folded what it learned into a general feature, not tuned to nginx, and that version found the other four critical bugs with no human guidance. One of those four was also found independently by other researchers around the same time, so Xint wasn’t working in a vacuum.

Four more flaws shipped fixes in the same release, each needing its own specific setup: a backend proxy running low on connections, a proxied server name built from variables combined with certain directives, HTTP/3 turned on, or nginx running as a mail proxy.

All five vulnerabilities have a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2 out of 10, making them critical. NGINX Plus is also affected across several release lines. The fixed versions 1.31.9 and 1.30.7 were released today, along with NGINX Plus versions 37.3.0.1 and 37.0.8.1

Check exposure with a simple test: run openssl s_client with -alpn h2 against your server, and if it answers “ALPN protocol: h2,” HTTP/2 is on. If you’re behind a CDN, test the origin directly, since an origin serving only HTTP/1.1 can’t be hit through HTTP/2 even if your CDN speaks it to visitors. Update to the fixed version and restart nginx fully, because a reload alone leaves the old binary running.

If you can’t patch right away, turn off HTTP/2 on affected listeners as a stopgap. That blocks the worst flaw and one other, but not the remaining three, so it’s a bridge, not a destination. Distribution packages can carry a backported fix without changing the version string nginx -v reports, so check your vendor’s own advisory rather than trusting that number alone.

Xint says the main value of this discovery goes beyond finding these vulnerabilities. It’s about building a process that can quickly turn the next AI-driven attack, similar to ARTEX, into security detections before attackers can take advantage of it.

[24] Recorded Future - Regional Insights: 2027 State of Threat Intelligence
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/2027-soti-regional-summaries

[TAM METİN - 1732 kelime]
Regional Insights: 2027 State of Threat Intelligence

Recorded Future’s 2027 State of Threat Intelligence report surveyed 768 security professionals worldwide on how they use threat intelligence today, how they apply AI to it, and where they plan to invest. The report presents the global findings. The regional executive summaries in this blog cover insights from the same survey, breaking data down by global region into dedicated briefs covering the specific conclusions for cybersecurity leaders in North America, EMEA, APJ, and LATAM. Start with your region to see how your peers are approaching threat intelligence, and read the others for a wider view.

North America (United States and Canada)

In North America, threat intelligence now informs decisions well beyond the security organization. Five teams consume it on average, and 85% of organizations surveyed have teams outside core security using it, including risk management (54%), fraud prevention (44%), and risk and compliance (44%). With intelligence supporting this many teams, 81% plan to invest more in the next 12 months. The challenge is getting the right intelligence to each of those teams in a form they can act on.

Doing that depends on how well intelligence comes together, and this is an ongoing effort for many programs. Most surveyed organizations (63%) draw on four or more intelligence vendors. Only 21% combine that external intelligence with their own internal telemetry in a single platform, where analysts can see which threats apply to their environment. Consolidation plans reflect this. Of the 76% planning to consolidate, the largest share are doing it to improve correlation across signals (40%), compared with only 8% who cite cost.

Moving intelligence from those sources to risk owners is also largely manual. 54% put intelligence into action manually across tools and processes. Among organizations sharing intelligence outside security, 38% rely on ad hoc messages or scheduled reports. Manual handoffs make it harder to see what risk owners did with the intelligence and whether it worked. While 98% measure program value, 28% track whether triggered actions were effective. Only 22% report closed-loop operationalization, where stakeholders act on intelligence, the results are validated, and those results inform the program’s priorities and collection.

AI is already part of this work for nearly all respondents (98%), and 60% use agents that take actions inside their intelligence tools. Leaders become more cautious as the stakes rise: comfort with letting AI act falls from 71% for summarization to 56% for enforcement actions. The leading barriers are lack of auditability (40%) and unproven accuracy (31%). 73% say intelligence regularly feeds their AI and automation systems or is a core input to them. That makes the quality and correlation of their intelligence a factor in how far they can trust AI to act.

North America Field CISO at Recorded Future

This summary draws on responses from 347 North American security professionals. They were part of a global survey of 768 that asked how organizations use threat intelligence, apply AI to it, and plan future investments.

The full report covers how intelligence is expanding across the business, what full visibility requires, and what stands between teams and proven impact.

Europe, Middle East, and Africa (EMEA)

Across EMEA, threat intelligence now informs decisions well beyond the security organization, and leaders plan to extend it further. 86% of organizations surveyed have teams outside core security using it, and 35% name expanding intelligence to more teams as a top priority for the next two years. Budgets support that goal. Half (50%) spend more than $500K a year on external intelligence products, 87% plan to invest more in the next 12 months, and 47% plan to add deepfake or narrative intelligence within six months.

As leaders plan that expansion, the quality of what they share is a central concern. The vendor complaint respondents cite most is generic intelligence that isn’t differentiated from other sources (34%). 66% name higher-fidelity intelligence as a top investment priority. The same focus shows up in consolidation plans. Among the 73% of respondents planning to consolidate, the most common goals are improving correlation (34%) and simplifying operations (28%), while 5% cite cost.

Reaching more teams also raises the question of how each team will use intelligence and how its results will be shown. 60% of respondents have formally documented priority intelligence requirements (PIRs), which define what each stakeholder needs from intelligence and give the program a baseline to measure against. Fewer validate outcomes. 24% track whether triggered actions worked, and 24% have reached closed-loop operationalization. In programs that have “closed the loop”, stakeholders act on intelligence, the results are validated, and those findings feed back into the program’s priorities and collection. For teams new to intelligence, this feedback is one of the clearest ways to connect it to results they recognize.

Governance is also front of mind as AI takes on more intelligence work. 98% of respondents use AI in intelligence work, and 74% are comfortable letting it act on alert triage, but comfort falls to 55% for enforcement actions. The most cited barriers are lack of auditability (37%), skills gaps (34%), and regulatory concerns (34%). These barriers center on the controls and expertise around AI as much as on the technology itself.

CISO at Recorded Future

We surveyed 139 security professionals across EMEA as part of a global study of 768. We asked how they use threat intelligence today, how they apply AI to it, and where they plan to invest next.

Read the full report for the complete findings on expanding intelligence across the business, achieving full visibility, and proving impact.

Asia Pacific and Japan (APJ)

In APJ, threat intelligence now informs decisions well beyond the security organization, and organizations have invested heavily to support it. 76% of organizations surveyed have teams outside core security using it, and five teams consume it on average. 51% spend more than $500K a year on external intelligence products, including 28% who spend more than $1M.

With that much already invested, plans for more spending are measured. 70% plan to invest more in the next 12 months, and 26% have not yet decided. Respondents’ frustrations with vendors center on a lack of clarity around the value they deliver across the business. Poor integration with existing tools (36%) and high or unpredictable cost (33%) top the list, and 28% struggle to extend intelligence to wider business functions. Among the 69% planning to consolidate vendors, the main goals are bringing siloed tools together (38%) and improving correlation (35%).

Proving results will likely shape the next budget cycle, and many APJ programs have a strong foundation to build on. Every respondent measures program value. 30% of respondents report having reached closed-loop operationalization, meaning stakeholders act on intelligence, the results are validated, and those results inform the program’s priorities and collection. Even so, only 48% are very confident quantifying ROI to leadership and finance. Validated operational results do not yet appear to translate into the financial terms budget owners use.

Leaders appear to be applying a similar standard of proof to AI. 97% of respondents in APJ use AI for intelligence work, and 56% plan to expand its role in triage and prioritization. However, before giving AI more autonomy for this work, teams point to lack of auditability (45%), cost (44%), and unproven accuracy (41%) as the leading barriers. For AI, as for the wider program, the next step appears to depend on evidence of outputs and cost justification.

North America Field CISO at Recorded Future

These findings reflect 80 security professionals in APJ. They were drawn from a global survey of 768 that examined how organizations use threat intelligence, adopt AI, and plan investments.

The full report explores each of these themes in depth, from intelligence that serves the whole business to the AI that will help scale it.

Latin America (LATAM)

In LATAM, threat intelligence now informs decisions well beyond the security organization. Across surveyed organizations within the region, five teams consume threat intelligence on average, and 58% deliver it to five or more teams. These include risk management (61%), fraud prevention (57%), finance (22%), and legal (19%). Domain coverage for LATAM threat intelligence programs is just as broad. Most organizations surveyed have fraud intelligence (76%), brand and impersonation monitoring (75%), and vulnerability intelligence (75%) in place.

Coverage this broad often draws on many sources. 69% of respondents use four or more intelligence vendors, and 13% use more than ten. Leaders now want to bring those sources together. 90% of organizations surveyed plan to consolidate, most often to unite siloed tools for visibility across domains (39%). 96% say it is very important or essential for a vendor to provide complete intelligence across threat domains. Today, 34% unify external intelligence and internal telemetry in a single platform.

These programs also have a solid foundation for showing impact. 77% of surveyed organizations have formally documented priority intelligence requirements (PIRs), 42% track whether actions triggered by intelligence worked, and 69% are very confident quantifying ROI to leadership and finance. 25% report having reached closed-loop operationalization, where stakeholders act on intelligence, the results are validated, and those results inform the program’s priorities and collection. With consolidation underway, more programs have a chance to ensure they’re validating the actions stakeholders are taking based off of the threat intelligence program.

AI is already part of daily intelligence work. 94% of respondents use it often or daily for at least one intelligence task. The most cited barrier to giving AI more autonomy is regulatory or compliance concern (37%), followed by lack of auditability and unproven accuracy (35% each). Expanding AI’s role will likely depend as much on oversight as on capability.

CISO at Recorded Future

To build this picture, we surveyed 202 security professionals across Latin America as part of a global study of 768. The survey covered how organizations use threat intelligence, adopt AI, and plan future investments.

The full report details how intelligence is expanding across the business, what full visibility requires, and what stands between teams and proven impact.

Each region is at a different point, but the direction is the same. Threat intelligence now serves the whole business, and getting full value from it requires visibility across the risk surface, a way to prove outcomes, and AI that teams can trust. Your region’s summary shows where your peers stand today. The full report explains what it will take to move forward. Read the 2027 State of Threat Intelligence report.

[25] Cyberscoop - Sean Cairncross out as national cyber director
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 01:07:07 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/sean-cairncross-out-national-cyber-director/

[TAM METİN - 627 kelime]
Sean Cairncross out as national cyber director

Sean Cairncross is leaving his post as national cyber director, CyberScoop has learned.

Cairncross led the Office of the National Cyber Director, which coordinates cybersecurity policy and strategy across the federal government. Though he had experience in Republican politics and national security, he lacked a technical or cyber background. His office nevertheless became involved in key administration decisions on AI security and the protection of critical infrastructure.

A source close to the White House told CyberScoop that President Donald Trump fully supported firing Cairncross from his position as he lacked the strong cyber credentials to perform the job.

Department of Defense Chief Information Officer Kirsten Davies will move into an acting role in the White House office, a transition that comes amid staff departures and debate over how the government should manage artificial intelligence risks.

CyberScoop has reached out to the Pentagon for comment.

In a post on X, Davies thanked Trump and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller for the appointment. “Our nation stands in the crossroad of analog, digital, and quantum technologies, our American way of living increasingly interconnected. Our opportunity is to dominate the cyber landscape for generations to come …but only if we modernize and drive resilience into the foundations our great nation runs on, as well as build cyber talent for our future,” she wrote. “Under President Trump’s leadership we will have a more secure, resilient; and supercharged technological future.”

Cairncross met with technology company leaders and helped shape a June executive order that asked leading AI firms to submit new models for voluntary government testing before public release. He also was assigned to co-lead a working group tracking a nonbinding AI security commitment signed by major labs.

Cairncross advocated for collaboration between government and industry, arguing that security measures should not slow the development of AI. The administration has sought to deploy AI in critical infrastructure as a way to strengthen defenses, while lawmakers and some executives have called for stronger government guardrails. President Donald Trump has rejected new regulation.

The office’s work extended beyond AI policy. It launched a program offering cybersecurity resources to water utilities in Texas after attacks on water systems and a six-month pilot in South Carolina aimed at rural hospitals. Project Lifeline 250 connects companies, state agencies and local governments to help hospitals strengthen network defenses, keep critical care operating after an incident and replace vulnerable or aging equipment.

The projects reflect the challenges facing sectors with limited technology resources. Rural hospitals, like water utilities, often have fewer resources than other critical infrastructure operators. Federal officials have also cited healthcare as a major target for ransomware and other cyberattacks.

The leadership change at ONCD follows a period of staff turnover. Cairncross’ policy chief and senior adviser Thomas Lind joined OpenAI in October. Other senior staff members departed after the administration released its AI executive order, including an adviser involved in early policy negotiations.

The turnover comes as the administration attempts to fill other cyber leadership posts. Michael LoSapio, Trump’s nominee to lead the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, faced a contentious confirmation hearing Thursday. CISA helps protect federal civilian networks and critical infrastructure.

Cairncross’s departure also comes after Trump administration officials blamed a spate of recent tech outages on a race to close vulnerabilities uncovered by artificial intelligence.

[26] Cyberscoop - Dems call CISA nominee ‘disqualified’ after nomination hearing
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 17:11:34 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/michael-losapio-cisa-confirmation-hearing/

[TAM METİN - 733 kelime]
Dems call CISA nominee ‘disqualified’ after nomination hearing

Michael LoSapio, the Palantir executive and Trump administration nominee for Director of the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency, struck a combative tone with Democrats on the Homeland Security and Government Affairs Committee and took sides in several partisan disputes that are likely to complicate his path to confirmation.

LoSapio’s hearing lasted less than an hour, far less time than Congress has spent scrutinizing previous CISA nominees, as he was considered alongside a large tranche of other Trump administration nominees that Republicans are attempting to move through Congress during the lame duck session.

CISA has lacked a confirmed director for the first two years of the Trump administration, and policymakers have fresh concerns about cyber insecurity in U.S. critical infrastructure, artificial intelligence and other threats. Despite that, LoSapio and senators on the committee spent little time discussing the specifics of his vision for the federal government’s primary defensive cybersecurity agency.

When LoSapio was asked about cybersecurity issues, like how he intended to boost cybersecurity resilience of the nation in the wake of last week’s widespread outages, he said organizations should be more proactive implementing baseline protections around inventory, evaluation  and patching internet-connected devices.

“It’s really important to understand the landscape of the systems you have out in the wild,” said LoSapio. “The things that you operate day-to-day, the systems that you rely on, the systems that are ancillary, and do an extensive evaluation on their vulnerabilities, whether they’ve been patched or not, how they’ve been architected and their defensive capabilities, both things we need to add and the things that exist today, as to how that maps to the threats we’re facing.”

At another point he stated that there was “more we can do across the nation” to improve security, but did not articulate beyond that. He did not commit to hiring more staff, only to evaluate the agency’s resources once confirmed.

But LoSapio also waded into several partisan debates and made comments that led Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., to state that he had “disqualified” himself from the role of CISA director by demonstrating a lack of independence from the White House.

At one point, LoSapio said he didn’t view the Jan. 6, 2021 riots led by Trump supporters on Capitol Hill as an “attack,” doubling down on the assertion when pressed repeatedly by Blumenthal. After the hearing, the social media account for Senate Homeland Security Democrats posted the exchange on X, calling on the Senate to reject LoSapio’s nomination.

“Anyone who can so easily rewrite history in service to Trump should not be leading CISA, which has critical election security obligations,” the post read.

In another exchange, Sen. Bernie Moreno, R-Ohio, asked what the impact would be on CISA if “Democrats” did not fund the agency during the lame duck session. LoSapio answered it would be “catastrophic” and likened its impact on “ordinary Americans” to calling 9-1-1 and having no one pick up. While past shutdowns have hindered agency operations, CISA and other DHS component agencies would continue to operate under emergency appropriations during a shutdown.

Later, when Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., asked about the effects of thousands of firings and hundreds of millions of dollars in cuts over the past two years, as well as the White House budget proposal that would further cut hundreds of millions next year, LoSapio said he could not assess them because he was an outsider coming to the agency.

The late nomination of LoSapio gives him little time between now and January, when a less-friendly, Democratic-majority Congress takes office, to lobby support from the lame duck Senate. His nomination will likely compete for limited floor time as the Trump administration has put forward dozens of federal nominees since the 2026 election.

Following the hearing, Punchbowl News reported that retiring Sen. Thom Tillis, R-N.C. announced he would place a hold on LoSapio’s nomination.

[27] The Register - Flaws in VMware and KVM need attention, if you have time while fixing Java, PHP, and everything else
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 01:57:45 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/20/flaws-in-vmware-and-kvm-need-attention-if-you-have-time-while-fixing-java-php-and-everything-else/5311193

[TAM METİN - 565 kelime | KAYNAK: feed özeti]
If you’ve got a moment to spare in between sorting out emergency patches for PHP, Java, and PostgreSQL, VMware has just bid for any remaining space on your schedule by publishing two critical-rated security advisories. VMSA-2026-0009 addresses CVE-2026-123890, a 9.8-rated flaw that VMware says means a malicious actor with network access to vCenter may exploit this issue to bypass authentication and gain unauthorized access to the system. vCenter is the tool admins use to manage fleets of virtual machines. The flaw is present in VMware’s flagship Cloud Foundation suite. VMware also wants its users to address CVE-2026-123891, which allows attackers to read and write the database used by SDDC Manager, the tool for managing entire software-defined datacenters. Attackers can execute arbitrary commands and elevate privileges to root. The prospect of an unknown party getting access to vCenter or SDDC manager is not pleasant. At least VMware has fixed its software. The virty giant hasn't done the same to address a second advisory, VMSA-2026-0010, which warns that Cloud Foundation, Aria Operations, and VCF Operations HCX products all use Apache – which has a 9.8-rated remote code execution vulnerability (CVE-2026-119618). VMware plans to ship new versions of its products with a patched version of Apache, but for now only has workarounds that disable the http2 protocol. A KVM curiosity Admins who oversee virtual infrastructure also need to pay attention to Linux KVM, the subject of a curious incident over the last 24 hours. This mess started with the November 18 disclosure of CVE-2026-99312, a bug which allows an attacker to use a guest virtual machine to inflict Denial of Service (DoS) or memory corruption attacks on the host. It’s bad news whenever someone with access to a guest VM can attack the host, because it means gaining access to a single tenant can potentially harm all the other guest VMs or peer into the memory a guest uses. This flaw, however, isn’t a worst-case scenario guest-host escape, because it only impacts users of AMD processors who employ nested virtualization – the practice of running a hypervisor inside a virtual machine. Nested virtualization isn’t commonly used in the enterprise, but clouds employ it to do things like powering OS emulators. The bug’s CVSSv3 score is therefore a modest 5.5. Security researcher Oren Tomtov of Accomplish.ai says the flaw meant he could access 127 GB of host RAM on a Digital Ocean “Droplet” – the junior cloud’s name for its cloudy VMs. Yomtov says he told Digital Ocean and the Linux kernel security team about the flaw in October. Maintainers got busy and delivered a fix to the mainline kernel on November 2, full fixes landed on the 17th. The CVE then appeared on November 18. Yomtov and Terra Security quickly posted their analysis of the flaw – but then took the posts down within hours. The Register understands that Digital Ocean asked for the posts to come down because it needed more time to update its Linux implementations. The company has not responded to multiple requests for comment. Terra’s analysis of the flaw asserts that it was introduced to KVM in 2022, when developers wrote a fix for CVE-2022-3344. The company believes 3.2 percent of CVEs in the Linux kernel were introduced by the fix for another CVE. The good news about CVE-2026-99312 is it’s easy to defeat without an update, by disabling nested virtualization in KVM. ®

[28] The Register - Germ Network infects Android, but in a good way
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 01:06:35 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/20/germ-network-infects-android-but-in-a-good-way/5311186

[TAM METİN - 882 kelime]
Germ Network infects Android, but in a good way

Bluesky's preferred encrypted messaging app allows multiple identities without tying you to a phone number

Cops bust botnet of 1.3M infected Android TV boxes
17 days ago

Beware the claw: Do you want to win the bunny or reboot Android?
27 days ago

Want your AI chats to stay private? Brave TEEs up end-to-end encryption 
October 16, 2026

F-Droid app store thumbs its nose at Google's dev crackdown
September 24, 2026

Decades-old file security flaws found in Android, Linux, macOS, and Windows
September 24, 2026

The Germ Network encrypted messaging client, known as Germ DM, became available for Android on Wednesday, and made a bigger splash than its iOS touchdown back in 2023.

"Our usage took a 20x jump yesterday from our Android launch," co-founder Tessa Brown, a communications scholar and former Stanford lecturer, told The Register in an interview. "Android has been really exciting for us because not only is it 70 percent of the global mobile market, but we rolled out on iOS first. People were so excited to use Germ for their end-to-end encrypted Bluesky DMs, but half of their friends couldn't talk to them."

Building a new messaging platform isn't easy, much less one with credible end-to-end encryption. Co-founder Mark Xue hails from Apple, where he worked as a privacy engineer on systems like iMessage and FaceTime.

"At the core level, the end-to-end encryption is built on a standardized encryption and Messaging Layer Security," Xue said. "The premise of Messaging Layer Security was to be as good as Signal and deployable by many, many different parties in different applications, which we're taking advantage of to extend the reach of that encryption."

Xue said the app is built with its own transport protocol to minimize the amount of message metadata, adding that while the team hasn't gotten around to a formal security audit, it's a step they're considering.

Germ Network's goal, said Brown, is to give people more confidence when they communicate. "We do that by giving you more tools to protect your identity, to control what you're sharing with whom," she explained.

Instead of using a person's phone number as an identity key, Germ allows users to create cards which represent distinct identities on the network.

"Instead of sharing your number, you share a card," Brown explained. "You can build multiple cards; they're not mutually discoverable. And our vision for the product that will continue shipping over the coming years is that these cards become a more intelligent way to manage your social life, even your work life."

At some point, AI might play a role in that, but both Xue and Brown expressed reservations about the current state of play.

Citing concerns Signal has raised about the integration of AI and messaging, Xue said, "The products today that I might characterize as technology demos, they're most powerful when they run on someone else's computer. So you end up with these systems where you upload all your messages or you give another entity the power to observe your computing, and that's extremely dangerous for what we protect as private content."

Xue noted rapid progress toward addressing information exposure concerns and therefore plans to explore the kinds of smart tooling that users want while also meeting security and privacy requirements.

"I think a lot of the AI conversation sometimes is a hammer in search of a nail," said Brown. "But people do want more tools to make sense of their busy lives and following our consumers, our users, and what they want, we do want to give them smart tooling that will help them organize threads, stay in touch, find the right people to talk to in groups, in a private way that's using a lot of the intelligence that's already on their device."

If Germ DM were simply trying to win people away from widely adopted services like Instagram or respected encryption apps like Signal, it would be difficult to see a path to success.

But Germ DM may be able to reach a point of sustainability by standing on the shoulders of Bluesky.

Bluesky launched Direct Messages in 2024 but chose to focus its development resources elsewhere and left E2E DMs to Germ Network. Germ DM, which supports Bluesky's AT Protocol (ATproto), fills that gap and even has Bluesky promoting the app for that purpose.

"On a business strategic level, it's been a really wonderful move for us," said Brown. "So we're natively a multi-identity messenger. And you can say that ATproto is the first external identity that we brought into the app, after we first shipped local identities that you just build in our app.

"And so the way that we're marketing and growing right now is [by offering] end-to-end encrypted messages for Bluesky users. You log in with your handle into our app. We pull your friend graph so you can see your mutuals that already have Germ in the app."

Bluesky assists further with marketing by allowing Germ DM users to add a Germ messaging button to their Bluesky profiles. So anyone interested in encrypted DMs in Bluesky may end up seeing that option.

At some point, expect paid, premium features but at the moment, Germ Network is focused on spreading the encrypted word. ®
