[31] The Register - Russian national accused of stalking San Francisco techie in plot against US defense-tech giant
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 17:20:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/19/russian-national-accused-of-stalking-san-francisco-techie-in-plot-against-us-defense-tech-giant/5311126

[TAM METİN - 1483 kelime | KAYNAK: feed özeti]
A Russian national is being held in custody after being arrested for allegedly spying on a US defense tech company in Palo Alto, shoulder-surfing employees, and dropping remote surveillance devices at their homes. Igor D'iu was indicted after FBI agents surveilled his activities between June and October 2026, which they say were issued as "tasks" from what is thought to be Russia’s Federal Security Service (FSB), according to an affidavit [PDF] signed by an FBI special agent. After FBI agents secured a warrant to raid D'iu's temporary premises in October, they say that Telegram messages harvested from his seized devices confirmed investigators' findings and revealed that he was spying on an unnamed company. Court documents describe the company in question as a large technology and defense company with offices in Palo Alto, and media-reported links to the Russia-Ukraine war. While unconfirmed officially, the company has widely been reported as Palantir, which did not respond to our request for confirmation. D'iu allegedly received instructions from Telegram contacts, whom he only suspected were representatives of the Russian government, weeks after entering the US illegally in July. The indictment alleges he was ordered to surveil employees' homes, take photographs of their screens while working remotely at coffee shops, and permit his handlers to remotely access his laptop for the purpose of capturing data from nearby devices owned by the company’s employees. In some cases, the filing alleges, D’iu was asked to leave a laptop outside one employee's apartment complex, all while allowing his Telegram handlers to remotely operate the device. The first time, he was asked to leave the laptop for one hour while the handlers worked, and another time, he was told to drop it off in the middle of the night and only come back to collect it three hours later, the filing claims. D'iu is also allegedly to have followed two female employees in his rented car, driving dangerously and violating multiple road laws to do so. One of the two employees was followed briefly, but D'iu followed another for nearly 30 miles, according to the affidavit signed by FBI special agent Winston Wong. D'iu was said to have followed the woman as she performed U-turns, illegal left turns, ran red lights, navigated via back and side streets, and through grocery store parking lots. The car following her only peeled away after the woman drove to the San Francisco Mission police station on the advice of a 911 call handler. Questioned about the incident, which according to police reports caused the woman significant emotional distress, D'iu confirmed he was the driver but claimed he was following her to ask her out on a date. Inside recruitment The affidavit did not detail the suspect's background beyond his alleged offenses in the US earlier this year, although it mentioned Russian intelligence's history of recruiting "criminal proxies, organized crime groups, and other disposable agents" to carry out operations. According to the FBI's account, D'iu entered the US illegally – without relevant visas – in either June or July 2026. Travel records obtained on September 10 showed that his last recorded journey was from Istanbul to Mexico City. There was no record of him entering the US. Investigators would later come to know that he travelled on that Istanbul-Mexico City flight on June 7, allegedly using a ticket provided to him by one of the three contacts from whom he received orders via Telegram. A Facebook account, registered using a Mexican +528 number and D'iu's name, posted on June 30 asking for help getting "closer to New York," adding that "I'll pay as much as needed." The account replied to a response from someone who told him to contact a Mexico phone number on WhatsApp. The filing claims the first mobile phone signals associated with D'iu's devices did not ping US towers until a month later, on July 31, suggesting he did not arrive in the US - specifically McAllen, Texas - until around that date. Telegram messages would also go on to confirm that he was allegedly helped across the border by an "Uzbek contact" in Mexico. Records obtained from Google showed D'iu created a Google account in April. According to the affidavit, D'iu used it to search for the company's Wikipedia page on July 28, and on August 3, used Google's AI to devise a route around Texas that bypassed border checkpoints. The FBI believes D'iu's "spying" began in mid-August, after he bought a laptop, a Wi-Fi device, a puck device, and an additional phone. At this point, the agency claims, he had received his first order to visit a coffee shop near the Palo Alto company’s office and snap employees' laptop screens before sending copies back to his handlers. Three days later, D'iu was ordered to go back to a coffee shop and allow his Telegram contacts to remotely control the laptop and collect data from employee devices that were in range, claims the filing. D'iu was paid $15,000 on three occasions between August 12 and October 6, according to the court documents. To verify the transaction, he was told to send a picture of a dollar bill's serial number to the Telegram contacts, along with a description of what he was wearing. A courier would then arrive, confirming the serial number and handing D'iu the cash. The filing alleges he used this money to purchase the tech equipment, accommodation, and travel, including a rented silver 2026 Lexus RX 350 from a private owner in Los Angeles on August 22, which he is said to have driven back to San Francisco. The same Lexus was captured on security footage outside the unnamed company's office on September 1, and over the following two days was implicated in the two instances of female employees being followed. Contacted by Palo Alto police on September 4, D'iu confirmed he was the driver who dangerously followed the female staffer for nearly 30 minutes, but claimed he knew nothing about the nearby businesses. D'iu would then claim he followed the woman because he thought she was beautiful and wanted to ask her out on a date, and denied following anyone else. The affidavit goes on to allege that D'iu then traveled to Reno, Nevada, to lay low after his run-in with the police. He spent the time trying to reroute DHL deliveries from San Francisco to his hideout address, which US Customs would later intercept. These ended up containing fake IDs with the images the DoJ claims matched D'iu's, including Uzbek documents, a US passport whose number belonged to a New York woman, and a social security card registered to a "Hugo Landry Johns." He traveled back to the Bay Area in late September, swapping his silver Lexus for a white 2016 Dodge Challenger, and received additional instructions from his handlers, including the order to leave a remote-controlled laptop outside the apartment complex, says the filing. One Telegram contact named Ian Morales sent photos of at least four employees and their addresses, along with instructions for D'iu to dress as a food delivery worker and visit those locations, asking whoever answered to verify they lived there. FBI agents executed a search warrant on October 8, seizing a cache of evidence, including what they listed as being:  The forged ID documents  Two Russian passports  $18,035 in cash  Two laptops  Two iPhones  Two T-Mobile SIM cards  Wi-Fi equipment  A notebook with 10 handwritten addresses supplied by Telegram contacts, along with times the suspect was supposed to attend those locations, at least five of which were for company employees’ homes  Profile packages for at least three employees, including: Names, personal information, education and employment histories, names of relatives, narrative histories, and assessments of leadership qualities, strengths, and weaknesses. Suspicions and charges Per the affidavit, D'iu was said to have only become suspicious of the tasks he was being ordered to complete in October, when his handlers ordered the laptop to be dropped near the apartment complex. "D'iu gradually began to understand he was doing something bad and that his actions were illegal," the affidavit stated. At this point, he allegedly asked the Telegram contacts who he was working for, to which they replied "the Motherland," and the company he was surveilling was involved in providing weapons to Ukraine, resulting in D’iu suspecting the Russian government. Wong said in the affidavit: "Based on my training, experience, and research, D'iu's communications with his Telegram contacts is consistent with the tactics of [Russian intelligence] and its criminal proxies for spotting, assessing, recruiting, and handling intelligence assets and sources. "D'iu's targeting of the company and its employees is also consistent with [Russian intelligence] objectives in Russia's war against Ukraine." The Russian national was indicted and charged with two counts related to spying offences, and one count related to the fake identity documents. He is next due in court on November 24 but remains in federal custody. ®

Cyberattack on service provider exposes payee data at 40 Irish credit unions

Minister calls notification delay 'not acceptable' after October attack on Irish banking player Wellington IT came to light in November

7 hours ago

An attack on software provider Wellington IT exposed saved payee details held by around 40 Irish credit unions, prompting a minister to criticize the company's delay in notifying them.

Robert Troy, Ireland's minister of state with responsibility for credit unions, said the breach happened in October but many affected institutions only learned of it in recent days.

Troy told Irish broadcaster RTÉ that the breach occurred "sometime in October," and Wellington IT's delay in providing details to its credit union customers was "not acceptable."

News of the issues affecting Irish credit unions broke on Tuesday, although the government said at the time it was only investigating a "possible cybersecurity incident" in the first instance, and did not name Wellington IT until Wednesday.

Affected credit unions said attackers exploited a vulnerability in their provider's online banking platform between October 2 and 4. The flaw was closed on October 6.

Troy said the affected credit unions were only informed for the first time in recent days.

The exposed records concern payees saved in members' online banking accounts. Those records can also be linked to the members who created them.

The information exposed varies between institutions and individual records, but disclosures list the following data types:

Names on payee bank accounts and saved nicknames

Names on payee bank accounts and saved nicknames

Business Identifier Codes (BICs)

Business Identifier Codes (BICs)

International Bank Account Numbers (IBANs)

International Bank Account Numbers (IBANs)

Internal reference numbers that do not identify customers

Internal reference numbers that do not identify customers

Credit unions including St. Canice's said online banking passwords, PINs, and login credentials were unaffected, and that the exposed payee details did not provide access to members' accounts or funds.

St. Canice's warned members not to transfer money in response to anyone claiming it needed to be moved to safety because of the breach.

The CEO of Belfast-based Wellington IT, Colin Mitchell, said in a statement that the company notified the affected organizations "as soon as we confirmed the potential risk to customer data."

He said Wellington IT has engaged outside cybersecurity experts to help investigate the breach, and reported the matter to the relevant authorities.

Mitchell added: "We are directly supporting our customers in their assessment of the potential impact on affected individuals and to help them meet any applicable regulatory obligations.

"We recognize the importance of this matter to our customers and their members and are treating it with the utmost seriousness. We remain focused on completing a thorough investigation and communicating directly with our customers as the investigation progresses."

Owned by Canadian company Vencora, Wellington IT has offices in Belfast and Dublin, and provides credit unions with software, mobile apps, private cloud infrastructure, and fully managed services.

Around 40 credit unions in Ireland were affected, roughly a quarter of the national total, Troy said.

Credit unions operate similarly to traditional banks, offering savings accounts and loans, but are owned by their members and are not-for-profit organizations.

Troy told RTÉ that cybersecurity is a constant challenge for financial institutions, as technology and threats evolve over time.

"What's most important is that financial institutions take all the right measures to mitigate against these threats and that is what is happening," he said.

Separately, the UK's Financial Conduct Authority, Prudential Regulation Authority, and Bank of England opened a joint consultation on cyber resilience guidance on Thursday.

The FCA said October brought its second-highest monthly tally of reported cyber incidents in five years, and the highest in 18 months. The proposed guidance clarifies how firms should meet existing requirements rather than introducing new rules. ®

[33] The Register - TfL takes Oyster photocard website offline after attackers compromise customer accounts
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 12:01:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/19/tfl-takes-oyster-photocard-website-offline-after-attackers-compromise-customer-accounts/5310985

[TAM METİN - 518 kelime | KAYNAK: feed özeti]
Transport for London (TfL) has copped to another cybersecurity breach, this time involving the account details of around 265,000 Oyster photocard customers. The capital's transport operator told The Register it first became aware something was amiss on November 16, when Oyster photocard customers started receiving emails asking them to create accounts they already had. The emails came from TfL's own systems, the transport body confirmed, but were generated as a result of the compromise. "We immediately took the TfL photocard website down while this was being investigated," said Shashi Verma, TfL's director of technology strategy and revenue. "The protection of personal information is a priority for us, and I apologize to our customers affected by this incident." TfL reckons its speedy response spared a considerable number of customers from having their details compromised. The transport operator says it has since contained the incident and contacted those potentially affected, though it is rather more forthcoming about what the hackers didn't get their hands on than what they did. TfL says no passwords, bank or payment card details, or customer journey information were compromised. It also believes the names and dates of birth of children with Zip Oyster cards were unaffected. Among those whose accounts may have been compromised are holders of Zip Oyster cards – issued to children as young as five – as well as students, apprentices, care leavers, veterans, and Londoners aged 60 and over. Despite the assurances about passwords, TfL says it will ask all photocard customers to change theirs, along with their security questions and answers. Those in the habit of recycling passwords across multiple websites are being advised to change them elsewhere, too. TfL says it has yet to find any evidence that the compromised data has been used and has notified the Information Commissioner's Office (ICO). The transport operator hasn't disclosed how the perpetrators got into its systems. Existing photocards will still let holders through the ticket barriers, though anyone needing to renew one or check their journey history will have to contact TfL's customer services team while the website is out of action. There's no word yet on when it'll be back. "We hope to restore the affected website services as quickly as possible," Verma said. TfL should be well versed in dealing with data breaches by now. In March, the transport operator told The Register it had notified more than 7 million customers following its September 2024 cyberattack. The hackers had accessed systems holding customers' names, email addresses, and home addresses, while around 5,000 customers were contacted as a priority because their bank account details may also have been compromised. Two teenagers were subsequently charged in connection with the attack, which authorities linked to the notorious Scattered Spider cybercrime crew. The ICO looked into that incident but opted not to take enforcement action against TfL, having deemed its response proportionate. There's nothing at present to suggest the two incidents are related. Londoners can at least rest assured that their Oyster photocards still work. Whether the information they handed over to obtain them is in someone else's hands is another matter. ®

[34] The Register - Sticky note on monitor provided keys to the company’s kingdom
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 11:15:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/19/sticky-note-on-monitor-provided-keys-to-the-companys-kingdom/5310908

[TAM METİN - 562 kelime | KAYNAK: feed özeti]
Welcome, once again, to PWNED, where we talk smack about organizations that have smacked themselves down by practicing poor security hygiene. This week's story involves everyone's favorite low-tech password manager: the sticky note. Our terrifying tale comes courtesy of Christian Espinosa, a certified penetration tester with over 30 years of experience and the CEO of Blue Goat Cyber, a security company. Espinosa shared an experience where critical credentials were right out in the open for any person in the office to see. During a walkthrough of a client's office, he noticed a password written on a sticky note that was taped to an employee's monitor. While writing down your password and placing it in a physically secure paper notebook might be ok, taping it to the computer is definitely not. "Before I get my fingers on any system, I first walk the physical area that the system is located in — attackers do that when they're looking at a target too," Espinosa explained. “Physical security vulnerabilities are neglected much more often than technical security vulnerabilities and firms invest in firewalls where a sticky note is placed 3 feet from the front door." Espinosa took a photo of the sticky note and immediately tried logging into that employee's account. Success. Then, he tried the very same password on the VPN, which apparently had been maintained by that employee. Once he connected to the VPN, Espinosa had full access to the company network and all of its shared drives. He opened a shared drive that this employee had access to and there was an HR folder with all kinds of personally identifiable information (PII) about employees, including home addresses, driver's licenses, and scanned W-4 forms. There were even spreadsheets that listed everyone's salaries. The employee account also had access to a document that contained a list of internal server names and the names of the people in charge of each server (great for phishing). Another document had instructions for how to log into the accounting server specifically. The accounting server was a particular treasure trove of client information. On that server, Espinosa found a folder with vendor banking information, invoices, and payment records. "At that point, a password I had read from a sticky note during a walk-through had taken me from one desk to information that could have been used for payroll fraud, identity theft, or vendor-payment fraud," Espinosa said. After getting access to all this information, the pen tester presented his findings to the client. They quickly disabled the rogue account and started reviewing VPN access logs to see who else might have gotten into the network. They then searched the office to see if anyone else was exposing a password on a sticky note. Finally, they tightened permissions on the HR and accounting shares so that only people who really needed access to that data could get in. The lessons here are pretty obvious. Don't expose your passwords and, perhaps even more importantly, limit who has access to file shares that contain mission-critical data such as employee or client information. "The part that really got the client's attention was how easily the attack could have continued after I left the building," Espinosa posited. "Someone could have photographed that sticky note, walked out, connected to the VPN later that night, and started going through employee and financial records from somewhere else." ®

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/19/sticky-note-on-monitor-provided-keys-to-the-companys-kingdom/5310908, theregister.com, 19.11.2026)

[35] TechCrunch Security - These tech outages are giving Y2K, AI agent style
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 21:42:31 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/19/these-tech-outages-are-giving-y2k-ai-agent-style/

[TAM METİN - 1157 kelime]
It’s going down, as the American rapper Yung Joc might say: the airline booking systems, banking platforms, and productivity platforms. In the past two weeks, the U.S.’s top businesses have faced technical outages that have disrupted the systems that run everyday life.

Apple Pay went down. NASA’s internal communication network went down. The FAA had to ground all flights for hours (only the third time in history since 9/11), and Microsoft was hit with an outage that took down Outlook, Teams, and Copilot for nearly two days.

The first thought, naturally, is an AI hacker invasion of some sort, especially after hackers managed to attack fridges in a grocery store and a blood bank in France a few weeks ago. Here in the U.S., the director of National Intelligence said these outages don’t seem related to state-sponsored terrorism, while President Trump called the issues “individual events.” Many of the companies themselves have said the outages were not caused by cyberattacks or AI attacks, but tech issues.

Only, there’s the fact that they all struck within a short timespan of each other, and just a few weeks after Cybersecurity’s so-called “Black October,” when a slew of also-unrelated cybersecurity incidents occurred on October 21, striking PHP, Node.js, LiteLLM, more.

While there’s no reason to view these outages as cyberattacks, there may be more than coincidence going on: It’s giving AI-native Y2K, frankly.

Dave DeWalt, an investor who worked in cybersecurity for more than three decades (including as president and CEO of McAfee and as vice chairman of the CISA Advisory Council for the Department of Homeland Security), also thinks it’s probably AI-related.

The technical outages are most likely the “growing pains of a security system that’s getting stronger, not weaker,” and that “AI is surfacing vulnerabilities at a speed and scale we’ve never had before through AI tools like Mythos and others,” he told TechCrunch.

The thing is, many of these technical flaws have always existed, but because AI is surfacing them so fast, enterprises are rushing to fix them before bad actors can exploit them. “That surge in patching is disrupting things in the short term,” he said.

This was, perhaps, the first moment when legacy meets modern, Anusha Iyer, a software engineer and founder of the security startup Corsha, told TechCrunch. “These industrial systems were never designed to be connected, let alone securely connected in the way we are pushing them to be today,” she said.

Virginia Senator Mark Warner, vice chair of the Senate Intel Committee, came to a similar conclusion (although investigations are still underway as to what could be happening). “These disruptions should serve as a wake-up call. It feels like every day brings another reminder that AI is moving faster than our ability to manage its consequences,” the senator wrote on X.

AI is reshaping how software is built, deployed, and operated, on a scale where everyone feels the sharp learning curve. Iyer cited the FAA outage as an example of legacy tech that is not as sophisticated or advanced as the technologies companies are trying to merge it with.

In less technical terms, she said the outage may have been caused (and preliminary findings point to this) when the FAA was trying to modernize its telecommunications infrastructure and the transition overwhelmed parts of its legacy hardware, where the new tech likely operated at a normal modern capacity while the legacy hardware couldn’t keep up.

When looked at this way, she said, AI is actually getting the bad rep. “It’s not rapid adoption of AI yet causing these outages,” she said. “The fault is the rushed mix of the legacy and modern systems without a clear understanding of operational and cyber risk or the ability to gatekeep and protect the legacy systems.”

Organizations need a more steadfast approach to AI adoption, including more testing and prioritizing what needs to be fixed and when, DeWalt said.

Iyer added, “These industrial systems need to be segmented more tightly and more access controlled, so as modern systems are introduced, it can be phased in and risk mitigated.”

Actually, there might be a silver lining to all of this for companies (and for venture investors). DeWalt said he’d rather see these U.S. enterprises find and patch their flaws, even in this messy way, than have bad actors find and exploit those vulnerabilities in AI warfare that could cause even more national security issues.

And maybe some aspiring founders were inspired by them. DeWalt expects a rise in companies focused on “AI-driven vulnerability management, autonomous remediation, safe patch testing and deployment, and resilience for the systems that keep critical infrastructure running,” he said.

“There’s real pain in this transition as we’ve seen in the past week,” DeWalt said. “But there’s also enormous opportunity for the companies that help organizations move at AI speed without breaking things.”

[36] TechCrunch Security - UK government forced to disclose secret Apple backdoor order
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 15:32:20 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/19/u-k-government-forced-to-disclose-secret-apple-backdoor-order/

[TAM METİN - 681 kelime]
The U.K. government has been ordered to confirm that it issued a secret order demanding Apple to provide authorities with encrypted customer information.

The Thursday ruling from the U.K.’s Investigatory Powers Tribunal, an independent court that allows citizens to challenge government surveillance orders, is a rare rebuke for the government, which sought to keep the encryption backdoor order a state secret.

The tribunal’s ruling rejected the Home Office’s claim that disclosing the order would be damaging to national security.

The ruling comes a year after the U.K. government reportedly demanded that Apple build a backdoor to allow U.K. authorities access to customer data anywhere in the world. Details of the so-called “technical capabilities notice,” which are issued under strict non-disclosure rules, leaked to the media, which drew ire from White House officials and U.S. lawmakers who criticized the effort, arguing that the U.K. order would put Americans’ privacy at risk.

California-based Apple refused the order, and blocked U.K. users from switching on its Advanced Data Protection data-encryption feature for iCloud, rather than comply with the demand. The U.K.’s secret order was later narrowed to allow access to British nationals’ data stored in Apple’s encrypted cloud, and Apple also challenged this second order.

Prior to the ruling, Apple was legally barred from disclosing the order, even after details leaked. When reached for comment, Apple told TechCrunch that it welcomes the decision to make public the fact that the U.K. Home Office issued a technical capability notice.

Apple previously said that it has “never built a backdoor or master key” to any of its products or services and “never will.”

A spokesperson for the U.K. Home Office, which has yet to confirm the existence of a notice, did not respond to a request for comment.

Rights group Liberty International, which brought the legal challenge on behalf of two individuals, said that this is the first known time that the government has been compelled to confirm the existence of a secret technical order.

A final hearing on what specific order details can be released is anticipated next year.

[37] CSO Online - Do AI’s mathematical advances put PKI under threat?
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 08:25:00 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4248694/do-ais-mathematical-advances-put-pki-under-threat.html

[TAM METİN - 1085 kelime]
Cryptographers are debating the long-term outlook for public-key cryptography now that AI is proving that hard analytical problems are no longer solvable by humans alone.

Warnings by a leading cryptographer that industry-wide public-key cryptography could fail because of advances in AI has sparked a debate in the security community.

In a series of post on X, Johns Hopkins cryptographer Matthew Green warned that existing public-key cryptography systems were at risk because machines have begun outperforming humans in their ability to address some mathematical problems.

“I think there is a 1% chance we live in Minicrypt, and a 15% chance we functionally lose confidence in our existing public-key encryption algorithms,” Green wrote last month.

Green’s initial warning and follow-up comments offered a warning about the cryptographic foundations beneath public key infrastructure (PKI), rather than an obituary for PKI, the system of certificates, keys, and certificate authorities that secures Transport Layer Security (TLS), code signing, and many other information security functions.

“I’m worried about standardized public-key encryption (and some signatures) and specifically the fact that most of it is based on a small number of hard problems,” Green added.

These hard mathematical problems have been extensively analyzed by humans for years but any comforting notion that this means they are safe is now being undermined because machines have begun outperforming humans in solving some mathematical problems.

Green warns that a disruptive loss of confidence in internet security systems would arise if a major cryptanalytic surprise arrives faster than standards and deployments can adapt — so the industry needs to focus on improved cryptographic agility.

The comments sparked a discussions on LinkedIn and elsewhere about how public-key crypto could fail. Green is yet to respond to CSO’s request for comment on the debate he sparked.

Markku-Juhani O. Saarinen, a cryptographer and professor at Finland’s Tampere University who has looked closely at AI-powered crypto-analysis, told CSO that he takes Green’s warning seriously but urges caution, saying there is no need to rip and replace established industry cryptographic standards.

“We really can’t entirely rule out surprises, but I’d also definitely not recommend some hasty moves, because those are sure to cause problems,” Saarinen said. “Cryptography has good standards, best practices, and active modernization paths (the ‘PQC transition’) — just following those is the most sensible approach from information security perspective.”

Saarinen explained that there are two kinds of “hard problems” in mathematics:

Numerical problems for which a solution is thought to be hard to compute (for computers)

Analytic problems that are open and have been expected to be settled by a human with pencil and paper via a proof, counterexample, new theory, etc.

“What has happened in 2026 is that mathematics had its ‘Lee Sedol moment’ (after the ‘last Go grandmaster’) and [analytical] problems previously thought to be in the human domain (2) are now being solved by computers,” Saarinen told CSO.

Cryptography uses numerical problems in class — traditional ones like factoring the products of large prime numbers and discrete logarithm, and newer ones like learning with errors (LWE). Analyzing those algorithms and their complexity is part of theoretical computer science.

“The fear in cryptography is that AI will find a shortcut for some hard computational problems and make them easy,” Saarinen said. “While AI is very good at optimizing computation, we don’t expect it to turn all hard computational problems into trivial ones.”

2026: Breaking HAWK and McEliece

Cryptographic problems involving set (1) above are typically “sub-exponential”; solving them requires an almost exponential number of operations relative to the problem size or distance.

“A cryptographically relevant break can be exponential but might halve the number of bits; such an analytic break has happened to some relatively new schemes, such as HAWK by AI in 2026, and to numerous older proposals by humans,” Saarinen explained.

AI also played a significant role in finding a significant shortcut to the parameters of the McEliece cryptosystem (one of the earliest public-key schemes, designed in 1978).

“The German cyber agency had been recommending a variant called ‘Classic McEliece’ for quantum security since 2020, but withdrew its recommendation at the end of September,” Saarinen added.

Saarinen clarified that, in both cases, the schemes could still be “considered secure if security parameters were adjusted, but that would have doubled public and secret keys, signatures, and ciphertexts.”

Meanwhile, SA and Elliptic-Curve cryptosystems are already “broken” from a computational-complexity viewpoint because “polynomial-time quantum algorithms for them have been known since 1994, and building quantum computers is ‘just an engineering problem,’” according to Saarinen.

The main post-quantum alternatives, ML-KEM, ML-DSA, and SLH-DSA, have received more than a decade of intense analysis.

Looking for a ‘mathematical shortcut’

Another question that cryptographers are asking is whether public-key cryptography or symmetric cryptography is more vulnerable to AI cryptanalysis.

Saarinen suggested a “mathematical shortcut” is more likely to exist in public-key cryptography given that it, by definition, requires mathematical structure for the “trapdoor” (a secret key that opens a public-key encrypted message or creates a signature that the public key can verify). “Symmetric-key cryptography (hashes and keyed ciphers) has simply more design freedom in this sense,” Saarinen concluded.

Professor Alan Woodward, a computer scientist at the University of Surrey, said that LLMs are yet to make a huge dent on cryptography.

“We’ve seen some inroads and erosion of the security guarantees, but what’s actually happening as far as I can see is that the LLMs are acting as a great tool for mathematicians, cryptographers, and computer scientists to speed up their research: that leap of intuition needed to find the fatal flaw still seems to need humans,” Prof. Woodward said. “LLMs are very effective at finding flaws in those implementations rather than the underlying mathematics.”

Prof. Woodward compared the threat posed by AI in breaking encryption schemes to the danger to legacy encryption schemes posed by quantum computers. Both problems highlight the need for improved agility in the cryptographic schemes in use.

“We’ve already seen people migrating in preparation for ‘Q-Day’ so in some ways that should already be addressing the risks posed, if they’re real, by LLMs,” according to Prof. Woodward, referring to the hypothetical future time when quantum computers are powerful enough to break public-key encryption. “The most effective route so far seems to be a hybrid route where we have a mix of cryptographic schemes that, whilst one or two might fall away, leaves us with others we can rely upon. Any found to be flawed for whatever reason can be deprecated rapidly and the intact schemes used instead.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4248694/do-ais-mathematical-advances-put-pki-under-threat.html, csoonline.com, 20.11.2026)

[38] CSO Online - A single pull request could have handed attackers the keys to Microsoft’s software supply chain
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 03:10:04 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4251117/a-single-pull-request-could-have-handed-attackers-the-keys-to-microsofts-software-supply-chain.html

[TAM METİN - 1121 kelime]
A now patched vulnerability in WinGet's moderation tooling could have handed attackers a Microsoft employee's GitHub credentials through a single pull request.

The most dangerous actor in a software supply chain may now be an automated script with powerful credentials and no idea where it’s sending them.

Researchers from pen-testing company Cycode have discovered a now patched critical zero-click supply chain vulnerability in the open-source Windows Package Manager (WinGet), which sits on billions of devices worldwide.

A single pull request could have tricked Microsoft’s internal tooling into freely handing over a moderator’s GitHub access token, with no phishing, no malware, no moderator clicking or approval required.

There is currently no evidence that the flaw has been exploited in the wild. But it serves as a cautionary tale for enterprises that the automation they trust is becoming an attacker entry point, and package repositories are particularly enticing targets.

“Package registries have become the highest-profile target in the software supply chain,” Moriel Harush, Cycode security research lead, wrote in a blog post. “Compromise a single maintainer, and you reach everyone who trusts them. Compromise the gatekeepers, and you reach the entire ecosystem.”

Whatever the employee can do, the attacker can do

WinGet is Microsoft’s free, open source command line tool that helps users discover, install, upgrade, configure, and remove apps. It exists on more than 1.4 billion active devices running Windows 10, Windows 11, and Windows Server 2025.

WinGet’s catalog is a public GitHub repository, and its gatekeeper, so to speak, is a small, official group of Microsoft moderators who review a constant stream of community pull requests and proposed changes. By default, WinGet installations on every Windows machine trust the actions those keepers take.

The hole is in the moderators’ own internal triage tool. This PowerShell helper attached the moderator’s secret GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT) to any HTTP request, without checking who owned that address.

This meant an attacker merely had to supply an address. Theoretically, if they opened a pull request and pointed an InstallerURL software installation package at their own server, “the trap was set,” Harush explained.

Microsoft’s automated Azure DevOps validation pipeline would then attempt to fetch the URL, but the attacker’s server would intentionally return an error. This added a validation error label to the pull request.

The moderator’s internal script would respond to that label by running a CheckInstaller routine which would send a HEAD request to the attacker via an authenticated HTTP helper, whose job is to automatically attach credentials or tokens to outgoing HTTP requests and handle authorization errors. Then all the attacker had to do was check their logs and extract the moderator’s official GitHub token, which was “casually” handed over.

But the token is not merely a repository-scoped credential, it is a full identity credential, meaning whatever that Microsoft employee can do on GitHub, the token can do.

Harush explained: “It is like a courier stapling your house key to every parcel that has your name on it, because most of them are destined for your own address.” But if a stranger uses your name and changes the address to one they control, “your key is delivered directly to them.”

Armed with this key to Microsoft’s internal environment, an attacker could push malicious manifests, approve their own pull requests, and slip in backdoored installers to 14,000 exposed packages, all under a highly trusted, official Microsoft badge.

They could also access and write to every other Microsoft repository the moderator had access to, modify GitHub Actions workflows across those repositories to achieve remote code execution (RCE) and exfiltrate corporate secrets.

There is a precondition for this attack: At least one moderator has to have the triage tracker running in a continuous background loop, which it is designed to do as it waits for work.

Underlying model ‘fundamentally flawed’

Harush pointed out that Microsoft’s C# version of the tool, in the same directory, did not share the flaw; as such, it would have offered safer authentication. Microsoft has since patched the PowerShell vulnerability, safely scoping the authentication mechanism to ensure that users’ tokens are only attached when a request host is an authorized GitHub endpoint, such as api.github.com, github.com, or raw.githubusercontent.com.

Harush noted, however, that the underlying credential model is “fundamentally flawed,” and that relying on Classic PATs is a “severe liability.”

Cycode advises binding credentials to destinations, rather than functions; auditing every internal tool that handles maintainer credentials; and ensuring that headers are conditionally applied based on the target host, “not unconditionally stapled by a wrapper function.” Treat all user inputs as hostile, validated hosts before credentials are attached, and deprecate classic PATs.

“The blast radius of a leaked token must be limited to one repository and the bare minimum permissions,” Harush said. “A leaked token should never mean a leaked corporate identity.”

He emphasized, though, that WinGet moderators are doing exactly what’s asked of them: Using automated tools to manage a “flood of community submissions” and make their jobs easier. “But automation does not just remove the human who would have clicked the malicious link; it removes the human who would have noticed the anomaly.”

Scripts are a soft spot

The WinGet flaw was a near miss, but “a very serious one,” said Frank Dickson of Dickson Research. Exploiting it would have let an attacker publish malware with “Microsoft’s seal of approval.”

This means attackers could have pushed tampered installers to anyone who ran an install or an update, reached into any other Microsoft repositories the moderator could touch, and hijacked the build machinery to steal more secrets.

And the damage would have looked like routine work. An IT team scripting a standard software rollout would have installed the attacker’s code on every machine, and the logs would have shown an approved package from an approved source.

“The attack would have been a Trojan horse delivered by the mail carrier everyone trusts,” Dickson said.

Enterprise security teams should learn that their own scripts are a soft spot, he pointed out; internal tools, bots, and automation get production-level keys and hobby-level review. CISOs should inventory every script and bot that holds a token, and answer important questions: What could this key do in the wrong hands? Where is it allowed to go?

Additionally, he advised replacing broad, long-lived tokens with narrow, short-lived ones, and binding every credential to the specific services that need it, which is exactly how Microsoft fixed this problem. “Also, enterprises should stop trusting public registries blindly,” Dickson said.

Ultimately, security teams should review their automations the way they review their people, he said. “A script that runs in a loop around the clock with a moderator’s keys is, in effect, a moderator who never sleeps and never questions anything.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4251117/a-single-pull-request-could-have-handed-attackers-the-keys-to-microsofts-software-supply-chain.html, csoonline.com, 20.11.2026)

[39] CSO Online - European Commission HR hit by cyber attack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 02:08:48 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4251068/european-commission-hr-hit-by-cyber-attack.html

[TAM METİN - 819 kelime]
Shortly after the FBI warned about risks from open source AI tool ARTEX, the Commission confirmed that attackers used it to try to access personnel records.

Barely a week after the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) warned companies of the cyber attacker interest in an open source penetration testing tool called ARTEX, the European Commission has confirmed that attackers armed with that tool broke into its HR systems.

The attack, which Commission officials detected on October 22, forced them to shut off much of the access to the systems for more than 10 days.

“As a protective and preventive measure, we temporarily took down several applications and most of them have since then been restored,” Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier said Thursday. “We have not detected any evidence of a data breach affecting Commission staff as a whole.”

During a news media briefing, a transcript of which was provided to CSOonline, he said that the European Data Protection Supervisor (EDPS) was informed of the attack, “so there was no hiding whatsoever.”

Regnier would not go into detail about the attacks, given that the matter is still being investigated, but he did say, “it is a campaign, so there was a series of incidents.”

In an email, Commission officials added, “we are also preventively checking other systems while staying in close contact with CERT-EU and the EDPS.”

ARTEX popular with attackers

The November 13 FBI statement about ARTEX, described as an autonomous penetration testing framework, said that the tool is becoming very popular with attackers.

“Since at least August 2026, a variety of malicious cyber actors have used ARTEX to conduct campaigns targeting organizations globally across multiple sectors, including financial services, government, technology, defense, semiconductor, energy, healthcare, manufacturing, telecommunications, and legal industries,” the FBI advisory said. “ARTEX significantly reduces the time required to chain attack techniques, with some intrusions progressing from initial targeting to privileged access in under three hours. The framework enables threat actors with minimal technical expertise to conduct complex, multi-target operations simultaneously, fundamentally accelerating attack velocity and expanding operational scale.”

Although ARTEX can handle a wide range of pentesting functions, it appeared to be primarily used in the Commission attack to search for and locate credentials.

The FBI said that ARTEX can “automatically map effective permissions of Azure credentials–SAS tokens, Service Principals, Kudu credentials, Storage Account keys–using sub-second capability checks.”

ARTEX has been known to SOC operations since GitHub first released the tool in July, but widespread misuse of the tool prompted GitHub to make it unavailable on October 8. “Despite this action, ARTEX remains widely accessible through independent routes and mirrors on code-sharing platforms,” the FBI said.

Uses different tactics

Brian Levine, a partner with consulting firm Control Risks, added that ARTEX’s AI-fueled tactics are different from what many cybersecurity teams are used to.

“ARTEX doesn’t need to steal your keys. It finds the ones you left lying around, like a SAS token in a code repo, a storage key in a config file, or a Kudu credential nobody rotated. Then it tries each one against every door in under a second,” he said. “Picture a burglar walking the block at 3 a.m. and checking every car door handle. He isn’t breaking glass. He’s taking inventory of what you left unlocked and AI lets him work a thousand blocks at once.”

Companies need a secure fallback

Noah Kenney, principal consultant at Digital 520, said he was unimpressed with the Commission’s attempts to tell EU citizens that all was well.

“The Commission says it has no evidence of a breach affecting its staff ‘as a whole,’ which is very different from saying that there is no evidence of a breach affecting any of the staff,” Kenney said. “The wording doesn’t give me much comfort.”

Kenney also said that the attackers’ decision to target HR was not an accident, and that the Commission’s decision to temporarily shut down some systems may have inadvertently helped the intruders.

“When payroll, leave, and medical reimbursement systems go down, people start using spreadsheets, email and manual approvals. Those workarounds often have weaker access controls and worse audit trails than the systems taken offline,” he said. “Enterprises need to define a secure fallback process before the attack, because improvising one often leads to greater exposure.”

Levine also said that the choice of an HR victim was understandable.

“HR systems are a prize. Names, home addresses, pay grades and health insurance records for EU civil servants make a ready-made targeting list for espionage and social engineering,” he said. “Whether or not data left the building, every Commission employee should prepare to receive a very convincing phishing email soon.”

His advice to CISOs: “Get secrets out of code and pipelines and rotate anything that has been exposed. Give every token and service principal only the access it needs, so a found key opens a closet, not the vault. Put cloud admin interfaces behind private access.”

[40] CSO Online - Palo Alto Networks bets $4.5B on enterprise AI with Applied Compute acquisition
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 11:36:10 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250669/palo-alto-networks-bets-4-5b-on-enterprise-ai-with-applied-compute-acquisition.html

[TAM METİN - 814 kelime]
The deal will give the cybersecurity company technology to train, customize, and deploy open-weight AI models

Palo Alto Networks has agreed to buy Applied Compute in an all-stock deal valued at $4.5 billion, a move the company said will let it train, deploy and secure open-weight AI models for enterprises in the public cloud or in their own sovereign environments.

“The acquisition will bring Applied Compute’s platform and world-class AI research team to Palo Alto Networks to help enterprises adopt AI securely, whether they choose open-weight models, closed frontier models, or both,” the company said in a statement released alongside its fiscal first quarter 2027 results. The statement described Applied Compute as “a leading platform for post-training and inference of open-weight AI models” and said the startup will continue to serve AI-native companies, digital natives and large enterprises.

The transaction is expected to close in Palo Alto’s fiscal third quarter of 2027, which runs from February to April, the statement said. Applied Compute co-founder and CEO Yash Patil will become Palo Alto’s co-chief AI officer, and the startup’s research team will help build AI models across the company’s offerings.

A security company that wants to run models

Nikesh Arora, Palo Alto’s chairman and CEO, said on the company’s earnings call that beyond building AI into its own security products, “we will help train and serve secure sovereign implementations of open-weight models to the enterprise.”

“Open-weight model implementations will allow us to deliver secure, open-weight models to our customers to enable adoption of secure AI in the enterprise,” Arora said. He said open-weight models had grown from under 5% of token traffic to more than 60% over the past year, without saying where the figure came from.

Asked by JP Morgan analyst Brian Essex about customer demand, Arora said deterministic security controls “cannot anticipate every scenario that an agent is going to conduct.” Enterprises building AI applications “will have to train a supervisory AI model at the same time,” he said, because Palo Alto cannot secure models it has no part in building.

“I cannot go in and intercept and understand somebody else’s application,” he said.

Sanchit Vir Gogia, chief analyst at Greyhound Research, said Arora has a point, since a generic filter cannot know how a particular application is meant to behave. But most failures in AI applications happen at runtime, through prompts, tools, and permissions, whoever trained the model, he said. He noted that Palo Alto has sold model-agnostic protection through Prisma AIRS since 2025.

“A supervisor trained by the same hands as the system it supervises is a colleague, not a check,” Gogia said, adding that CIOs should make sure someone other than the trainer tests the result.

GPUs and data centers

To train and host those models, Palo Alto plans to build its own compute capacity, CFO Dipak Golechha said on the call. The spending “will mostly go to GPUs and servers,” with a smaller share for the facilities that house them, he said. Palo Alto will “continue to source from our cloud partners” and add dedicated capacity over time, Golechha said. Arora said the company aims for more than100 megawatts of compute in the next three to five years.

Gogia said Palo Alto has sovereignty credentials for security data, including customer-held keys and a European Cortex service governed by Deutsche Telekom. “None of that yet extends to GPUs,” he said, adding that a US-headquartered operator faces the same jurisdictional questions as the hyperscalers it rents capacity from.

“A sovereign hosting offer with no named region, operator or date is a promise looking for an address,” Gogia said. Compliance officers should ask Palo Alto for a region list, the operator, and a dated certification plan, he said.

Arora said on the call that the training and inference business could eventually exceed Palo Alto’s core market. “This could be bigger than what we do today,” he said.

One vendor, more of the stack

The deal follows Palo Alto’s $25 billion purchase of CyberArk, which added identity security to its platform. Gogia said Applied Compute would now add the models themselves to a supplier already at the center of many customers’ firewalls, SOC, and identity controls.

“Palo Alto is asking enterprises to let the guard build the vault it guards,” he said. One team watching both model and telemetry can spot trouble sooner, he said, but a single fault, breach or pricing dispute would land “on the protection and the protected together.”

CIOs should get a costed exit in writing before any model work moves across, Gogia said. Applied Compute customers should use the months before closing to secure ownership of trained weights in an exportable format, no training on their data without consent, a right to exit on change of control and price protection that survives bundling, he said. “Terms are cheapest to win while the suitor is still courting,” Gogia said.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250669/palo-alto-networks-bets-4-5b-on-enterprise-ai-with-applied-compute-acquisition.html, csoonline.com, 19.11.2026)

[41] SecurityWeek - JPMorgan, Visa, Mastercard Cite Third-Party Incidents Amid Data Leak
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 07:35:46 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/jpmorgan-visa-mastercard-cite-third-party-incidents-amid-data-leak/

[TAM METİN - 3501 kelime]
JPMorgan, Visa, Mastercard Cite Third-Party Incidents Amid Data Leak

The companies point to third-party providers after the N0n extortion group leaked data allegedly tied to their customers.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

JPMorgan Chase, Visa and Mastercard have disclosed incidents involving outside service providers after the N0n extortion group claimed it stole their customers’ data.
JPMorgan said in a post on X on Thursday that some of the companies providing services to the bank had been hit by a cyberattack.



“Based on our investigation so far, we believe the attackers accessed certain marketing data held by those vendors, which may include some customer names and email addresses. There is no disruption to our services,” the bank said.



Where appropriate, the bank plans to reach out to impacted customers and give them free credit monitoring. It said the incident has nothing to do with the problems some customers saw earlier this week.



In a statement to the media, Visa said the claims are “associated with an incident involving a third-party service provider in Asia Pacific.” The payments giant said neither its own systems nor payment data such as card numbers had been impacted.



Mastercard said in a statement to Reuters that it had recently learned of an incident “at a third-party provider that resulted in the exposure of limited amount of email addresses — non-payments information — from a small subset of cardholders enrolled in a specific benefit program.”Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Mastercard said its payment network was not affected and that cardholders can still use their cards.



On November 16, N0n claimed to have obtained JPMorgan-related engagement data spanning “hundreds of thousands of campaign interactions for tens of thousands of distinct cardmember email addresses,” according to Reuters. It made comparable claims about Visa and Mastercard the same day.



SecurityWeek has seen N0n’s leak site, where all three companies are marked as not having paid, and the allegedly stolen data is offered for download.



JPMorgan, Visa and Mastercard data leak

Reuters’ review of the JPMorgan-related data found over 16,000 customer emails, some accompanied by phone numbers and postal codes, along with what appear to be 124 email addresses belonging to bank employees.



The Visa files hold thousands of purported customer emails, phone numbers and marketing campaign records, mostly from Asia-Pacific markets, Reuters said. 



None of the three companies has identified the affected providers or said how many customers were impacted.



N0n, which emerged in September, currently lists roughly 200 alleged victims on its leak site.



Related: Nvidia Responds to Gentlemen Ransomware Claim
Related: Newly Patched VMware vCenter Flaw Exploited as Zero-Day by Chinese APT
Related: Hackers Exploit Cisco Expressway Vulnerability Soon After PoC Release

JPMorgan said in a post on X on Thursday that some of the companies providing services to the bank had been hit by a cyberattack.



“Based on our investigation so far, we believe the attackers accessed certain marketing data held by those vendors, which may include some customer names and email addresses. There is no disruption to our services,” the bank said.



Where appropriate, the bank plans to reach out to impacted customers and give them free credit monitoring. It said the incident has nothing to do with the problems some customers saw earlier this week.



In a statement to the media, Visa said the claims are “associated with an incident involving a third-party service provider in Asia Pacific.” The payments giant said neither its own systems nor payment data such as card numbers had been impacted.



Mastercard said in a statement to Reuters that it had recently learned of an incident “at a third-party provider that resulted in the exposure of limited amount of email addresses — non-payments information — from a small subset of cardholders enrolled in a specific benefit program.”Advertisement. [42] SecurityWeek - PHP Patches Seven Critical RCE Vulnerabilities
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 15:54:20 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/php-patches-seven-critical-rce-vulnerabilities/

[TAM METİN - 4992 kelime]
PHP Patches Seven Critical RCE Vulnerabilities

Successful exploitation could allow attackers to crash the worker process and execute code with the PHP worker’s privileges.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

PHP on Thursday rolled out a fresh set of security patches as part of its four-week update schedule, including fixes for seven critical-severity remote code execution (RCE) vulnerabilities.
First in line is CVE-2026-115560, which was publicly disclosed last month, two days after PHP2Shell patches landed. Residing in the session upload progress feature, it could be triggered to write attacker data using one session serialization format and then read it using another.



“A mismatched decoder can interpret part of a session key as serialized object data. An application with a suitable object gadget chain can then execute code with the privileges of the PHP worker,” PHP’s advisory reads.



Next in line is CVE-2026-129481, described as a use-after-free that occurs after a session shutdown has been interrupted, and which PHP describes as an incomplete fix for CVE-2026-109619, one of the two PHP2Shell flaws fixed in October and now widely exploited.



“An attacker who can cause an application’s session cleanup to fail in this way may corrupt memory or crash a reused PHP worker. The use-after-free could potentially lead to remote code execution, but code execution through this specific field has not been demonstrated,” PHP notes, adding that PHP 8.2 is not affected.



Another use-after-free, CVE-2026-129182, covers additional exploitation scenarios for CVE-2026-109619, an RCE via stale global state. According to PHP, however, RCE via these scenarios has not been demonstrated.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Impacting PHP 8.5 and later, CVE-2026-129474 is a use-after-free weakness that could allow an unauthenticated attacker to cause a fatal error and achieve RCE. “Exploitation depends on the application and requires control over enough request data to influence memory allocation, a reachable magic method call, and a fatal error at the relevant point,” PHP says.



Attackers able to influence session contents and interrupt the write of serialized data to a session file may trigger the deserialization of attacker-controlled content when the session is next opened. Tracked as CVE-2026-126714, the flaw could lead to RCE via a suitable object gadget chain in the application.



PHP also patched CVE-2026-129289, a PHP-FPM request smuggling issue caused by PHP reading a request-body stream lazily, treating a request body as handled during request shutdown and skipping the normal cleanup that discards unread input.



“PHP can fail to discard unread bytes from an HTTP request body before reusing a persistent PHP-FPM or LSAPI connection. In PHP-FPM, an unauthenticated attacker can make those bytes form a separate FastCGI request, bypass front-end routing and execute attacker-controlled PHP code with the privileges of the worker,” PHP explains.



The seventh critical-severity vulnerability patched today, CVE-2026-129496, resides in the timezone cache. PHP can retain a reference to it when writing a diagnostic to a PHP error log file during process startup, and a later diagnostic can access the freed cache before it is reset.



“On affected configurations, a remote attacker can trigger memory corruption, cause a worker to crash, and potentially execute arbitrary code with the privileges of the PHP worker,” PHP explains.



PHP 8.6.0 and PHP 8.5.13 were rolled out with fixes for all critical bugs and for 10 high and medium-severity flaws. Patches for most issues were also included in PHP 8.4.28, PHP 8.3.37, and PHP 8.2.36. More information is available on PHP’s news archive page.



Related: PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
Related: Hackers Exploit Cisco Expressway Vulnerability Soon After PoC Release
Related: Critical Apache Roller, Axis Vulnerabilities Exploited in Attacks
Related: Splunk Patches Nearly 140 Flaws Across Three Core Products

Attackers able to influence session contents and interrupt the write of serialized data to a session file may trigger the deserialization of attacker-controlled content when the session is next opened. Tracked as CVE-2026-126714, the flaw could lead to RCE via a suitable object gadget chain in the application.



PHP also patched CVE-2026-129289, a PHP-FPM request smuggling issue caused by PHP reading a request-body stream lazily, treating a request body as handled during request shutdown and skipping the normal cleanup that discards unread input.



“PHP can fail to discard unread bytes from an HTTP request body before reusing a persistent PHP-FPM or LSAPI connection. In PHP-FPM, an unauthenticated attacker can make those bytes form a separate FastCGI request, bypass front-end routing and execute attacker-controlled PHP code with the privileges of the worker,” PHP explains.



The seventh critical-severity vulnerability patched today, CVE-2026-129496, resides in the timezone cache. PHP can retain a reference to it when writing a diagnostic to a PHP error log file during process startup, and a later diagnostic can access the freed cache before it is reset.



“On affected configurations, a remote attacker can trigger memory corruption, cause a worker to crash, and potentially execute arbitrary code with the privileges of the PHP worker,” PHP explains.



PHP 8.6.0 and PHP 8.5.13 were rolled out with fixes for all critical bugs and for 10 high and medium-severity flaws. Patches for most issues were also included in PHP 8.4.28, PHP 8.3.37, and PHP 8.2.36. More information is available on PHP’s news archive page.



Related: PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
Related: Hackers Exploit Cisco Expressway Vulnerability Soon After PoC Release
Related: Critical Apache Roller, Axis Vulnerabilities Exploited in Attacks
Related: Splunk Patches Nearly 140 Flaws Across Three Core Products

Next in line is CVE-2026-129481, described as a use-after-free that occurs after a session shutdown has been interrupted, and which PHP describes as an incomplete fix for CVE-2026-109619, one of the two PHP2Shell flaws fixed in October and now widely exploited.



“An attacker who can cause an application’s session cleanup to fail in this way may corrupt memory or crash a reused PHP worker. The use-after-free could potentially lead to remote code execution, but code execution through this specific field has not been demonstrated,” PHP notes, adding that PHP 8.2 is not affected.



Another use-after-free, CVE-2026-129182, covers additional exploitation scenarios for CVE-2026-109619, an RCE via stale global state. According to PHP, however, RCE via these scenarios has not been demonstrated.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.

[43] SecurityWeek - Hackers Exploit Cisco Expressway Vulnerability Soon After PoC Release
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 13:12:20 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/hackers-exploit-cisco-expressway-vulnerability-soon-after-poc-release/

[TAM METİN - 2192 kelime]
Hackers Exploit Cisco Expressway Vulnerability Soon After PoC Release

Attackers hacked clustered Cisco Expressway devices at a Russian organization and prepared tooling for lateral movement.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Expressway X15.5.20 resolves the vulnerability. There are no workarounds, and customers on X14 or earlier releases need to move to a fixed release, Cisco says.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The advisory has not been revised since its initial release and still states that Cisco is unaware of public announcements or malicious use of the flaw. However, a PoC exploit has since been posted on GitHub, and F6 says the attack it investigated came shortly after.



According to F6, the attackers first breached two Expressway X15.5.3 devices exposed to the internet. They then pivoted from one of them to two other cluster nodes sitting on the organization’s internal network.



After securing root privileges, the attackers prepared tools for lateral movement. These included custom Python scripts designed to scan the network, locate SMB resources, interact with SQL Server and WinRM, and search GitLab repositories, aiming to exfiltrate credentials and other sensitive information.



F6 determined that the attackers modified the scripts on the fly, during the attack. “The source code contained characteristic comments and descriptions of fixes indicating the use of an AI assistant,” the company says.



F6 did not name the victim or attribute the attack to a known threat actor.



To detect exploitation, F6 points to the Erlang system log on Expressway devices. The security firm has also published IP addresses and file paths associated with the attack.



Although Cisco lists no workarounds, F6 recommends that organizations unable to patch immediately block connections to TCP ports 4371–4372 at the firewall, except from the IP addresses of trusted cluster nodes.



SecurityWeek has reached out to Cisco for comment on F6’s findings and will update this article if the company responds.



Related: Java4Shell Exploitation Attempts Begin as Vendors Rush Out Advisories
Related: F5 Patches Critical NGINX2Root Flaws as PoC Exploits Go Public
Related: PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw

Although Cisco lists no workarounds, F6 recommends that organizations unable to patch immediately block connections to TCP ports 4371–4372 at the firewall, except from the IP addresses of trusted cluster nodes.



SecurityWeek has reached out to Cisco for comment on F6’s findings and will update this article if the company responds.

[44] SecurityWeek - StepSecurity Secures $20 Million to Expand Supply Chain Protection
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 12:23:34 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/stepsecurity-secures-20-million-to-expand-supply-chain-protection/

[TAM METİN - 2134 kelime]
Cybersecurity Funding

StepSecurity Secures $20 Million to Expand Supply Chain Protection

The company will use the funds to accelerate the development and distribution of its security platform.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Supply chain security startup StepSecurity has raised $20 million in a Series A funding round, which brings the total raised by the company to $23 million.
The new investment round was led by Elephant, with additional support from Runtime Ventures, Inner Loop Capital, and SaaS Ventures.



Founded in 2021, Seattle-based StepSecurity has built a platform that combines developer-machine protection, repository checks, a hosted upstream proxy and policy gate, and a CI/CD security agent to identify and block malicious components.



According to the company, its platform can secure every stage of the software development process, including developers’ systems, AI coding agents, repositories, package registries, and CI/CD runners.



The startup offers a free community tier of its CI/CD runtime security agent Harden-Runner, already used by over 20,000 open source projects, which now also integrates a free Secure Registry proxy and policy gate tier to help open source maintainers block compromised packages.



StepSecurity will use the fresh funding to accelerate the development and distribution of its platform by growing its engineering team, enhancing the security research team’s capabilities, and expanding go-to-market efforts.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“We believe independent controls at all control points in agentic software pipelines are the strongest defense for agentic software development,” said StepSecurity co-founder and CTO Ashish Kurmi.



“We have seen our customers adopt one product, find value, and ask us to protect the next part of their development process. This funding gives us the resources to respond faster,” Kurmi added.

Related: Pretxt Emerges Stealth With $5M Seed and an AI Supervision Tool
Related: Corma Receives $125 Million to Train Foundation Models for Defensive Cybersecurity
Related: Daylight Lands $80 Million for AI-Powered Security Operations

================================================================================

[45] SecurityWeek - The Governance Gap in Open-Weight AI and What to Do About It
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/the-governance-gap-in-open-weight-ai-and-what-to-do-about-it/

[TAM METİN - 6884 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

The Governance Gap in Open-Weight AI and What to Do About It

Lower costs, data privacy, and greater control are driving open-weight AI adoption, but many organizations are not addressing the risks that come with it.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Open-weight AI has gone mainstream. As of September 2026, some estimates suggest 42% of enterprises surveyed were running open-weight models in production, up from 31% just two months earlier. But downloading a model also means taking on responsibilities that a provider might otherwise handle, and many organizations have yet to put clear ownership and controls in place.



Renting Capability Vs. Running Your Own Copy
When you use an AI model through a provider’s app or service, you’re essentially renting its capabilities. The provider controls the model and handles the infrastructure, updates, and safety controls. You access it through an app or a software connection.



An open-weight AI model makes its learned parameters, the ‘weights’ that shape its behavior, available to download and run yourself. You can run the model on your own infrastructure and adapt it for a particular use case using your own data. The license may limit how you can use it, and running it still carries infrastructure and staffing costs.



Open-weight models are becoming more capable. One estimate puts the best open-weight models roughly four months behind leading models.



Open-weight does not necessarily mean open-source. Access to the weights does not guarantee access to the training code or details of the training data.



When Benefits Clash with Risks
Zoey is a bank analyst using a provider-hosted model to summarize a loan application. The model does a good job of delivering an actionable summary, but to do so, her request, loan file included, has to go to the provider’s servers. The customer’s data is no longer solely in the bank’s hands. A few quarters later, the provider raises its prices and changes its policies in ways that force workflow changes. Switching providers would take time and effort, so Zoey and her team have little choice but to go along, even though it hurts both their budget and their operations.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Let’s look at the same scenario from the perspective of an open-weight model that is running on the bank’s server. If the model is configured to run entirely within the bank’s system, the loan file can stay there too. The bank can apply its own access controls to the model and the data.



In the above example, an open-weight model does seem like a good option, but there are risks that are easy to overlook:




Trojan horses: Some model files can execute malicious code when loaded. Others may contain hidden behavior that a particular input can trigger.


An origin story with missing chapters: A popular model on a public repository may not be the developer’s original. Someone may have modified and reuploaded it without explaining what changed. That version could contain a hidden backdoor or have weak safety rules.


The upgrade won’t knock on your door: A developer may release improved versions, but your copy is not updated automatically. Your team must track updates, test them, and decide when to switch.


Attack surface just got bigger: Running your own model creates new targets for attackers. You are essentially adding to your IT infrastructure. This includes the servers and GPUs that keep the model running and the systems that retrain it.

Controls to Address Risks
If an open-weight AI model makes sense for your organization, governance should walk in lockstep with deployment:




Set a regular review schedule for updates and security notices. Decide who approves a move to a new version.
Assign an owner for each model, with clear responsibility for approving its use, reviewing updates, and responding when something goes wrong.
Record each model’s source, version, license, and every fine-tune. Also record its file hash. It’s a unique fingerprint that changes if anyone alters the file.
Give teams an approved internal repository to fetch models. Before adding a model, verify its source and check its file fingerprint or digital signature against a trusted reference.
The rule of thumb should be: ‘trust after testing.’ Test the model as an attacker might. Try to bypass its safety rules and look for unexpected behavior, including whether it produces insecure code.
If you do choose to download a modified version, run the same tests on the developer’s original. This may help reveal weak safeguards or unexpected behavior.


Higher-risk models need more control. For example, a model that reads source code should also run in isolation, with no access to passwords or keys, and a person assigned to review its output.
Run the model on a separate server while giving it only the access it needs. Monitor all requests and outputs for unusual behavior, while protecting sensitive information in the logs. If you think a model is misbehaving, have a backup in the form of an earlier version you can switch back to quickly.

Open-weight models need to be scrutinized like any other software brought inhouse. Their benefits are clearly driving their popularity, but there are tradeoffs to be aware of. It’s wise to put ownership and controls in place before putting them into production.
