If you do choose to download a modified version, run the same tests on the developer’s original. This may help reveal weak safeguards or unexpected behavior.


Higher-risk models need more control. For example, a model that reads source code should also run in isolation, with no access to passwords or keys, and a person assigned to review its output.
Run the model on a separate server while giving it only the access it needs. Monitor all requests and outputs for unusual behavior, while protecting sensitive information in the logs. If you think a model is misbehaving, have a backup in the form of an earlier version you can switch back to quickly.

Open-weight models need to be scrutinized like any other software brought inhouse. Their benefits are clearly driving their popularity, but there are tradeoffs to be aware of. It’s wise to put ownership and controls in place before putting them into production.



Learn More at the AI Risk Summit

Open-weight models are becoming more capable. One estimate puts the best open-weight models roughly four months behind leading models.



Open-weight does not necessarily mean open-source. Access to the weights does not guarantee access to the training code or details of the training data.



When Benefits Clash with Risks
Zoey is a bank analyst using a provider-hosted model to summarize a loan application. The model does a good job of delivering an actionable summary, but to do so, her request, loan file included, has to go to the provider’s servers. The customer’s data is no longer solely in the bank’s hands. A few quarters later, the provider raises its prices and changes its policies in ways that force workflow changes. Switching providers would take time and effort, so Zoey and her team have little choice but to go along, even though it hurts both their budget and their operations.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Let’s look at the same scenario from the perspective of an open-weight model that is running on the bank’s server. If the model is configured to run entirely within the bank’s system, the loan file can stay there too. The bank can apply its own access controls to the model and the data.



In the above example, an open-weight model does seem like a good option, but there are risks that are easy to overlook:




Trojan horses: Some model files can execute malicious code when loaded. Others may contain hidden behavior that a particular input can trigger.


An origin story with missing chapters: A popular model on a public repository may not be the developer’s original. Someone may have modified and reuploaded it without explaining what changed. That version could contain a hidden backdoor or have weak safety rules.


The upgrade won’t knock on your door: A developer may release improved versions, but your copy is not updated automatically. Your team must track updates, test them, and decide when to switch.


Attack surface just got bigger: Running your own model creates new targets for attackers. You are essentially adding to your IT infrastructure. This includes the servers and GPUs that keep the model running and the systems that retrain it.

Controls to Address Risks
If an open-weight AI model makes sense for your organization, governance should walk in lockstep with deployment:




Set a regular review schedule for updates and security notices. Decide who approves a move to a new version.
Assign an owner for each model, with clear responsibility for approving its use, reviewing updates, and responding when something goes wrong.
Record each model’s source, version, license, and every fine-tune. Also record its file hash. It’s a unique fingerprint that changes if anyone alters the file.
Give teams an approved internal repository to fetch models. Before adding a model, verify its source and check its file fingerprint or digital signature against a trusted reference.
The rule of thumb should be: ‘trust after testing.’ Test the model as an attacker might. Try to bypass its safety rules and look for unexpected behavior, including whether it produces insecure code.
If you do choose to download a modified version, run the same tests on the developer’s original. This may help reveal weak safeguards or unexpected behavior.


Higher-risk models need more control. For example, a model that reads source code should also run in isolation, with no access to passwords or keys, and a person assigned to review its output.
Run the model on a separate server while giving it only the access it needs. Monitor all requests and outputs for unusual behavior, while protecting sensitive information in the logs. If you think a model is misbehaving, have a backup in the form of an earlier version you can switch back to quickly.

Open-weight models need to be scrutinized like any other software brought inhouse. Their benefits are clearly driving their popularity, but there are tradeoffs to be aware of. It’s wise to put ownership and controls in place before putting them into production.



Learn More at the AI Risk Summit

Etay Maor is Vice President of Threat Intelligence at Cato Networks, a founding member of Cato CTRL, and an industry-recognized cybersecurity researcher. Prior to joining Cato in 2021, Etay was the chief security officer for IntSights (acquired by Rapid7), where he led strategic cybersecurity research and security services. Etay has also held senior security positions at Trusteer (acquired by IBM) and RSA Security’s Cyber Threats Research Labs. Etay is an adjunct professor at Boston College and is part of the Call for Paper (CFP) committees for the RSA Conference and Qubits Conference. Etay holds a Master’s degree in Counterterrorism and Cyber-Terrorism and a Bachelor's degree in Computer Science from IDC Herzliya.

[46] SecurityWeek - Splunk Patches Nearly 140 Flaws Across Three Core Products
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 10:41:05 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/splunk-patches-nearly-140-flaws-across-three-core-products/

[TAM METİN - 2614 kelime]
Splunk Patches Nearly 140 Flaws Across Three Core Products

The security updates resolve critical and high-severity issues leading to authentication bypass, remote code execution, and privilege escalation.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Splunk on Wednesday announced patches for nearly 140 vulnerabilities in its Enterprise, Enterprise Security, and SOAR products.
Enterprise received fixes for 38 security defects, including two critical and 13 high-severity flaws that could allow unauthenticated attackers to bypass authentication, inject arbitrary code, and cause denial-of-service (DoS) conditions.



The critical bugs, tracked as CVE-2026-76536 and CVE-2026-76535, could allow attackers to read and modify clustering configuration using an empty clustering shared secret, or read or modify data and configurations by running arbitrary Search Processing Language (SPL) searches.



Fixes were included in Splunk Enterprise versions 10.6.1, 10.4.5, 10.2.9, 10.0.12, and 9.4.17.



Splunk released Enterprise Security and Splunk App for PCI Compliance version 8.7.0 with patches for 42 vulnerabilities, including a critical flaw (CVE-2026-131202) leading to remote code execution (RCE). It can be exploited by an attacker with the ‘ess_admin’ role by combining lookup file upload and Configuration Checker functionality.



The security updates also resolve 11 high-severity code injection and privilege escalation issues leading to data tampering, service disruption, and command execution with elevated privileges.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Splunk SOAR version 8.8.0 arrived on Wednesday with fixes for 59 security bugs, including two critical flaws leading to remote code execution (CVE-2026-131253 and CVE-2026-131287). Both require authentication.



The SOAR refresh also resolves 27 high-severity information disclosure, missing authorization, RCE, path traversal, XSS, improper authorization, improper access control, and privilege escalation weaknesses.



On Wednesday, Splunk also announced fixes for three vulnerabilities in App for Stream, four flaws in AI Assistant, and nearly 160 bugs in third-party packages for Enterprise, Enterprise Security, and SOAR.



The company makes no mention of any of these security defects being exploited in the wild. More details are available on Splunk’s security advisories page.

Related: PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
Related: Oracle Patches 950 Vulnerabilities With November 2026 CSPU

The security updates also resolve 11 high-severity code injection and privilege escalation issues leading to data tampering, service disruption, and command execution with elevated privileges.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Splunk SOAR version 8.8.0 arrived on Wednesday with fixes for 59 security bugs, including two critical flaws leading to remote code execution (CVE-2026-131253 and CVE-2026-131287). Both require authentication.



The SOAR refresh also resolves 27 high-severity information disclosure, missing authorization, RCE, path traversal, XSS, improper authorization, improper access control, and privilege escalation weaknesses.



On Wednesday, Splunk also announced fixes for three vulnerabilities in App for Stream, four flaws in AI Assistant, and nearly 160 bugs in third-party packages for Enterprise, Enterprise Security, and SOAR.



The company makes no mention of any of these security defects being exploited in the wild. More details are available on Splunk’s security advisories page.

================================================================================

[47] SecurityWeek - F5 Patches Critical NGINX2Root Flaws as PoC Exploits Go Public
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 10:26:51 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/f5-patches-critical-nginx2root-flaws-as-poc-exploits-go-public/

[TAM METİN - 6390 kelime]
F5 Patches Critical NGINX2Root Flaws as PoC Exploits Go Public

The five NGINX2Root vulnerabilities could allow remote, unauthenticated attackers to execute arbitrary code.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

F5 on Wednesday announced patches for 22 vulnerabilities, including five critical-severity NGINX bugs that could lead to unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE).
Referred to as NGINX2Root, the five issues are tracked as CVE-2026-113425, CVE-2026-121810, CVE-2026-123007, CVE-2026-123633, and CVE-2026-123634 (CVSS score of 9.2).



CVE-2026-113425 affects NGINX instances configured as mail proxy servers and can be exploited via specially crafted mail sessions. CVE-2026-121810 can be exploited on servers that use HTTP/3 to trigger a use-after-free in the NGINX worker process.



CVE-2026-123634 impacts servers configured as proxies and can be triggered if the proxied server name includes variables, and a subrequest to a location resolving that server name is received from a Server Side Includes (SSI) or mirror directive.



CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633 require HTTP/2 to be enabled on an affected server. Successful exploitation of the former leads to a buffer overflow, while the latter triggers a use-after-free in the worker process.



The two issues were independently reported by Ikotas Labs, Metnir, Rapid7, Wiz, and Xint. They explain that attackers could exploit them to run commands with the privileges of the nginx worker process and fully compromise the server.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“Our testing of CVE-2026-123007 demonstrated code execution on NGINX serving ordinary static files, without a backend application or third-party modules,” Wiz explains, noting that the flaw affects all NGINX iterations since 1.9.5 (released in 2015).



Xint says it chained CVE-2026-123007 with a Linux kernel vulnerability to escape a Docker container and gain root privileges on the underlying host.



According to F5, code execution is only possible if ASLR is disabled or bypassed. However, Wiz says CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633 can themselves be used to bypass ASLR, and Rapid7’s public exploit defeats the protection by leaking memory addresses from server responses.



PoC code available
Rapid7 has published proof-of-concept (PoC) code targeting CVE-2026-123007, but warns that successful exploitation for RCE requires “a significant amount of network traffic to be transmitted, approximately 128 GB of HTTP/2 data.”



Depending on network bandwidth and connection timeouts, exploitation could take between two minutes and one hour. On slow connections, NGINX’s default one-hour connection lifetime prevents exploitation.



“Additionally, should an HTTP/2 connection be proxied, the precise attacker-controlled HTTP/2 frames being transmitted will likely not be retransmitted in a way that satisfies exploiting the vulnerability,” Rapid7 notes.



Internet intelligence provider Censys says it tested the NGINX2Root flaws in its lab and was able to reproduce CVE-2026-123007’s behavior using cleartext HTTP/2 without generating the large traffic volume reported by Rapid7.



Censys published its PoC on GitHub, warning that there are approximately 9 million “NGINX services on an affected version with HTTP/2 on” and that 103 million additional services do not reveal their version.



F5 patches
F5 resolved the vulnerabilities in NGINX Open Source versions 1.30.7 and 1.31.9, and in NGINX Plus versions 37.0.8.1 and 37.3.0.1.



The updates also include patches for five flaws that can lead to SSL/TLS connection termination, certificate verification bypass, segmentation faults, worker process termination, and denial-of-service (DoS) by placing the worker process in an infinite loop.



Additionally, the company rolled out patches for 12 BIG-IP bugs, including critical and high-severity issues that could lead to RCE, DoS, file manipulation, and token lifetime manipulation.



F5 makes no mention of any of these vulnerabilities being exploited in the wild. More information is available in the company’s out-of-band security notification.



Related: PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
Related: Java4Shell Exploitation Attempts Begin as Vendors Rush Out Advisories
Related: Chrome 158 Patches 204 Vulnerabilities

F5 resolved the vulnerabilities in NGINX Open Source versions 1.30.7 and 1.31.9, and in NGINX Plus versions 37.0.8.1 and 37.3.0.1.



The updates also include patches for five flaws that can lead to SSL/TLS connection termination, certificate verification bypass, segmentation faults, worker process termination, and denial-of-service (DoS) by placing the worker process in an infinite loop.



Additionally, the company rolled out patches for 12 BIG-IP bugs, including critical and high-severity issues that could lead to RCE, DoS, file manipulation, and token lifetime manipulation.



F5 makes no mention of any of these vulnerabilities being exploited in the wild. More information is available in the company’s out-of-band security notification.



Related: PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
Related: Java4Shell Exploitation Attempts Begin as Vendors Rush Out Advisories
Related: Chrome 158 Patches 204 Vulnerabilities
Related: Critical Perforce Puppet RCE Exploited in Attacks

“Our testing of CVE-2026-123007 demonstrated code execution on NGINX serving ordinary static files, without a backend application or third-party modules,” Wiz explains, noting that the flaw affects all NGINX iterations since 1.9.5 (released in 2015).



Xint says it chained CVE-2026-123007 with a Linux kernel vulnerability to escape a Docker container and gain root privileges on the underlying host.



According to F5, code execution is only possible if ASLR is disabled or bypassed. However, Wiz says CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633 can themselves be used to bypass ASLR, and Rapid7’s public exploit defeats the protection by leaking memory addresses from server responses.



PoC code available
Rapid7 has published proof-of-concept (PoC) code targeting CVE-2026-123007, but warns that successful exploitation for RCE requires “a significant amount of network traffic to be transmitted, approximately 128 GB of HTTP/2 data.”



Depending on network bandwidth and connection timeouts, exploitation could take between two minutes and one hour. On slow connections, NGINX’s default one-hour connection lifetime prevents exploitation.



“Additionally, should an HTTP/2 connection be proxied, the precise attacker-controlled HTTP/2 frames being transmitted will likely not be retransmitted in a way that satisfies exploiting the vulnerability,” Rapid7 notes.



Internet intelligence provider Censys says it tested the NGINX2Root flaws in its lab and was able to reproduce CVE-2026-123007’s behavior using cleartext HTTP/2 without generating the large traffic volume reported by Rapid7.



Censys published its PoC on GitHub, warning that there are approximately 9 million “NGINX services on an affected version with HTTP/2 on” and that 103 million additional services do not reveal their version.



F5 patches
F5 resolved the vulnerabilities in NGINX Open Source versions 1.30.7 and 1.31.9, and in NGINX Plus versions 37.0.8.1 and 37.3.0.1.



The updates also include patches for five flaws that can lead to SSL/TLS connection termination, certificate verification bypass, segmentation faults, worker process termination, and denial-of-service (DoS) by placing the worker process in an infinite loop.



Additionally, the company rolled out patches for 12 BIG-IP bugs, including critical and high-severity issues that could lead to RCE, DoS, file manipulation, and token lifetime manipulation.



F5 makes no mention of any of these vulnerabilities being exploited in the wild. More information is available in the company’s out-of-band security notification.

[48] Help Net Security - European Commission confirms AI-generated cyberattack disrupted its systems
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 08:52:29 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/european-commission-ai-generated-cyberattack-publicly-available-tool/

[TAM METİN - 767 kelime]
European Commission confirms AI-generated cyberattack disrupted its systems

The European Commission has confirmed it was hit by a cyberattack that misused a publicly available AI tool and forced the institution to take several of its applications offline.

Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier gave the confirmation at Thursday’s midday press briefing in Brussels. Politico was the first to report the attack, in an exclusive published earlier that day.

Reporters used the Q&A session to press Regnier on which systems were hit, whether staff data was stolen and who was behind the attack. On the AI angle, Regnier said that “following our investigation, we can confirm that this was an AI generated cyber campaign,” describing it as “a misuse of an AI tool.” Later in the session he added that “it was an AI tool that is publicly available.”

He did not name the tool, and he did not address a reporter’s request to confirm that it was a Chinese AI tool used to identify vulnerabilities.

Regnier opened his answers with a general statement on AI and security. “While AI can of course help to detect vulnerabilities and prevent cyberattacks, it can of course be misused for cyber operations,” he began, adding that “no one is immune to this.” He also mentioned the action plan on cybersecurity and AI that the Commission presented in July, noting it “shows that we are very well aware of what’s going on around us.”

According to the spokesperson, the incident “was detected on the 22nd of October and it was confirmed following the initial investigation on the 26th of October.” The Commission then opened an investigation and took preventive steps.

Among those steps, the Commission “temporarily took down several applications” as “a protective and preventive measure,” Regnier explained, and “most of them have since then been restored.” He did not say which Commission services were targeted.

The Commission is in “close contact with both CERT-EU and the European Data Protection Supervisor,” he added, and the investigation is ongoing.

No sign of a staff data breach so far

Asked whether personal data had been taken, Regnier responded that “at this stage of the investigation, we have not detected any evidence of a data breach affecting Commission staff as a whole.”

He would not say whether the attack has ended, though “many elements have been contained, so that’s the good news.” Conclusive remarks, he made plain, would have to wait until the investigation wraps up.

One reporter asked whether the attackers had demanded money. “I’m really not aware of any ransomware request made to the Commission, so definitely not,” Regnier replied.

“So on the attribution, I will not go there,” Regnier said when asked who was behind the attack, citing the ongoing probe. When a journalist asked what the attackers wanted, he answered that “it’s not a question for me, that’s more a question for the threat actor.”

He also declined to say whether the Commission used AI tools to defend itself, explaining that for security reasons he would not go into “the measures we’re taking and which tools we’re using.” On whether other EU institutions had been compromised, he replied, “I’ll not speak on behalf of other institutions, I only speak on behalf of ours.”

CERT-EU warned about ARTEX

Politico reported that CERT-EU detected the attackers using ARTEX, a publicly available AI tool for finding weaknesses in IT systems.

CERT-EU had warned about ARTEX on 5 November, describing it as “an AI-powered penetration-testing framework” whose original repository was pulled in October after reports of misuse, though forks are still available. The agency said it had seen similar AI-driven activity against the EU bodies it protects, highly likely involving ARTEX, and cautioned that “ARTEX’s public availability will likely accelerate adoption by threat actors.”

A journalist wanted to know why the Commission had said nothing about an incident that took place weeks earlier, until Politico broke the story.

Regnier argued that measures taken against a cyber campaign stay out of public view “for them to be effective.” The Commission followed what he called “the processes as per usual practice,” informing CERT-EU, the European Data Protection Supervisor and the affected services within the institution.

“There was no hiding whatsoever,” he insisted. “It’s just the Commission taken and following the usual procedural steps.”

Reporters also wanted to know whether the incident deserved the “huge” label Politico had given it. “I’m not going to qualify the scale and extent of this cyber attack,” Regnier told the room. “It is a campaign, so there was a series of incidents indeed, and that’s all I can share with you at this stage.”

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/european-commission-ai-generated-cyberattack-publicly-available-tool/, helpnetsecurity.com, 20.11.2026)

[49] Help Net Security - Your AI red team may be finding the same prompt injection thousands of times
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 06:30:20 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/ai-prompt-injection-red-team/

[TAM METİN - 815 kelime]
Your AI red team may be finding the same prompt injection thousands of times

AI agents retrained on a bigger pile of prompt injection attacks came out easier to hijack in three of four tests run by researchers at the University of Waterloo, Zhejiang University and KTH Royal Institute of Technology. The automated red-teaming tool that found more working attacks produced the weaker defense.

Prompt injection purple-teaming workflow (Source: Research paper)

Someone hides instructions in an email, a web page or a tool’s output, and the AI agent that reads it follows them: forward this file, send this payment. To harden an agent, developers point automated attack tools at it, keep every injection that works and retrain the agent on the pile. So when a vendor says its agent was tested against thousands of successful attacks, it’s fair to wonder how many of them were really the same attack.

The researchers set up a simple contest. Two published attack tools, PISmith and RL-Hammer, went after the same small model, Qwen3-4B, across 560 practice tasks. PISmith looked like the winner by a mile. It broke into 68.75% of the tasks, about twice RL-Hammer’s 33.39%, and came back with 7,132 working attacks to RL-Hammer’s 4,282.

The team retrained the model on each tool’s haul using reinforcement learning, then threw three attacks at it that it had never seen. At its worst, the version trained on PISmith’s bigger pile got hijacked 47.5% of the time. The RL-Hammer version topped out at 27.5%.

That’s 19 hijacked tasks against 11, out of 40, so it’s a small test, and a tough one. The team picked those 40 because simple attacks had already broken them on an unprotected Qwen3-4B, so read the numbers as a stress test on known weak spots. Still, the same pattern showed up in three of the four setups the team ran.

Same trick, new wording

The team blames the gap, at least in part, on attack tools that get stuck. Once a tool finds a trick that works, such as telling the agent the user’s task is already finished, it tends to reuse that trick on task after task. Both tools include checks meant to force variety, but the checks measure differences in wording. RL-Hammer’s authors reported that their tool satisfied its check by changing capital letters or adding irrelevant text.

If the researchers are right, the agent ends up retrained on thousands of rewordings of a few attacks, which closes a few holes and can leave the rest open. By their own measure, both tools’ collections stopped covering much new ground early in training. In that case, the bigger collection bought more copies, not more coverage.

The fix, and its limits

The researchers built a system called PIPETTE that sorts successful attacks by technique and gives the attack tool an extra reward when a working attack uses a technique it hasn’t found before. PIPETTE then retrains the agent to check, before each action, whether that action serves what the user asked for. That step matters because agents hardened with standard methods can learn to quit whenever data looks suspicious, which stops the attack and the user’s task together.

On AgentDojo, a public test bed of everyday agent tasks, off-the-shelf injection attacks almost never worked against PIPETTE-trained agents, getting through on less than half a percent of attempts, on average, on both small models.

Attackers that kept rewriting their injections for around 250 tries did better, breaking into up to 10% of those same 40 weak spots on the three models tested this way. And the agents didn’t get timid. The hardened Qwen3-4B finished slightly more clean tasks than the original, 64.95% against 61.86%.

Some other defenses stumble once an attack shows up. A detection tool like PromptGuard throws out any email or document it flags, and with it the data the agent needed. Under one attack on Qwen3-4B, PromptGuard blocked a bit more than PIPETTE did, but the agent behind it finished the user’s real task only 16.19% of the time. PIPETTE’s agent finished it 58.10% of the time.

The four models tested ranged from 4 billion to 27 billion parameters, all open-weight. The adaptive attackers ran on a 4-billion-parameter model against 40 tasks, and the authors say a bigger budget may get through more often. They have not checked the grader that scores technique coverage against human judgment, and they do not claim narrow coverage is the sole cause of the gap. On GPT-OSS-20B, one search-based attacker still got past the hardened agent on 4 of 40 tasks.

A count of successful attacks measures how busy the red team was. The more useful numbers are how many distinct techniques the testing covered, how the agent held up against an attacker allowed hundreds of attempts, and how often the hardened agent still finished the job it was given.

[50] Help Net Security - It’s time to retire vanity security dashboards
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 06:00:21 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/security-dashboards-tips-video/

[TAM METİN - 167 kelime]
It’s time to retire vanity security dashboards

In this Help Net Security video, Abdul Khadir Mahamed, OT Security Architect at Estée Lauder Companies, explains why security teams should retire vanity dashboards in IT and OT environments.

He walks through an example from a manufacturing site, where the same approved maintenance work triggers the same alert week after week. Analysts close thousands of tickets, but the underlying problem stays. He describes how SOC and control engineering teams can review these patterns together, tune detection rules with the right context, and test that unexpected activity still raises an alert.

He applies the same thinking to vulnerability dashboards. A lower severity finding on a server reachable through a supplier connection may need more attention than a high severity issue on a restricted workstation.

Mahamed argues that security dashboards should show what exposure was removed, who owns the next action, and whether that action worked, not just how many alerts or findings were closed.

[51] Help Net Security - AI agent API security hasn’t caught up with agents already running in production
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 05:30:11 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/ai-agent-api-security-production/

[TAM METİN - 563 kelime]
AI agent API security hasn’t caught up with agents already running in production

Postman asked nearly 9,000 developers, architects and executives how they’re handling AI agents, and the answers tell a familiar story. Companies put the new thing to work first and left the question of who holds the keys for later. A quarter of the organizations that answered already have agents calling their APIs in production.

The keys are where it gets uncomfortable. About four in ten respondents haven’t worked out how credentials and identity should work for agents at all. Three in ten let agents log in with the same credentials as any other API client, so the agent shows up looking like every other script on the network. If it starts reaching for data it shouldn’t, the company has no separate identity to rein in or cut off. That is the scenario respondents worry about most: an agent getting into data or endpoints it was never authorized to touch.

Agents got access before they got rules

Picture an agent as a new hire who skipped orientation. It can read your systems, call your services and act on its own, but at many companies it never got a badge, a manager or an exit interview.

Companies are far more likely to have dedicated security controls for their APIs than for the agents calling them. A third have no formal process for managing agents. Fewer than a quarter keep a list of the agents they run, or monitor what those agents do. And 9% have a process for retiring an agent, which means most of these companies can switch one on with no plan for switching it off.

The best-run shops share keys too

Postman sorts respondents by how API-first they are, meaning whether they design the API before the software around it. The companies that call themselves API-first are better organized almost everywhere you look. They’re more likely to keep an inventory of their agents, name an owner for each one, and give every agent its own identity.

They also run a lot more agents, and 43.2% of them let those agents share ordinary credentials. Among companies that aren’t API-first at all, the figure is 23.0%. More agents in production means more of them using borrowed keys, even at the companies doing the most homework. The researchers are careful to say the survey shows correlation and not cause, and that its API-first group leans toward executives and directors, whose view from the top may not match what their teams do day to day.

Most APIs were built for people

A quarter of organizations run agents in production, but only 13.2% say their APIs are ready for agents to use. Most APIs were designed for a human developer who reads the docs and messages a colleague when something doesn’t make sense. An agent can’t do the second part. Outdated documentation was already the top complaint among the developers surveyed, and for an agent it can mean the end of the road.

Respondents seem to sense where this goes. Asked about the next two years, they ranked AI-powered attacks and rogue agents misusing APIs as joint top worries, with attacks climbing since last year.

Postman’s first suggestion is to give every agent its own identity. About a third of the companies surveyed have already done it.

[52] Help Net Security - Most CIOs and CISOs expect to contain attacks, though few can reach their gear right away
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Fri, 20 Nov 2026 05:00:27 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/network-outage-recovery-resilience/

[TAM METİN - 497 kelime]
Most CIOs and CISOs expect to contain attacks, though few can reach their gear right away

Most organizations can’t quickly get secure management access to their own critical network infrastructure once the primary network goes down. Opengear surveyed 514 CIOs and CISOs and only a small share said they could get that access immediately or close to it.

That gap bites hardest during a ransomware attack or a bad update, the moments when someone has to log in, cut off what’s compromised and bring services back. If the only path to the equipment runs over the network that just failed, recovery waits until an engineer can get there in person. The CrowdStrike outage in July 2024 showed what that looks like at scale, with millions of Windows machines that had to be fixed one at a time, largely by hand.

Getting in is only the start. The researchers point out that teams still have to triage the incident, find where the problem sits and decide how to respond, and the sooner they have access, the sooner that work begins. One answer is out-of-band management, a separate connection to the equipment that stays up when the main network goes down.

Teams expect to contain attacks but can’t get in fast

The researchers sorted respondents into two groups based on how well they could recover remotely, how often they tested that, and how much they relied on sending engineers onsite. Even the better-prepared group had trouble with speed. Among them, 93% were confident they could isolate compromised systems during a cyber incident while keeping access to everything else. Only 15% of that same group said they could get secure management access right away if the primary network failed.

Ninety-three percent expect to contain an attacker. Fifteen percent can get in quickly. Isolating a compromised system means reaching it first, and every figure here is self-reported, so the confidence is the softer of the two numbers.

AI and edge projects are stalling

The problem grows as companies push computing out to more places. Edge infrastructure, meaning equipment in branch offices and other remote locations, often sits well beyond easy physical reach. Nearly three in four respondents said their organization had delayed, scaled back or paused at least one AI, edge or distributed infrastructure project in the past year over concerns about resilience, remote access or recovery.

The better-prepared group reported this almost as often as everyone else, so experience with resilience doesn’t make the problem go away. In both groups, fewer than a third had secure remote access and recovery in place before those systems went live.

Test with the network off

About one in six organizations in each group said they don’t formally test network resilience at all. Fewer than one in four respondents said their organizations consider resilience from a project’s first planning stage. Teams that plan access that early have a second way in already installed when an incident hits.

[53] Help Net Security - Android developer verification will reach every install source, with a side door for power users
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 23:03:24 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/20/android-developer-verification-power-users/

[TAM METİN - 383 kelime]
Android developer verification will reach every install source, with a side door for power users

Google will extend Android developer verification to every country starting in January 2027, a check that already applies at participating app stores in four countries. Developers who have not verified their identity with Google and registered their apps will see those apps lose the normal install experience on certified Android devices.

The rollout opens with seven app distributors: Google Play, Galaxy Store, Honor, Oppo, Xiaomi, Transsion and Vivo. Over several months it widens to every installation source, which brings sideloaded apps and open-source registries under the same rule as the big stores. Developers who ship outside Google Play, and the people who install their apps, have the most to sort out.

Four countries went first

The protections went live Sept. 30 for participating stores in Brazil, Indonesia, Singapore and Thailand. Google and its partners had prepared for more than a year, working with distributors and Android communities, said Matthew Forsythe, Google’s director of product management for Android app safety. Verification opened to all developers in March. Millions of apps have been registered since then, and Google says they make up over 99% of Android installs worldwide.

The unregistered remainder accounts for under 1% of installs. That is a small number unless the app is yours.

Google says registering an app takes less than ten minutes. Students and hobbyists can use limited distribution accounts, launched in August, which skip registration fees and government ID checks. Google raised their sharing cap from 20 to 50 authorized devices.

Teams with many apps can automate the work inside continuous integration pipelines, the systems that build and ship code automatically. Google offers two APIs for this: the Android Developer ID Status API and the Android Developer Console API. Play developers have one more item to check. Any app they also distribute outside Play needs to be registered too.

If you maintain an app on an open-source registry, Google has published a separate guide for registering existing and new apps there.

A side door for power users

“We built an advanced flow that allows experienced users to install unregistered apps when they choose to accept the risk, while keeping critical guardrails in place against social engineering scams for everyday users,” Forsythe said.

[54] Help Net Security - PHP 8.6 is out, and here’s what changes for your code
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 15:22:36 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/php-8-6-released/

[TAM METİN - 345 kelime]
PHP 8.6 is out, and here’s what changes for your code

PHP 8.6 has been released. The new version adds partial function application, a Time\Duration class, a polling API for event loops and a clamp() function.

Failed writes now report failure

fclose(), file_put_contents() and copy() return false when flushing or closing a file fails. Earlier versions ignored that failure, so a write that broke at the last step could still look like it succeeded. Many other functions now throw a ValueError on out-of-range values, invalid flags, or strings that contain NUL bytes. You can also set stream operations to throw a StreamException. The exception carries structured error objects that name the wrapper and the path or URL involved.

What you can write now

Partial function application lets you fill in some of a function’s arguments ahead of time. You get back a closure that waits for the rest, and a ? marks each argument still to come:

Time\Duration measures elapsed time the way a stopwatch does, with no clock time or timezone attached. It covers more than 250 years at nanosecond precision, which is more than any retry delay or timeout will need. Io\Poll watches many streams at once through epoll, kqueue, event ports or WSAPoll, without the limits of stream_select(). PHP’s developers built it as a base for event-loop libraries such as ReactPHP and AMPHP. And clamp(110, 0, 100) returns 100.

The release also adds a SortDirection enum, default values for readonly properties, and post-quantum key encapsulation in Sodium (X-Wing and ML-KEM-768). TLS streams gain support for TLS 1.3 early data.

What’s on the way out

The mbstring regex functions, including mb_ereg() and mb_split(), are deprecated because Oniguruma, the regex library behind them, is no longer maintained. PHP’s developers recommend PCRE with the u modifier instead. is_double(), is_long(), is_integer() and doubleval() are deprecated in favor of is_float(), is_int() and floatval(), and spl_object_hash() is deprecated in favor of spl_object_id(). Using return inside a finally block is deprecated too. One behavior change applies right away: trim(), ltrim() and rtrim() now strip form feeds (\f) by default.

[55] Help Net Security - Russian spies turn one cloud breach into a stepping stone for the next
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 14:42:20 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/russian-spies-impersonation-cloud-breach/

[TAM METİN - 732 kelime]
Russian spies turn one cloud breach into a stepping stone for the next

A Russian cyberespionage group is posing as employees of organizations it has already breached to phish new targets, Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) found.

Google tracks the group as COLD RELIC (also known as Star Blizzard, Callisto and Gossamer Bear), and the US Department of Justice has assessed that it operates within Russia’s Federal Security Service (FSB).

COLD RELIC goes after government officials, defense contractors and NGOs in Europe and North America.

“The group is known for implementing sophisticated phishing and malware campaigns with the aim to gain access to information of strategic or political relevance to the Russian government, as well as to leverage compromised access to target compromised individuals’ broader social and professional networks,” researchers wrote.

The report’s authors say the group has drawn less public attention than other Russian groups while still breaking into high-value targets. “Once COLD RELIC compromises a target, it uses stolen emails and data to establish authentic pretexts and impersonate contacts when reaching out to subsequent targets,” they added.

A Super Admin account and a flood of MFA prompts

The first intrusion began with stolen credentials for a Super Admin account. The attackers logged in through a US residential proxy close to the victim’s location, so the sign-ins would not trigger alerts for impossible travel, and kept sending MFA push requests until the account holder approved one.

With admin rights in hand, they downloaded the contents of the organization’s entire cloud drive, exported employee lists and started a domain-wide data export through Google Workspace Customer Takeout. They searched the audit logs to see what defenders could see, and “viewed, modified, and deleted Alert Center notifications to remove forensic evidence of the initial intrusion.”

To keep their access, they enrolled passkeys, MFA backup codes and MFA devices of their own. They stayed inside for several months. During that time they synced inboxes to outside email clients, and their passkeys “persisted across password and session resets and bypassed device registration controls.”

Some employees had stored passwords in the company’s cloud storage, which made the damage worse. The attackers used those to get into other accounts, including employees’ personal accounts, “which are typically unmonitored by their employers.”

Even after the organization removed the intruders, GTIG observed COLD RELIC send phishing emails from accounts posing as its employees to their colleagues and to people in the same professional circles.

A familiar name on an encrypted messaging app

In 2026, someone posing as an employee of the first victim contacted a person at a second US organization over an encrypted messaging app. Partway through the conversation, the attacker shared phishing links.

“These phishing links, designed to steal credentials and authentication tokens for Entra ID, redirected the victim to document lures relevant to the ongoing conversation in order to avoid raising suspicion. Upon successful authentication using the stolen credentials, COLD RELIC registered and used a new MFA device in the SSO solution to retain access to the user account throughout the intrusion,” they explained.

“By impersonating someone known in the same professional circles as the new target, COLD RELIC increased their chance of phishing success.”

The group’s phishing links tied the two cases together. COLD RELIC usually sends targets through a compromised website that checks each visitor before passing them on to the phishing domain, and the attacker often adds a link parameter that names a previous victim or the lure. A redirector URL from the second intrusion was submitted to VirusTotal in June 2026, and its parameter referenced the first victim organization.

After the break-in, the attacker changed their profile name on the messaging app “to a completely new person.” GTIG believes the new persona was probably used to approach other organizations in the same circles.

Password resets weren’t enough

In at least one case, a victim spotted the intrusion and reset the passwords on affected accounts. COLD RELIC had hijacked sessions that were never revoked, so it kept its access, and Google warns that “password changes alone are insufficient.”

“COLD RELIC’s campaigns demonstrate the convergence of human-centric social engineering and cloud identity abuse. By obtaining access to strategic personnel rosters and operational documents, Russian intelligence services turn legitimate business communications into attack vectors against trusted partners. Their ability to navigate across multiple cloud ecosystems highlights that organizations must defend their entire identity estate holistically,” Google concluded.

[56] Help Net Security - Exploited Puppet flaw puts the server that configures your endpoint fleet at risk (CVE-2026-115731)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 13:07:41 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/19/puppet-enterprise-vulnerability-cve-2026-115731-exploited/

[TAM METİN - 654 kelime]
Exploited Puppet flaw puts the server that configures your endpoint fleet at risk (CVE-2026-115731)

Attackers are exploiting CVE-2026-115731, a critical remote code execution vulnerability in Perforce Puppet Enterprise, an enterprise solution used by IT teams to centralize and automatize endpoint management.

Details about the in-the-wild attacks have yet to be made public, but the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) added the flaw to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog on Tuesday and ordered US federal civilian agencies to address it by December 1, 2026.

Perforce Puppet explained

Perforce Puppet works on a client-server model: a central Puppet Server holds the configuration code that describes how each managed machine should be set up, and a Puppet agent installed on every endpoint periodically checks in with the server, retrieves its configuration and applies it.

Agents and server authenticate each other with certificates issued by the Puppet Server’s own certificate authority (CA).

About CVE-2026-115731

CVE-2026-115731 is a deserialization of untrusted data vulnerability in Puppet’s report-processing code, i.e., the part of Puppet Server that ingests the reports agents send back after each configuration run.

A client holding any certificate signed by the Puppet CA can submit a report containing a crafted rich-data type via a PUT request and get the server to execute arbitrary code with the privileges of the Puppet Server service user, CISA explained.

It might seem that requiring a signed agent certificate is a meaningful barrier to exploitation, but if the CA signs certificate requests without strong verification (e.g., when autosigning is enabled) an unauthenticated attacker on the network could obtain a certificate and exploit the flaw.

“If exploited, this vulnerability could allow an attacker to run arbitrary code on the Puppet Server with the privileges of its service account. This could give the attacker access to sensitive data held on the server (Confidentiality), let them modify data and configuration on the server (Integrity), or disrupt the service (Availability),” Centre for Cybersecurity Belgium noted.

Patch and check for evidence of compromise

CVE-2026-115731 affects:

Puppet Enterprise 2023.8.0 before 2023.8.13, 2025.0.0 before 2025.12.1, and 2026.0.0 before 2026.0.1

Puppet Core 8.x before 8.22.2 and 9.0.0 before 9.1.3

OpenVox, a community-maintained open-source Puppet fork, is also affected: OpenVox Agent versions before 9.3.0 and 8.32.0 and OpenVox Server versions before 9.2.0 and 8.18.0 are vulnerable.

Perforce released security updates fixing CVE-2026-115731 and CVE-2026-115732, a certificate name authorization bypass flaw that was also reported by Alain Rödel of Neodyme AG, on November 3.

“Customers should upgrade to the latest applicable security release and then restart the server as soon as possible. Fixed versions are Puppet Enterprise/Advanced 2026.0.1, 2025.12.1, or 2023.8.13, and Puppet Core 9.1.3 or 8.22.2,” the company said and advised customers who are unable to upgrade immediately to review their autosign configuration and authorization rules as a temporary mitigation.

Patching should be a priority for any organization that relies on autosigning or whose Puppet Server is reachable from untrusted networks (it listens on port 8140 by default).

Admins should also check the list of signed certificates for nodes they don’t recognize and look for unexpected processes, files or outbound connections tied to the Puppet Server service user.

If the server shows signs of compromise, patching is not enough: the CA should be treated as compromised and rebuilt.

The OpenVox project’s GitHub advisory, published alongside the fixes, includes a proof-of-concept malicious report (in the form of a JSON event) that runs a shell command on the OpenVox / Puppet server.

“In configuration with autosign (which is common for enterprise deployment), this is an unauthenticated attack chain to compromise the OpenVox / Puppet server, as a valid cert can be requested via the OpenVox / Puppet client enrollment endpoint,” the maintainers noted.

“Code execution as puppet gives access to the CA signing key. This allows the attacker to sign certificates with EKU critical, serverAuth, clientAuth, which could enable direct command execution on other hosts if certain kinds of orchestration is configured.”

Lawmakers launch new AI safety caucus amid rising security concerns

A bipartisan group of than two dozen House lawmakers announced Thursday they are joining a new AI Safety and Innovation Caucus to confront the security risks posed by evolving and increasingly sophisticated artificial intelligence models.

The July breach of Hugging Face by an OpenAI agent that escaped a sandbox — and the subsequent Anthropic warning that its own AI models also broke out of testing environments to obtain unauthorized access to third-party organizations — have “underscored the need for Congress to better understand these risks and develop policy solutions,” according to a press release from Rep. George Whitesides (D-CA), a caucus co-chair.

“As AI capabilities accelerate, so do the potential consequences of catastrophic misuse, loss of control over advanced systems, and threats to national security.”

Whitesides and co-chairs Reps. Nathaniel Moran (R-TX) and Don Beyer (D-VA) called for bipartisan collaboration to forge basic guardrails in the face of accelerating risks, citing Americans’ well-documented concerns about the technology.

Seven of 26 founding members of the caucus are Republicans, a notable number given the White House’s steadfast opposition to regulating AI. The caucus is focused on finding “targeted” solutions that will not imperil innovation, Whitesides said in a statement.

The lawmakers will push bipartisan legislation to augment AI safety and quickly respond to AI security incidents by gathering AI developers, independent experts and government officials to brief lawmakers and help develop solutions, the press release said.

It will aggressively pursue relationships with outside experts who can help lawmakers navigate complicated AI policy decisions, focus on raising public awareness and give members outside of relevant committees the tools needed to contribute to relevant legislation and debate, according to the press release.

Midterm polling showed more than half of American voters considered AI a major issue, and 43% said they trust neither party to properly manage the issue, according to the press release.

“By automating vulnerability discovery and exploit development, AI tools have drastically reduced the technical skills required for offensive cyber operations,” a caucus one-pager said.

“As threat actors deploy these capabilities at scale, they pose acute risks to critical infrastructure, sensitive personal data, and financial sector stability.”

[58] The Record - DOJ, DHS partner on new unit tackling scams and cybercrime
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 17:25:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/doj-dhs-partner-on-new-unit-tackling-scams-cybercrime

[TAM METİN - 766 kelime]
DOJ, DHS partner on new unit tackling scams and cybercrime

The Department of Justice and Department of Homeland Security announced Thursday the creation of a new task force aimed at disrupting the flow of victim funds to foreign criminal networks powering scams, cybercrime and sextortion schemes that target Americans.

The departments are partnering on the Threat Disruption Center to better triage victim complaints, map cybercriminal networks and provide a direct channel to private sector organizations that work with victims.

Senior FBI official Heith Janke said Threat Disruption Center members have already worked together on efforts to warn thousands of victims before they lose money to scammers, and the center will allow them to better coordinate their efforts.

“The Threat Disruption Center builds on those efforts by providing advanced tools and operational capabilities that enable faster action at greater scale, strengthening the work of every agency on the task force,” Janke said.

TRM Labs, which is providing technology support for the effort, said that the center has already moved to take down more than 1,600 scam websites, added more than 6,600 scam-linked cryptocurrency wallet addresses to a screening network and identified 101 new fraud networks for investigation.

The center is part of the federal government’s efforts to implement an executive order issued in March centered around stopping cyber scams, which continue to siphon billions of dollars each year from unwitting Americans.

Cyber scam losses reported to the FBI’s Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3) grew to $20.9 billion in 2025 — a number that does not account for the thousands of people who never file victim reports.

The staggering losses have prompted multiple concurrent efforts by the federal government and Congress to disrupt the scams and provide better avenues for victims to report crimes.

Much of the center's work will focus on collaborating with tech companies that get a firsthand view on criminal network activity because of how many gangs use social media platforms, web hosting, domain infrastructure, cryptocurrency services and banking systems.

“The Center establishes a standing mechanism for law enforcement and participating companies to appropriately share intelligence in near real-time, and convert that intelligence into action, for example, by pausing victim payments; taking down malicious web domains; or banning social media accounts used by scammers,” federal officials said in a statement.

“Participating private-sector companies can take these actions independently under their terms of service, while law enforcement agencies proceed with investigations, prosecutions, and court-authorized seizures.”

The center is being housed within the Homeland Security Task Force — a permanent interagency law enforcement network of more than 30 federal agencies and over 440 state and local partners that focus on transnational criminal organizations, cartels and foreign terrorist organizations.

Prince Group sanctions

The initiative is just one part of a multi-prong effort the U.S. is undertaking to address cyber scams. On Thursday, for example, the Treasury Department issued more sanctions on scam operators in Myanmar and affiliates of the Prince Group, a Cambodian company accused of managing an empire of scam centers across Southeast Asia.

The sanctions include Zhao Hui, a Cambodian national who leased a scam center from Prince Group and paid the company fees to run the compound. U.S. officials accused Zhao of being directly involved in the scam center’s operations — which targeted Americans and Europeans with cryptocurrency investment scams.

Two of Hui’s companies, Bei Fang Bo Lin Construction Decoration and ZHZ Investment, are included in the sanctions.

The Treasury Department also sanctioned two leaders running the Democratic Karen Benevolent Army (DKBA), an armed group backing scam centers located along the Thailand-Myanmar border.

General Saw Shwe Wah is the commander-in-chief of the DKBA and Colonel Saw A One is a commander who runs the Three Pagodas Pass in Karen State near the Thai border. Both own land along the border and provide security to scam compound operators.

"These scammers rarely stop after one payment — they drain a victim's savings, or their retirement, over months and years," Esteban Castaño, CEO of TRM Labs, said of the DOJ and DHS joint venture. "Stepping in sooner changes everything: a warning before the next payment, a scam website removed before it victimizes another family, or stolen funds frozen while they can still be recovered. That's the difference between a recoverable setback and a loss from which an entire family may never recover.”

is a Breaking News Reporter at Recorded Future News. Jonathan has worked across the globe as a journalist since 2014. Before moving back to New York City, he worked for news outlets in South Africa, Jordan and Cambodia. He previously covered cybersecurity at ZDNet and TechRepublic.

[59] The Record - CISA nominee LoSapio vows to bolster cyber defenses while denying Jan. 6 Capitol attack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 17:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/cisa-losapio-nomination-trump

[TAM METİN - 576 kelime]
CISA nominee LoSapio vows to bolster cyber defenses while denying Jan. 6 Capitol attack

President Donald Trump’s pick to lead the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency on Thursday vowed to uncover any “gaps” within the organization that have been created by massive personnel and funding cuts.

“I'm going to get involved and understand where the gaps are and I will come back to this committee with the gap analysis,” Mike LoSapio, Palantir’s chief information security officer, told the Senate Homeland Security Committee.

LoSapio was nominated earlier this month to helm the nation’s cyber defense agency, which has been without a Senate-confirmed chief for almost two years. A previous nominee, Sean Plankey, withdrew from consideration earlier this year after spending months in limbo on Capitol Hill. Nick Andersen has led CISA in an acting capacity since February.

The agency has been a target of Trump’s ire over the role it played in debunking the conspiracy theories he spread that the 2020 presidential election was rigged. Since he returned to the Oval Office, CISA has lost one-third of its staff and had its budget slashed by millions.

In a brief nomination hearing, LoSapio described CISA as “effectively the cybersecurity version of the 911 system. You pick up the phone, you say you have an emergency, you need help.”

“If confirmed, I intend to bring together the community and the national labs to help collaborate and coordinate and to give defenders the tools they need to defend their networks,” including AI tools, he told the panel.

In his written statement to the committee, LoSapio also pledged to “recruit and keep talented people, give them clear priorities, and make sure every dollar Congress provides is spent with discipline and tied to measurable results.”

However, he demurred when asked to weigh in on the recent technical outages that have upended key sectors of the U.S. as he’s “not inside” the agency today. And he stopped short of saying CISA’s budget needed to be increased to meet digital threats.

“I don't necessarily believe more money means more security. I believe spending money on the right solutions in the right ways actually decreases risk,” LoSapio told Sen. Elissa Slotkin (D-Mich.).

He later testified that if analysis showed the need for more funding he would “come back to this committee and request for more resources.”

When asked by Sen. Richard Blumenthal (D-Conn.) who won the 2020 presidential election, LoSapio repeatedly declined to directly answer.

“I'm not going to answer the question about an election six years ago,” he said. “My only involvement in the 2020 election was voting for the president of the United States.”

LoSapio also disagreed with Blumenthal when the policymaker asked if the Capitol was attacked on January 6, 2021.

“No, I don't believe there was an attack. No,” he said under persistent questioning.

In response, Blumenthal told LoSapio he was “disqualified from the position that you've been nominated to fill.”

The committee likely won’t vote on LoSapio’s nomination until after next week’s Thanksgiving recess. If advanced, he could face a full floor vote during the final weeks of the lame-duck session of Congress.

is the senior cybersecurity reporter for The Record. Prior to joining Recorded Future News in 2021, he spent more than five years at Politico, where he covered digital and national security developments across Capitol Hill, the Pentagon and the U.S. intelligence community. He previously was a reporter at The Hill, National Journal Group and Inside Washington Publishers.

[60] The Record - UK court confirms British government served Apple with secret encryption notice
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 16:15:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/apple-uk-encryption-notice

[TAM METİN - 899 kelime]
UK court confirms British government served Apple with secret encryption notice

The British government served Apple with a secret legal notice targeting the company’s cryptographic protections for iCloud accounts, a tribunal has confirmed, ending almost two years of official refusal to acknowledge what had been widely reported.

In a judgment handed down Thursday, the Investigatory Powers Tribunal — the only court in the country that can hear certain national security cases — rejected the government’s “neither confirm nor deny” policy based on the argument that even confirming the existence of the notice would damage national security.

The court’s three judges stated explicitly that the government “has given Apple a technical capability notice,” referencing the legal power which the government used to demand that Apple retain the ability to access the content of iCloud accounts.

At the core of the dispute is Apple’s Advanced Data Protection (ADP) feature which generates and stores encryption keys on users’ own devices instead of Apple’s servers. Because Apple never holds the keys, it is technically unable to access the content of iCloud accounts even when served with a lawful request.

Last February, Apple withdrew ADP for British users entirely after receiving the technical capability notice, as was first reported by The Washington Post. Although the details of the notice are not public, it appears that Apple effectively complied by withdrawing ADP for British users.

Since the initial report about the notice, Westminster officials have continued to repeatedly state they do “not comment on operational matters, including for example confirming or denying the existence of any such notices.”

Addressing Parliament earlier this year, Security Minister Dan Jarvis again declined to respond after the issue was raised by a lawmaker, and said that not doing so was “a long-standing position held by successive Governments for obvious reasons of national security.”

Those “obvious reasons” were challenged by the tribunal, as well as in comments from both within the U.K. intelligence community and from academic experts.

In a preliminary judgment last April, the tribunal cited numerous public comments by senior U.S. politicians, including President Donald Trump, that appeared to confirm the existence of the notice, with Trump comparing the British government’s move to “something that you hear about with China.”

The then-U.S. Director of National Intelligence Tulsi Gabbard ordered a legal review of the secret directive in February 2025, and said she had a “grave concern” about its implications. In its judgment, the tribunal also referenced numerous British media reports about the notice, citing government officials as sources, as additional justification for finding against the “neither confirm nor deny” policy.

Experts, including from Britain’s own intelligence community, have argued the government’s attempts to access encrypted messaging platforms should be more transparent. Academics described the Home Office's ongoing refusal to either confirm or deny the legal demand as unsustainable and unjustifiable.

Tim Stevens, the head of the King's Cyber Security Research Group at King's College London, previously told Recorded Future News that a default of no comment left the government unable to control the story when details came out.

Sir Jeremy Fleming, the former director of GCHQ, told the Munich Cyber Security Conference in 2025 that greater transparency was needed to protect the agencies’ “license to operate.”

In 2018, GCHQ technical officials argued in an essay for Lawfare that the details of any exceptional access scheme would probably become public anyway, and that criminals would not simply switch services once it was known to exist. The tribunal found that confirmation of a notice’s existence would not tell adversaries anything that they could not already find elsewhere.

Andrew Dwyer, a lecturer in information security at Royal Holloway, University of London, argued that a notice capable of removing an encryption service from millions of users is not like a live operation and should not be shielded in the same way.

The ruling does not make a finding about whether the notice issued to Apple is lawful. A substantive hearing has been scheduled for the first half of 2027.

The tribunal's April 2025 judgment, quoted again in this ruling, noted that the government can choose to withdraw or settle proceedings rather than depart from its “neither confirm nor deny” approach.

A government spokesperson said: “Terrorists, child abusers and organised crime groups cannot be allowed to operate beyond the reach of the law. The right to privacy is crucial but it should not come at the expense of the safety of the British public.

“A judge can authorise access to a criminal’s home, a parcel they’ve sent, or a car they drive. The online world is no different. These powers are subject to strict safeguards and the government never has direct access to a company’s communications systems or data.

“Apple has been helping the police and intelligence agencies pursue criminals and bring them to justice for several years, in accordance with the law. This Technical Capability Notice simply requires that they maintain the ability to do so.

“Decisions affecting the safety of the British public must be made by democratically elected governments and independent judges, not in the boardrooms of technology companies.”

Editor's note: Story updated at 19:35 GMT with a government spokesperson's statement.

is the UK Editor for Recorded Future News. He was previously a technology reporter for Sky News and a fellow at the European Cyber Conflict Research Initiative, now Virtual Routes. He can be reached securely using Signal on: AlexanderMartin.79

[61] Talos Intelligence - The internet needs a nap (and you do, too)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 19:00:00 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/the-internet-needs-a-nap-and-you-do-too/

[TAM METİN - 1217 kelime]
The internet needs a nap (and you do, too)

“Not everything that is faced can be changed; but nothing can be changed until it is faced.” — James Baldwin

I can start with conversation on the most viral cybersecurity news of the week. So obviously I had to go with PHP... oh wait, Java. Let’s type this up... wait, Outlook, Apple? The NSA? As soon as I start to type nginx comes pounding on my door. It’s the kind of news cycle that makes me wonder if the internet is trying to tell us something. I think it just wants everyone to stop asking it for things for a few hours, and can you blame it?

These are different events with different causes. A vulnerability is not an outage, and an outage is not automatically an attack. But they all arrive in the same place: someone’s queue, someone’s pager, someone’s overflowing calendar. The headlines might be viral and “new,” but the work they create is not.

For all of us, the work starts with knowing what is in the environment. Which systems run PHP or Java? Where is nginx deployed? Who owns those devices and services? Are they still supported? Are they properly admin’d? In every environment there is an application that everyone knows is important, and no one is quite sure how to patch it without breaking something else (or everything else). Isolating these devices and applications and getting them into the modern era is a challenging lift, but it’s beyond time and a big part of “know your environment.”

Then there’s the telemetry. A new vulnerability or a service disruption can make ordinary activity look strange, and strange activity can disappear into the noise when everyone is busy chasing the latest alert. Teams need time to understand what “normal” looks like in your own network and ensure that your tools are being leveraged to their full potential by your smart people. Because people still have to decide what it means and what to do next.

“Patch faster” isn’t an answer, and it’s not realistic for anyone.

The new normal is a steady stream of things that demand attention, each one landing on systems and people already carrying too much. The answer is still the same unglamorous work: Know your environment, keep it as up to date as possible and watch it carefully for the anomalies, and most importantly give the people doing the work enough room to do it well.

“Every quality that made a person more valuable than a database was a product of experience.” — Ted Chiang

Cisco Talos is tracking the disclosure of "Java4Shell" (CVE-2026-114938), a critical vulnerability in OpenJDK with a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. This flaw can lead to unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE) when a malformed Base64 input is sent to affected Java Virtual Machines. The vulnerability impacts OpenJDK Java versions 17.0.6 and later running on x86-64 CPUs with AVX-512 VBMI support enabled. While we haven't observed in-the-wild exploitation yet, the vulnerability has already been successfully tested against a suite of Java applications.

This vulnerability requires a specific subset of processors that support AVX-512 VBMI, which includes commonly deployed hardware from both Intel and AMD. Because of this, there is a large potential attack surface of vulnerable systems spanning both on-premise and cloud environments. Furthermore, as additional targets are tested and confirmed, that attack surface is likely to grow, and we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure.

Defenders should apply the software update for this vulnerability. If immediate patching is not possible, you can mitigate the threat by starting the JVM with AVX-512 support disabled using "=XX:UseAVX=2". Keep in mind this mitigation is only applicable for x86-64 systems, as ARM-based processors will not recognize the command and will likely fail to start.

Deploy the Snort rules and review the Splunk detections mentioned in the blog to help detect malformed Base64 data and suspicious post-exploitation indicators. We will continue to monitor for exploitation attempts and provide updates accordingly.

[62] Talos Intelligence - CVE-2026-114938: Critical vulnerability in OpenJDK Java
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 01:53:03 GMT
Link: https://blog.talosintelligence.com/java4shell/

[TAM METİN - 430 kelime]
CVE-2026-114938: Critical vulnerability in OpenJDK Java

Update 11/18/2026: Cisco has released a security advisory on products potentially affected by this vulnerability.

Cisco Talos is tracking the disclosure of a critical vulnerability in OpenJDK Java (CVE-2026-114938), dubbed “Java4Shell,” with a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2 that may lead to unauthenticated remote code execution (RCE). This vulnerability can be triggered by sending a malformed Base64 input to affected JVMs, which can result in RCE.

This vulnerability affects OpenJDK Java versions 17.0.6 and later running on x86-64 CPUs with AVX-512 VBMI (Vector Byte Manipulation Instructions) support enabled. These vulnerabilities have already been tested against a suite of Java applications with success, but the attack surface is likely much larger as additional targets are tested and confirmed.

This vulnerability does require a specific subset of processors that support AVX-512 VBMI; these include commonly deployed processors from both Intel and AMD, resulting in a large potential attack surface of vulnerable systems, including both on-premise and cloud environments.

Cisco Talos would like to thank Calif.io for sharing the details of the vulnerability in advance of public disclosure.

Observed exploitation

Talos has not observed in-the-wild exploitation of this vulnerability yet. However, we anticipate probing attempts may pick up soon after public disclosure. Talos will continue to monitor for exploitation attempts and will provide updates accordingly.

Recommendations and guidance

Talos recommends applying the software update for this vulnerability. If immediate patching is not possible, other mitigations can be implemented in the interim. The primary mitigation is starting the JVM with AVX-512 support disabled. This can be done by starting the application with “=XX:UseAVX=2”. Note that this is only applicable for x86-64 systems, as ARM-based processors will not recognize the command, likely resulting in failure to start.

Cisco is following its well-established process to evaluate its products and customers are strongly advised to refer to Cisco’s security advisory on potentially affected products. Customers should reach out to their sales teams with specific questions.

Snort2: 1:67502 - 1:67513

Snort3: 1:301754, 1:67502 - 1:67513

Free community rules are live through our Community Ruleset.

These rules detect malformed Base64 data in specific HTTP request fields associated with the vulnerability. This vulnerability is implementation specific. Talos made every effort to identify as many common vulnerable applications as possible and cover potential attack vectors in those applications.

Splunk customers can refer to Splunk’s ES Content Updates (ESCU) pre-packaged security content for detections against high-profile vulnerabilities. Existing detections from Splunk cover suspicious indicators such as heavily encoded content, and post-exploitation scenarios such as arbitrary command execution or processes and web or application servers spawning shells.
