[63] Mandiant (Google Cloud) - ARMORY CASTLE Exploits vSphere
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 14:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/armory-castle-exploits-vsphere/

[TAM METİN - 3814 kelime]
ARMORY CASTLE Exploits vSphere

Stop attacks, reduce risk, and advance your security.

Written by: Jayson Frost, Bryce Greathouse, Dhanesh Kizhakkinan, John Palmisano, Steve Rasch, Daniel Sislo, John Wolfram

On Oct. 15, 2026, Mandiant and Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) published a blog post detailing recent activity in which the suspected PRC-nexus threat actor ARMORY CASTLE (formerly tracked as UNC5221) exploited CVE-2026-102764, a zero-day vulnerability in BeyondTrust Remote Support, which was patched in BT26-05. We also described some of the threat actor’s post-exploitation operations. In this blog we describe additional post-compromise activity involving the exploitation of a vulnerability in VMware vCenter and the deployment of additional malware families.

On Nov. 19, 2026, VMware disclosed CVE-2026-123890, which impacts VMware vCenter Server deployments. vSphere consists of both ESXi (the hypervisor) and vCenter Server. The vCenter server is a centralized platform deployed as a Photon Linux virtual appliance and is used to configure, control, and monitor ESXi host environments. CVE-2026-123890 is an unauthenticated Secure Remote Password (SRP-6a) session key disclosure vulnerability over Distributed Computing Environment/Remote Procedure Calls (DCE/RPC). Successful exploitation results in a remote authentication bypass which creates an authenticated session established as administrator@vsphere.local. Using the authenticated administrator session, the attacker can execute arbitrary commands and acquire files, including the data.mdb database, by leveraging the vCenter Server Directory Service (vmdird) RPC interface vmdirdbcp. We strongly recommend that affected organizations immediately apply the security patches provided by Broadcom to remediate the vulnerability.

ARMORY CASTLE exploited this vulnerability in early September 2026 using BONESAW, according to forensic evidence. BONESAW is a utility primarily built to facilitate remote exploitation written in Golang , which utilizes CVE-2026-123890 to exfiltrate the arbitrary Lightning Memory-Mapped Database (LMDB), hijack the SAML Security Token Service (STS), and create a Single Sign On (SSO) Administrator account.

ARMORY CASTLE’s use of BONESAW represents significant new insight into this threat actor’s post-exploitation tactics. In this campaign, ARMORY CASTLE exploited two zero-days, and deployed three sets of malware specifically tailored for operating on targeted devices, indicating deep platform expertise and substantial resource backing. We are publishing this analysis, along with mitigation and hardening guidance, to support defenders in detecting this threat.

Moving laterally from the Remote Support device, ARMORY CASTLE established a foothold on an internal Linux appliance, escalated privileges to root, and staged a suite of malware to support expanding access, persistence, and targeting of vCenter. ARMORY CASTLE exploited a zero-day vulnerability to gain access to the VMware server, and likely used an additional set of malware customized to this environment to conduct reconnaissance, credential theft, and data theft.

Figure 1: ARMORY CASTLE lateral movement and vCenter compromise sequence of events

Following the establishment of persistence and the deployment of lateral movement capabilities on the Remote Support appliance, Mandiant observed ARMORY CASTLE conducting internal reconnaissance. This reconnaissance was heavily focused on VMware systems, based on the specific ports ARMORY CASTLE scanned and the URIs the group visited such as /sdk and /ui. The group leveraged nmap to scan the internal environment for systems running open ports specific to VMware, namely ports 9080, 5480, and 2012. Notably, port 2012 is used as the control interface RPC for vCenter Single Sign-On (SSO) and is an integral part of ARMORY CASTLE’s remote exploitation of CVE-2026-123890 through BONESAW.

Figure 2: ARMORY CASTLE nmap port scanning

ARMORY CASTLE moved laterally to internal Linux appliances within the environment that lacked endpoint detection and response (EDR) tooling. ARMORY CASTLE deployed five newly identified malware families and utilities: the SLIMSAVAGE and CAGEMATCH webshells, the BEARBONES backdoor, the BONESAW remote exploitation utility, and the SAWSNAKE SAML utility. ARMORY CASTLE also deployed the LAZYBONES backdoor, which Mandiant and GTIG previously observed in a separate ARMORY CASTLE intrusion.

To establish a foothold on the first Linux appliance, the actor dropped a webshell ActiveSer.jsp, tracked by Mandiant as SLIMSAVAGE. SLIMSAVAGE functions as an upgraded command execution webshell that utilizes the authentication secret pwd=123.

Figure 3: SLIMSAVAGE Webshell

By leveraging ProcessBuilder("/bin/sh", "-c", ...), the SLIMSAVAGE webshell executed commands inside a command line interpreter, enabled shell pipelines, redirects, creation of environment variables, and chained execution. The webshell reads from both standard output (getInputStream()) and standard error (getErrorStream()), ensuring full diagnostic visibility. It also calls Process.waitFor() and appends the final process return code (\nEXIT:<code>).

Roughly 4 hours later, ARMORY CASTLE deployed an additional webshell to the Linux appliance, u.jsp, tracked as CAGEMATCH. While SLIMSAVAGE provided the group with a robust capability to execute commands on the appliance, the group was limited in their ability to transfer data. To solve this challenge, CAGEMATCH acts as a dedicated payload staging webshell. The webshell checks for a multipart/form-data request header, parses the boundary delimiter, and extracts raw file bytes along with the original filename.

Figure 4: CAGEMATCH File Staging Logic

CAGEMATCH creates the /tmp directory if necessary, saves the uploaded payload directly to /tmp/<filename>, and echoes the absolute target path back to the client. When accessed without an upload payload, it returns a minimal HTML upload form. This provides the attacker with a streamlined post-exploitation workflow: using CAGEMATCH to upload binary payloads, scripts, or tools from the Remote Support appliance directly into /tmp, and subsequently invoke SLIMSAVAGE to adjust execution permissions and execute the staged tools.

To escalate privileges to root on the Linux appliance, ARMORY CASTLE repeated the same escalation technique used on the Remote Support appliance through the enablement of Set User ID (SUID) permissions on the binary /bin/env. ARMORY CASTLE performed this permission modification using the GNU Debugger, gdb. By enabling SUID permissions on the binary /bin/env, any user would be allowed to execute commands as root.

Figure 5: ARMORY CASTLE gdb Privilege Escalation

After successfully escalating privileges to root, ARMORY CASTLE began staging additional tooling on the Linux appliance to enable lateral movement within the environment. ARMORY CASTLE used curl to download the RAR utility from the Remote Support appliance.

Figure 6: ARMORY CASTLE downloads staged RAR utility

ARMORY CASTLE then utilized curl to download a rar archive 123.rar which was subsequently extracted using their downloaded RAR binary.

Figure 7: ARMORY CASTLE extracts BONESAW

Tracked as BONESAW, /tmp/123 is a remote exploitation utility written in Golang that leverages CVE-2026-123890 in vCenter to enable remote arbitrary LMDB exfiltration, SAML STS hijacking, and SSO administrator account creation. Mandiant observed ARMORY CASTLE leveraging this utility to target an internal vCenter server, successfully creating an SSO admin account, exfiltrating and parsing the LMDB which enabled lateral movement to the server.

BONESAW achieves full system compromise against vCenter Server 7.x and 8.x appliances through the exploitation of CVE-2026-123890. This vulnerability exploits a cryptographic logic and authorization flaw in VMware's rpc_srp_verifier service over port 2012, exposing a shared session key that can be used to create authenticated sessions as administrator@vsphere.local. BONESAW also provides the ability to stream the raw underlying LMDB file for offline parsing, extract the VMware Security Token Service (STS) SAML private RSA signing key, create a backdoor machine account via vmdir, forge administrative SAML 2.0 assertion tokens, and assign Global Administrator privileges on the vCenter root folder via the vSphere SOAP API.

While CVE-2026-123890 shares some overlap with CVE-2026-59309, primarily the obtaining of the shared session key "K" without knowing the password, there are multiple key differences between them. CVE-2026-59309 is a flaw in the vCenter Server vmdir daemon that allows a remote, unauthenticated attacker with network access to TCP ports 389 or 636 to send crafted LDAP packets with malformed authorization tokens, bypassing authentication to forge administrative sessions and create rogue Single Sign-On (SSO) accounts in the vSphere directory. Whereas CVE-2026-123890 is a flaw in the vmdir DCE/RPC interface that enables unauthenticated Secure Remote Password session key disclosure by exploiting predictable UUIDv1 timestamp generation in the SRP authentication interface, enabling the compromise of the SRP session key without knowing any passwords.

Mandiant has evidence that ARMORY CASTLE had knowledge of CVE-2026-123890 prior to the disclosure of CVE-2026-59309 on July 29, 2026. Logs indicate that BONESAW was executed in early September 2026 against a vCenter server already patched against CVE-2026-59309, and the recovered Go binary contains a build timestamp of 2026-07-13T09:21:24Z (16 days before CVE-2026-59309 was disclosed), although Go build metadata can be modified prior to compilation.

ARMORY CASTLE named BONESAW vcenter-db-teamcheck-go and compiled it in a Windows development environment based on Golang build artifacts: E:/CodeFiles/VulnScan/Vcenter/go_teamcheck/. BONESAW supports multiple command-line arguments, with primary functionality centered on the csa subcommand to remotely create an SSO admin account.

Figure 8: BONESAW Command Line Arguments

When executed utilizing the CSA command line flag, BONESAW creates the following files on disk:

Figure 9: BONESAW Filesystem Artifacts

SAWSNAKE parses command-line arguments to determine its operating mode and supports the following command line arguments:

Figure 10: SAWSNAKE Command Line Arguments

SAWSNAKE creates the following files on disk:

Figure 11: Files created by SAWSNAKE

Mandiant identified a second sample of SAWSNAKE that is likely an updated version. This sample added verbose debug logging to standard output across TLS, HTTP, SAML initiation, XML signing, and login routines, along with expanded redirect handling for up to 10 HTTP 302 redirects to support multi-hop authentication. Consistent with earlier activity, ARMORY CASTLE deleted SAWSNAKE and its output files from disk using rm.

Shortly after the deployment of SAWSNAKE, ARMORY CASTLE leveraged an additional backdoor /tmp/dbr tracked as BEARBONES. BEARBONES is written in C and is based on the open-source Dropbear SSH Server. It is capable of bypassing system password authentication when using password-based authentication, logging plaintext credentials, and loading or generating server host keys from /tmp instead of the default directory /etc/dropbear. The authentication handler svr_auth_password for the packet type SSH_MSG_USERAUTH_REQUEST was backdoored to bypass /etc/shadow validation and grant access if the SHA-256 digest of the first 14 bytes of client password matches a hardcoded SHA-256 hash, while also logging plaintext credentials. Additionally, the internal version string and the session identification string were modified to “23345” and “SSH-2.0-pulsesecure_23345”. Pivoting on this SSH banner identified a suspected obfuscation network of compromised third-party firewalls and routers exposing SSH-2.0-pulsesecure_23345 on non-standard high TCP ports.

ARMORY CASTLE executed BEARBONES several times from the Linux appliance, first attempting to open a listener on TCP port 53 and later on TCP port 33992.

Figure 12: BEARBONES Command Line Execution

After the execution of BEARBONES to create a listener on TCP port 33992, ARMORY CASTLE moved laterally to the vCenter appliance as the Administrator and created a local administrator account named sps-<REDACTED>@vsphere.local. Notably, ARMORY CASTLE enabled SSH on vCenter via the vCenter Server Appliance Management Interface (VAMI) API through a PUT request to /rest/appliance/access/ssh. Mandiant also observed this TTP during BRICKSTORM intrusions conducted by suspected PRC-nexus actor UNC6201. After enabling SSH, ARMORY CASTLE connected to the vCenter via SSH using the newly created local administrator account.

ARMORY CASTLE then conducted credential theft, targeting the vCenter internal master crypto key file as well as the PostgreSQL password file using SSH. ARMORY CASTLE disabled the SSH service via the VAMI API immediately after extracting the vCenter master symmetric key and PostgreSQL database credentials. ARMORY CASTLE then was observed authenticating to ESXi Host Client (“ESXUI”) using the vpxuser account and also connected to it via SSH using the root account.

ARMORY CASTLE used the WinSCP client to enumerate the ESXi file system, attached disks, mounted Virtual Machine File System (VMFS) volumes, and added an SSH public key to the root account’s authorized keys. ARMORY CASTLE conducted further reconnaissance and data theft on the ESXi filesystem using the root account. The actor was primarily observed enumerating directories and VMFS volumes. To stage and exfiltrate data from the ESXi filesystem, ARMORY CASTLE created a hidden directory, .a, on a VMFS volume to which they established an SCP connection to the ESXi using scp -r -d -t "/vmfs/volumes/<volume>/.a". ARMORY CASTLE then staged and extracted a suspected VMware specific toolset onto the ESXi host using tar xzf vmfs-pydist.tar.gz.

Figure 13: ARMORY CASTLE reconnaissance of guest VMs

ARMORY CASTLE then archived the extracted files into o.tar.gz, copied the archive from the ESXi filesystem using scp -r -p -d -f "o.tar.gz", and deleted the hidden .a directory. On the Linux appliance, ARMORY CASTLE staged the corresponding OpenSSH RSA private key at /tmp/123.key (matching the public key added to ESXi root authorized_keys) to authenticate outbound from the Linux appliance to ESXi over SSH without a password, alongside the BEARBONES SSH listener on the Linux appliance. From the Linux appliance, staged archives were downloaded via HTTP GET requests to the Remote Support appliance, from which ARMORY CASTLE likely exfiltrated the data.

Figure 14: ARMORY CASTLE SSH Tunnel

ARMORY CASTLE deployed a Slack-based backdoor tracked as LAZYBONES (/usr/bin/vmtoolscs) to an additional Linux appliance alongside the /tmp/123.key SSH private key. ARMORY CASTLE established persistence using a systemd service file set to restart every 50 minutes. LAZYBONES is a lightweight backdoor, written in Go, that establishes command-and-control (C2) communication over HTTPS using Slack. This malware periodically checks the most recent message in a designated Slack channel for commands. LAZYBONES supports core backdoor functionalities, including command execution and file transfer. Specifically, this variant encrypts Slack C2 messages with AES-256-CTR prior to Base64 encoding, polls with a 150 to 450 second jitter interval, and supports four commands (cmd, upload, download, and sleep) for command execution, file transfer, and beacon interval adjustment. Mandiant observed ARMORY CASTLE deploying LAZYBONES in a separate campaign in early 2025 following the successful exploitation of CVE-2025-22457.

GTIG attributes this activity to the suspected PRC-nexus espionage actor ARMORY CASTLE (formerly tracked as UNC5221). As noted in our prior blog, the tooling and TTPs leveraged within this campaign align with previously observed activity from ARMORY CASTLE, and in the case of LAZYBONES, directly overlap with previously observed activity.

Outlook and Implications

While GTIG and Mandiant have tracked ARMORY CASTLE exploiting zero-days and n-days in edge and security appliances for initial access since 2023, BONESAW represents a significant expansion into post-compromise use of zero-days. The group's use of VMware-specific tools like SAWSNAKE and vmfs_browser.py, alongside malware designed to operate on Remote Support and Linux systems, represent a diverse toolset including capabilities specifically tailored to target several different appliances, demonstrating the threat actor's deep understanding of each. The use of three separate specialized sets of malware and two zero-days in the course of a single campaign indicates that there are significant resources supporting ARMORY CASTLE operations. It also shows the extent of the threat actor’s operational commitment to compromise high priority targets. Lastly, it is important to highlight that the build time of BONESAW predates its deployment in this intrusion by over a month, indicating it was likely developed for broader operations.

Remediation and Hardening

The exploitation of CVE-2026-123890 by ARMORY CASTLE underscores a reality: virtualization infrastructure such as VMware vCenter remains a prime target for advanced persistent threats (APTs). Because vCenter serves as the centralized control plane for the virtualized data center, relying solely on reactive patching is insufficient to protect it. Organizations must adopt a proactive, Zero Trust approach to infrastructure hardening to defend against current and undiscovered zero-day vulnerabilities.

For a comprehensive framework on securing virtualization platforms against advanced persistent threats, organizations are highly advised to consult the Mandiant guide  vSphere and BRICKSTORM Malware: A Defender's Guide. This guide details an infrastructure-centric defense strategy, encompassing STIG benchmarking, identity management via Privileged Access Workstations (PAWs), and transforming the vCenter appliance into a proactive security sensor.

To secure vulnerable systems, it is highly recommended that affected organizations promptly deploy the official Broadcom patch.

The Risks of Default Configurations: The VCSA Host-Based Firewall

A common misconception in defending the vCenter control plane is that the native VCSA host-based firewall managed via the Virtual Appliance Management Interface (VAMI) provides adequate protection against lateral movement. In practice, relying on this default GUI-based firewall introduces severe operational and security limitations that threat actors like ARMORY CASTLE exploit:

Lack of Port-Specific Granularity: The native VAMI GUI lacks the precision required for a Zero Trust model. To grant an internal server (such as a backup server) legitimate access to the vSphere API on TCP 443, an administrator is often forced to trust that IP for all ports. If that internal server is compromised, the attacker inherits an unobstructed path to highly sensitive management interfaces like SSH (22) and the VAMI (5480).

Lack of Port-Specific Granularity: The native VAMI GUI lacks the precision required for a Zero Trust model. To grant an internal server (such as a backup server) legitimate access to the vSphere API on TCP 443, an administrator is often forced to trust that IP for all ports. If that internal server is compromised, the attacker inherits an unobstructed path to highly sensitive management interfaces like SSH (22) and the VAMI (5480).

Circular Administrative Dependency: The VCSA firewall is managed via the VAMI, which resides on TCP port 5480. If an attacker gains VAMI access as ARMORY CASTLE demonstrated to enable SSH,  they can use their compromised role to deactivate the firewall entirely.

Circular Administrative Dependency: The VCSA firewall is managed via the VAMI, which resides on TCP port 5480. If an attacker gains VAMI access as ARMORY CASTLE demonstrated to enable SSH,  they can use their compromised role to deactivate the firewall entirely.

Forensic Visibility Gaps: The VAMI firewall is designed for connectivity management, not security monitoring, and does not generate remote logs for denied connection attempts or shell activity. This blinds the SOC to active lateral movement and port scanning.

Forensic Visibility Gaps: The VAMI firewall is designed for connectivity management, not security monitoring, and does not generate remote logs for denied connection attempts or shell activity. This blinds the SOC to active lateral movement and port scanning.

Inbound-Only Policy: The default GUI focuses exclusively on inbound traffic, leaving outbound (egress) policy unmanaged. This allows malware on a compromised VCSA to communicate freely with external command-and-control (C2) infrastructure.

Inbound-Only Policy: The default GUI focuses exclusively on inbound traffic, leaving outbound (egress) policy unmanaged. This allows malware on a compromised VCSA to communicate freely with external command-and-control (C2) infrastructure.

Proactive Hardening with the VCSA Hardening Tool

To secure the vCenter control plane against advanced campaigns and future zero-days, Mandiant recommends utilizing the VCSA Hardening & Logging Tool.

This script bypasses the vulnerable VAMI GUI and enforces security configurations directly at the Photon Linux kernel level via iptables. By transitioning the appliance to a strict "Default Deny" posture, enforcing granular egress filtering, and bridging kernel-level events to a remote SIEM, the tool transforms the VCSA from a passive appliance into an active security sensor.

The table below illustrates how deploying this proactive hardening script would have disrupted ARMORY CASTLE's attack chain at multiple stages, demonstrating its efficacy against zero-day exploits.

VCSA Hardening Feature

Impact on the ARMORY CASTLE Attack Chain

Protection Against Future Zero-Days

VCSA iptables Inbound DROP Policy & Connection Logging (Management Ports)

The nmap scans targeting TCP ports 443, 5480, and 22 are silently dropped because the attacker's source IP is not in the trusted whitelists. These blocked connection attempts are instantly forwarded to the SIEM via the iptables-log-bridge.sh service, exposing the active network reconnaissance.

Conceals critical management interfaces from internal network mapping tools and provides high-fidelity, early-warning alerts of active lateral movement before exploitation can occur.

VCSA iptables Inbound DROP Policy (TCP 443)

Provides defense-in-depth: Even if a zero-day allows credential theft or authentication bypass on a backend service, the stolen credentials/tokens cannot be used to access the management plane from an untrusted network.

VCSA iptables Inbound DROP Policy (TCP 2010)

An iptables DROP policy on TCP 2012 for non-allowlisted IPs prevents the exploit payload originating from a compromised Linux appliance from reaching the vmdird service.

Drops unauthorized inbound connections to management interfaces and RPC services, mitigating network-level exploitation of vulnerable services.

VCSA iptables Inbound DROP Policy (TCP 22 & 5480)

Blocks the OS-level pivot. The firewall drops the PUT request to the VAMI API (TCP 5480 or 443) used to enable the SSH daemon. Furthermore, even if SSH was already enabled, the VCSA_SSH_RULES chain drops all SSH connections

Protects the underlying OS and databases from direct credential theft or tampering. Even if an attacker gains administrative privileges within the web application, they are blocked from dropping down to a root shell.

VCSA iptables Egress DROP Policy

An egress DROP policy prevents SCP data exfiltration and blocks the LAZYBONES backdoor from establishing HTTPS C2 communications with external Slack endpoints.

Blocks unauthorized outbound internet and internal network connections, preventing the VCSA from acting as a pivot point or establishing external C2.

VCSA OS-Level Command and Syslog Auditing

Remote syslog forwarding captures the execution of tar and Python vmfs_browser.py over SSH, exposing the extraction of NTDS.dit and registry hives in real-time.

Forwards bash command execution and dropped network packets to a SIEM, bypassing local anti-forensics (e.g., .bash_history deletion) and alerting on unauthorized shell activity.

VCSA Kernel-Level Rule Enforcement

iptables rules operate independently of the VAMI application, preventing the actor from using the VAMI API (/rest/appliance/access/ssh) to circumvent network security controls.

Decouples network security from the vCenter application layer, ensuring firewall policies cannot be modified via compromised VAMI or vSphere administrative credentials.

[64] CrowdStrike - Java4Shell (CVE-2026-114938): CrowdStrike Detects Exposure and Post-Exploitation Activity
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 19, 2026 00:00:00-0600
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-helps-protect-against-java4shell-cve-2026-114938/

[TAM METİN - 1262 kelime]
CVE-2026-114938, dubbed "Java4Shell," is a memory corruption vulnerability in the HotSpot Java Virtual Machine (JVM), the runtime shared by Oracle Java SE, OpenJDK, and many downstream Java distributions.

Although HotSpot is widely deployed, exposure to this vulnerability is narrower than its footprint suggests. Exploitation requires three conditions: an affected Java version, x86-64 hardware that supports AVX-512 VBMI, and an application that decodes attacker-supplied Base64 data. Even when all three are met, the limited size of the memory overwrite makes reliable remote code execution difficult, though not impossible.

Oracle and OpenJDK released fixes on November 17, 2026. Organizations should patch systems that meet all three conditions immediately, starting with internet-facing Java services. Remaining Java deployments can be addressed through regular scheduled patching.

Oracle rates the vulnerability 8.1 (High) under CVSS 3.1. Red Hat rates it 9.8 (Critical) under CVSS 3.0. Oracle and OpenJDK released fixes on November 17, 2026.

The flaw resides in HotSpot's AVX-512-optimized Base64 decoding routine, a performance shortcut the JVM uses only on processors that support AVX-512 Vector Byte Manipulation Instructions (VBMI). Base64 is everywhere in modern applications. It carries credentials in HTTP Basic authentication headers, encodes JSON Web Tokens (JWTs) and SAML assertions, and wraps file uploads and binary API payloads. As a result, the vulnerable code path is frequently reachable from untrusted input before any application-level validation takes place.

What elevates this vulnerability is its placement in the stack. The defect sits in the runtime itself, rather than an application, framework, or library. An application that decodes attacker-supplied Base64 using the standard Java class library is exposed even if its own code is flawless. Because the vulnerable code lives in shared HotSpot code, exposure extends beyond Oracle-branded Java. It includes OpenJDK builds from multiple vendors, applications that bundle their own Java runtime, container images, and managed cloud services.

Java4Shell also has meaningful constraints. The vulnerable code path is only active on specific x86-64 hardware, and the size of the out-of-bounds write is limited.

An unauthenticated remote attacker who can pass a specially crafted Base64 string to an affected application can trigger an out-of-bounds heap write of up to 62 bytes. Because the overflow lands on the Java heap, its consequences depend on what sits adjacent to the destination buffer when the write occurs. These may include:

Denial of service: Corrupting object headers or JVM metadata causes the JVM to crash. This would be the most likely outcome.

Memory disclosure ("JavaBleed"): Under certain conditions, corruption of adjacent object state can cause the application to return heap contents it should not. This could include fragments of other requests, session tokens, or credentials.

Remote code execution: Achieving code execution requires the attacker to influence heap layout precisely enough that the limited overwrite corrupts a security-relevant structure, such as an object header, array length, or reference, in a way that can be leveraged further. The 62-byte ceiling, hardware dependency, and variability of heap layout across JVM versions, garbage collectors, and workloads make this the most difficult outcome to reliably achieve.

Several conditions must be met at once for successful exploitation to occur.

First, the Java runtime must match with the affected software.

Second, the runtime must be running on an x86-64 processor that supports AVX-512 VBMI, such as Intel Ice Lake or newer or AMD Zen 4 or newer. On supported hardware, HotSpot enables the vulnerable AVX-512 Base64 decoding routine by default, so no special configuration is needed for a JVM to be exposed.

Finally, an attacker must be able to deliver a specially crafted Base64 string to an application that decodes it using the standard Java class library. Common entry points include authentication headers, tokens, and file uploads, which are often processed before authentication completes. Denial of service is the most reliable outcome. Remote code execution additionally requires the attacker to shape heap memory precisely enough for the limited 62-byte overwrite to corrupt a useful structure, which significantly constrains large-scale exploitation.

Exposure depends on both hardware and software.

Only x86-64 processors that support AVX-512 VBMI are affected. This includes Intel Ice Lake and newer, and AMD Zen 4 and newer. ARM64 and i386 systems are not affected.

Standard Java 8 and Java 11 environments are not affected. The Oracle Java 8 Performance Pack is the exception.

Downstream distributions are also affected. These include, but are not limited to:

Microsoft Build of OpenJDK

What Should Organizations Do Now?

Upgrade to patched versions. Apply the fixes from Oracle's and OpenJDK's Critical Security Patch Update for November 2026, or the equivalent update from your Java vendor.

Restart running JVMs. Upgrading the runtime on disk does not protect processes that are already running. Restart all Java processes after patching.

Rebuild container images. Rebuild images on patched base images and redeploy them.

Inventory embedded runtimes. Many appliances and third-party applications ship their own Java runtime. Check vendor advisories and review the CISA alert on CVE-2026-114938.

How the CrowdStrike Falcon Platform Helps

CrowdStrike employs a layered approach to detecting adversary activity, using machine learning and behavior-based detections, including indicators of attack (IOAs). The CrowdStrike Falcon platform provides out-of-the-box visibility and detection coverage for post-exploitation activity that may follow exploitation of CVE-2026-114938. Existing IOAs detect and prevent common post-exploitation activity, including web shell deployment, suspicious child processes spawned by Java processes, hands-on-keyboard activity, and living-off-the-land techniques.

The Falcon platform also identifies the attack surface and exposure to the vulnerability using CrowdStrike Falcon® Exposure Management and CrowdStrike Falcon® Cloud Security’s Image Assessment functionality.

Falcon Exposure Management Finds Vulnerable Java Runtimes

Falcon Exposure Management provides comprehensive visibility across affected environments. Sensor-based assessment identifies Java runtimes affected by CVE-2026-114938 on supported Windows, Linux, and macOS hosts. This covers Oracle Java SE, OpenJDK, and downstream distributions such as Amazon Corretto, Eclipse Temurin, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, Azul Zulu, Red Hat OpenJDK, Oracle GraalVM, BellSoft Liberica, and SAP SapMachine.

Falcon Exposure Management flags hosts running affected versions: Java 17 (17.0.6 through 17.0.21), Java 21 (up to 21.0.13), Java 25 (up to 25.0.5), Java 27 (up to 27.0.1), and the Oracle Java 8 Performance Pack (8u511b33-perf). Hosts updated to 17.0.21.1, 21.0.13.1, 25.0.5.1, or 27.0.1.1 are no longer flagged.

Falcon Exposure Management detects standard Java installations on all supported sensor versions. These checks have no minimum sensor version requirement:

Windows: standard Windows installs

Linux: rpm and dpkg package installs

CrowdStrike has also added the following sensor-based detections. They find Java installations that the standard checks might miss, such as custom installs or runtimes bundled with other applications. They are available only on the sensor versions listed:

Windows (Falcon sensor 7.40 or later): A system-wide check for Java installations. This requires “Enable Full System Visibility” to be turned on in the Exposure Management policy (checks on C: drive only; if C: is not the default drive the in-depth check does not work).

Linux (Falcon sensor 7.31 or later): A check of running processes to identify Java installations performed outside the package manager.

Customers on earlier sensor versions are still covered by the standard install checks. They will not get the additional system-wide and running-process checks until they upgrade.

CrowdStrike has updated Falcon Exposure Management detections on all supported platforms to flag only x86-64 systems, and is adding a check on Linux for whether AVX-512 VBMI support is enabled. This hardware check (AVX-512 VBMI support) is available on Linux only. On Windows and macOS, Falcon Exposure Management may flag hosts that are not exploitable, so customers should prioritize patching on systems with supported processors.

Falcon Exposure Management and Falcon Spotlight customers can find exposed hosts in the Falcon console under Exposure Management > Vulnerability Management > Vulnerabilities.

[65] CrowdStrike - NGINX2Root: Identify and Protect with the CrowdStrike Falcon Platform
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 19, 2026 00:00:00-0600
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-helps-protect-against-nginx2root-cve-2026-123007/

[TAM METİN - 780 kelime]
A set of five critical vulnerabilities in nginx, one of the most widely deployed open-source web servers, was disclosed on November 18, 2026.

Collectively branded NGINX2Root by researchers, these flaws allow an unauthenticated remote attacker to execute code on a target host and, in a demonstrated exploit chain, escalate to root and escape a Docker container to reach the underlying host. The two most severe CVEs (CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633) each carry a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. F5 and the nginx project released patches the same day.

This post explains who is affected, what organizations should do now, and how the CrowdStrike Falcon® platform helps identify exposed assets and detect post-exploitation activity.

NGINX2Root is the name researchers gave to five nginx vulnerabilities that each allow remote code execution, plus a separate Linux kernel flaw that can be chained with them to gain root. The headline flaw is CVE-2026-123007, an integer underflow in nginx’s HTTP/2 request-handling code. HTTP/2 is the modern web protocol enabled by default in most production nginx deployments. When an attacker sends specially crafted HTTP/2 traffic, the flaw corrupts memory inside the nginx worker process and allows arbitrary command execution with no authentication or user interaction required.

What elevates the severity of NGINX2Root is the chain it enables. The nginx worker process runs as an unprivileged system account, typically www-data or nginx. While that account alone does not have root access, a second flaw in the Linux kernel’s SCTP networking subsystem can be reached from the compromised worker and exploited to escalate privileges to root. Researchers demonstrated the full chain end-to-end, including escaping a Docker container running nginx to reach the underlying host.

The attack chain in brief:

Attacker sends crafted HTTP/2 frames to a public-facing nginx instance with no credentials required

Memory corruption in the nginx worker yields code execution as the webserver account

A Linux kernel SCTP flaw elevates privileges from the worker account to root

The attacker escapes the Docker container to reach the host system

Additional CVEs round out the NGINX2Root set, each requiring different but common configurations: specific reverse proxy and resolver settings (CVE-2026-123634), HTTP/2 with upstream forwarding (CVE-2026-123633), HTTP/3 with upstream forwarding (CVE-2026-121810), and mail proxy mode (CVE-2026-113425).

A public proof-of-concept is available for CVE-2026-123007. Triggering the memory corruption requires transmitting approximately 128GB of HTTP/2 data (roughly two minutes over a 10 Gbit/s connection). This limits opportunistic mass internet exploitation today, but it does not prevent targeted attacks, exploitation from within a network, or future techniques that reduce the data requirement. Organizations should not treat this constraint as a reason to delay patching.

Nginx is estimated to serve more than a third of the world's websites.1 The five CVEs affect different version ranges and configuration conditions. HTTP/2 must be enabled for CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633, which are the most severe. The table below summarizes affected versions and conditions per the official nginx advisory.

NGINX Plus customers: fixed versions are 37.3.0.1 (mainline) and 37.0.8.1 (stable). No fix is available on the R branch.

Note that nginx on Windows is not affected by this advisory. nginx itself labels its Windows port as beta software not intended for high-performance use.

What Should Organizations Do Now?

Update nginx to a patched release: Apply nginx Open Source 1.31.9 or later (mainline branch) or 1.30.7 or later (stable branch). nginx Plus customers should update to 37.3.0.1 or 37.0.8.1 per the F5 advisory. Downstream Linux distribution packages (Red Hat, Debian, Ubuntu, Amazon Linux, and others) are being updated; check your distribution's security advisories for per-package fixed versions.

Restart nginx after patching: A configuration reload (nginx -s reload) replaces the configuration but does not replace the running binary. After installing the patched package, perform a full service restart to ensure the patched executable is running.

As a temporary mitigation: Disabling HTTP/2 on affected listening ports blocks CVE-2026-123007 and CVE-2026-123633. This mitigation does not cover CVE-2026-123634, CVE-2026-121810, or CVE-2026-113425. It is not a substitute for patching.

Inventory container images and third-party appliances: Any image built on a base layer that includes nginx, and any appliance whose vendor has not yet released a patch, remains exposed. Rebuild images on patched base layers and check vendor advisories.

Prioritize internet-exposed instances: Hosts running nginx with HTTP/2 enabled that are reachable from the internet carry the highest immediate risk. Prioritize these for patching.

How the CrowdStrike Falcon Platform Helps

Falcon Exposure Management Uncovers Exposed Assets

CrowdStrike Falcon® Exposure Management coverage for NGINX2Root is available across Linux, macOS, NVA/Network Scan, and external attack surface management (EASM).

Customers can find hosts exposed to NGINX2Root CVEs in the Falcon console under Exposure Management > Vulnerability Management > Vulnerabilities, then filter by CVE ID (CVE-2026-123007, CVE-2026-123634, CVE-2026-123633, CVE-2026-121810, or CVE-2026-113425).

[72] NIST - NIST Project Aims to Accelerate Mobile Driver’s License Adoption
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.nist.gov/news-events/news/2026/11/nist-project-aims-accelerate-mobile-drivers-license-adoption

[TAM METİN - 825 kelime]
NIST Project Aims to Accelerate Mobile Driver’s License Adoption

A NIST project aims to help accelerate the adoption of mobile driver’s licenses (mDLs) across financial institutions, government services and other sectors.

Mobile driver’s licenses could help U.S. citizens do business more securely, but there are few ways to use them — an issue the project is designed to help address.

NIST is requesting public comments on the project’s draft report by Dec. 20, 2026.

A new form of digital identification called a mobile driver’s license (mDL) is making its way into American pockets — the ones that hold cellphones. An mDL offers a more secure and user-friendly experience than a physical identification card. A new draft report from the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) aims to make mDLs more widely usable for license holders and organizations alike.

Twenty-two U.S. states and territories currently have active mDL programs. Across these programs, at least 5 million mDLs are in use around the U.S., with approximately 47% of the population having access to them.

An mDL can be used either in person or online. It can potentially make many digital transactions easier: An individual could use one to open a bank account online or access government benefits. Its information is cryptographically protected, making it difficult to steal or fake. An mDL eliminates the need to upload photos of a physical ID card while also providing strong security because of the cryptographic information it contains.

Despite these benefits, very few online service providers currently accept mDLs.

“It’s a chicken-and-egg problem,” said NIST security engineer Bill Fisher. “On the one hand, consumers are unlikely to adopt mDLs unless they are accepted in high-value, everyday use cases. Meanwhile, companies are hesitant to invest in mDL integration without widespread consumer demand or adoption.”

This issue helped motivate a project on mDLs from NIST’s National Cybersecurity Center of Excellence (NCCoE). The project’s draft report, entitled Digital Identities — Mobile Driver’s License (mDL): Enabling Secure and Interoperable Online Use (NIST Special Publication 1800-42), offers tools and resources to help organizations assess the feasibility of integrating mDLs with their current systems.

“Our goal is to help overcome the barriers preventing organizations from accepting mDLs online,” said Fisher, one of the project’s organizers. “By working with industry to test real-world applications, we can identify what’s working, what barriers remain, and provide a path to update their infrastructure so they can accommodate mDLs at a large scale.”

The NCCoE project brought together stakeholders from across the mDL ecosystem, including technology companies, government agencies and five of the 10 largest U.S. banks, to identify emerging approaches to mDL adoption. The project created a reference architecture that demonstrates three capabilities: how users can present their mDLs when verifying their identity for an online transaction; how in certain high-risk scenarios — such as high-dollar transactions or account recovery — an mDL could be requested and presented to reestablish trust in the user’s identity; and how to issue a phishing-resistant authenticator (i.e., a passkey) to a user.

“We built the system using commercially available, off-the-shelf technology, software and standards,” Fisher said. “We wanted to demonstrate how organizations can implement mDLs now, with minimum difficulty.”

The draft report details the reference architecture and its components, then outlines how organizations and their customers can benefit from the improved security capabilities and privacy protections that mDLs offer. The report also outlines potential threats to an mDL ecosystem and risk mitigations, offering references to other NIST publications that can help maintain security, including the NIST Privacy Framework and the security and privacy control catalog (NIST Special Publication 800-53 rev. 5).

Additionally, the report offers recommendations on improving the user experience of mDL holders, including a set of seven best practices that reflect patterns observed during usability testing performed with a set of volunteers covering the system’s performance, satisfaction and feedback.

Fisher said that while financial institutions provided the initial demonstration use case, the report contains information useful to any organization that accepts physical IDs now and is seeking to accept the presentation of mDLs. Other situations of broader applicability it addresses include accessing healthcare information and interacting with state and federal government services.

“This is the direction technology is moving,” Fisher said. “But what limits current mDL adoption isn’t the technology itself, but the maturity of the surrounding ecosystem. Constructing this ecosystem requires deep coordination among government ID issuers, technology providers and organizations across economic sectors. Our goal with this project is to help the country take steps in that direction.”

NIST is seeking comments on the report’s draft until Dec. 20, 2026. Comments may be submitted either via email at mdl-nccoe [at] nist.gov (mdl-nccoe[at]nist[dot]gov) or via the resource hub page.

[73] The Cyber Express - NGINX HTTP/2 Flaws CVE-2026-123633 and CVE-2026-123007 Enable Unauthenticated Remote Code Execution
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 11:42:28 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/cve-2026-123633-cve-2026-123007/

[TAM METİN - 708 kelime]
NGINX HTTP/2 Flaws CVE-2026-123633 and CVE-2026-123007 Enable Unauthenticated Remote Code Execution

Two critical memory-safety flaws in NGINX, tracked as CVE-2026-123633 and CVE-2026-123007, can let unauthenticated attackers run code on servers that have HTTP/2 enabled. Both carry a CVSS 4.0 score of 9.2. F5, which maintains NGINX, released fixes on November 18, 2026.

How CVE-2026-123633 and CVE-2026-123007 Were Found

Wiz Research, Ikotas Labs, Metnir, and Xint each discovered the bugs independently. Wiz says it found both with its Wiz Atlas harness and confirmed their impact using Gemini 4 Carbon and ChatGPT 5.6 Sol. Wiz reported the use-after-free on October 21.

Its report included a proposed fix and an exploit that bypassed ASLR. F5 replied that another team had already reported the same flaw. F5 shared a patch for review on November 10. Wiz reported the second flaw on November 16, and F5 confirmed the next day that it would be fixed in the November 18 release.

Wiz put the findings alongside its PHP2Shell disclosures from last month and Java4Shell, which Calif published a day earlier. Wiz argues that the complexity that once hid memory bugs like these no longer protects them, because AI can now find them. In its view, that also shortens the time between a bug’s disclosure and its exploitation.

Why NGINX Exposure Matters

NGINX powers more than 225 million websites. It often sits in front of several applications as a reverse proxy or load balancer. Because the flaws are in NGINX itself, secure application code offers no protection. A single compromised server could expose multiple sites and APIs to data theft, tampering, or outages.

CVE-2026-123633 is nearly six years old and affects versions 1.19.7 through 1.31.8. It occurs when an HTTP/2 connection stays on NGINX’s list of reusable connections after a request has started on it. If connection slots run low while NGINX is proxying to an upstream server, ngx_drain_connections() may close that connection. If building the GOAWAY frame then fails, NGINX frees memory the request is still using. Proxy configurations face the greatest risk.

CVE-2026-123007 dates back to version 1.9.5 in 2015 and affects releases through 1.31.8. When a stream is canceled, NGINX refunds the unsent data allowance to h2c->send_window without checking the protocol’s maximum. This causes an unsigned underflow in the bounds check for WINDOW_UPDATE frames.

The inflated value later turns into a negative length, which leads to an out-of-bounds memory copy in the TLS send path. Researchers achieved code execution with an ASLR bypass on official 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 builds. The test servers were 64-bit systems serving ordinary static files over HTTPS, with no backend application or third-party modules.

Wiz is withholding its proof-of-concept exploits. It notes that attacks require leaking memory addresses and shaping the heap. Packaged products, where many customers run identical builds, are easier targets. CDNs or WAFs that terminate HTTP/2 may block parts of an attack, depending on how they are implemented.

Patching and Mitigating NGINX

For NGINX OSS, the fixes are in 1.30.7 stable and 1.31.9 mainline. NGINX Plus is affected from R33 and 37.0.0. Versions 37.0.7.1 and 37.2.0.1 fix CVE-2026-123633, and versions 37.0.8.1 and 37.3.0.1 fix CVE-2026-123007.

Teams that cannot upgrade right away should temporarily disable HTTP/2:

Set http2 off; in affected server blocks on version 1.25.1 or later.

Remove the http2 parameter from listen directives.

Test application compatibility, reload NGINX, and confirm the old workers have exited.

Re-enable HTTP/2 after patching.

Several signs warrant investigation: unexpected worker crashes, commands launched by NGINX, floods of WINDOW_UPDATE frames, and long-lived HTTP/2 connections carrying large amounts of data.

The release also fixes eight other NGINX flaws:

Three use-after-frees rated 9.2: CVE-2026-121810 (HTTP/3 proxy), CVE-2026-123634 (DNS resolver), and CVE-2026-113425 (mail proxy with auth_http).

One rated 8.7: CVE-2026-118899, a denial-of-service on servers that support TLS 1.3.

Three rated 6.9: CVE-2026-120993 (proxy_protocol v2), CVE-2026-125283 (alias with try_files), and CVE-2026-123640 (ssl_preread).

One rated 6.3: CVE-2026-120967, a client certificate bypass.

[74] Industrial Cyber - Claroty finds 65,000 consumer devices connected to critical infrastructure networks, exposing OT segmentation gaps
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 12:00:35 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/reports/claroty-finds-65000-consumer-devices-connected-to-critical-infrastructure-networks-exposing-ot-segmentation-gaps/

[TAM METİN - 1690 kelime]
Claroty finds 65,000 consumer devices connected to critical infrastructure networks, exposing OT segmentation gaps

Claroty found more than 65,000 non-essential consumer devices connected to networks supporting critical infrastructure, including OT (operational technology), healthcare and commercial environments, raising concerns about network segmentation and cybersecurity governance. The findings are based on an analysis of more than 45 million devices across 30,000 critical infrastructure sites. The inventory included 16,745 gaming consoles, most located in hospitals, 44,059 smart home and entertainment devices, and 4,915 connected vehicles operating on OT networks.

In its research titled ‘Game Over for Consumer Tech Risk in Critical Infrastructure,’ Claroty identified security weaknesses associated with unmanaged consumer technology, including unpatched firmware, outdated operating systems, known remote code execution vulnerabilities and connections to external, vendor-controlled cloud services. Claroty found that 95% of gaming consoles in healthcare environments were connected to production networks rather than isolated guest or IoT networks.

Almost none of the connected vehicles analyzed were properly isolated, while researchers identified six critical CVEs affecting Tesla Model 3 vehicles and 121 security issues associated with Ford F-150 trucks. The findings point to deficiencies in network segmentation, asset visibility and access controls that could provide threat actors with pathways into mission-critical environments, particularly as AI lowers barriers to reconnaissance and vulnerability scanning.

Additional findings showed that 95% of gaming consoles in healthcare environments were connected to production networks rather than isolated guest or IoT networks or VLANs. By contrast, personal electronics such as smartwatches and smart speakers demonstrated better network hygiene, with 71% appropriately connected to guest networks. Almost none of the connected vehicles analyzed were properly isolated on guest networks. Researchers also identified six critical CVEs affecting Tesla Model 3 vehicles and 121 security issues associated with Ford F-150 trucks.

“Finding consumer technology like Apple Watches and Nintendo Switches residing on the same networks as OT and medical equipment points directly to ongoing deficiencies in network governance and segmentation within operational settings,” Sean Tufts, field CTO at Claroty, said in a media statement. “Addressing this issue does not mean stripping smart TVs from conference rooms or banning XBOX consoles from hospitals; it requires closing off unnecessary pathways for malicious actors from causing intolerable disruptions and negatively impacting businesses across industries.”

Despite millions of dollars invested in technology to protect critical infrastructure and build operational resilience, the explosion of connected devices has led to failures in virtual network segmentation, continuous threat detection, remote access, and overall governance. At a time when AI is lowering the barrier to entry even further for threat actors to not only carry out reconnaissance but also scan internet-facing assets for known vulnerabilities, the number of devices left to provide a foothold for attackers was staggering.

Organizations must adopt rigorous access controls and network segmentation, along with comprehensive visibility into connected assets, to safeguard the resilience of key operational systems. Enforcing continuous asset discovery and strict segmentation between guest, IoT, and core production environments eliminates unmanaged entry points and prevents threat actors from moving laterally to compromise mission-critical infrastructure.

Claroty’s research uncovered a consequential number of consumer technology devices such as smart home and entertainment systems, gaming consoles, and cars connected to sensitive operations networks. These devices may appear harmless, but when they share network infrastructure with ICS (industrial control systems), building management systems, and medical devices, they can become an entry point for attackers seeking to disrupt production, compromise critical processes, or threaten operational resilience.

Consumer devices are rarely designed to meet the security, reliability, and lifecycle requirements of operational environments. Software and firmware vulnerabilities are present on these devices, many of which are not secure by design or default. Many have insecure communications or must reach out to the public internet to a cloud service that is outside a factory’s or hospital’s control.

Most organizations don’t have visibility into these rogue third parties’ security posture and risks they may introduce to production and medical networks. So clearly, the fundamental problem is that these devices introduce unmanaged assets, unexpected communications, and potential vulnerabilities into an environment where a compromise can have physical consequences.

Of the more than 45 million devices analyzed, over 450,000, or approximately 3.5%, were classified as critical-risk devices because of known vulnerabilities, outdated firmware or a lack of security updates. The dataset covered PCs and tablets, automation equipment such as programmable logic controllers (PLCs), controllers and human-machine interfaces (HMIs), industrial printers, IP cameras and surveillance systems, as well as robotics and manufacturing systems.

Apple had the highest critical-risk devices, with 155,972, followed by Rockwell Automation with 132,868 and Axis Communications with 40,706. Other vendors included Schneider Electric with 28,568 devices, Zebra Technologies with 19,068, Siemens with 13,420, Hanwha Techwin with 12,775, Fanuc Robotics with 7,332, Honeywell with 4,335 and GE with 4,326.

The research also addressed that device risk is not only determined by a CVE or CVSS score; multiple factors make up scoring criteria. It considers presence of confirmed or potentially relevant common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs), particularly those with high Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) scores, known exploits listed in CISA’s Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog, or high Exploit Prediction Scoring System (EPSS) scores. It also examined whether devices communicated with the internet, particularly through inbound or server connections; risks associated with outdated, unsupported, or end-of-life operating systems; and weaknesses in security controls, including missing endpoint protection, unmanaged devices, or missing security updates. The assessment also considered each device’s operational importance and potential impact of its compromise.

Highlighting recent attacks on European supermarkets, for example, Claorty identified that attackers are accessing networks via unsecured internet-facing assets. “In the supermarket attacks, attackers were able to manipulate temperature controls and defrost food, causing millions in lost stock and revenue. Once a device is compromised, its position on the network becomes important. If it can communicate freely with other systems, an attacker may attempt to discover reachable assets, abuse improperly configured access permissions, steal credentials, or exploit vulnerabilities in adjacent computers and servers. A compromised device can also provide a foothold for further reconnaissance and lateral movement.”

The report observed that the greatest danger emerges when connectivity between IT and OT networks is insufficiently controlled. Determined attackers looking to manipulate or disrupt operations can exploit weak or non-existent segmentation, excessive permissions, shared credentials, or unmonitored connections to move deeper onto the network. An innocuous connection from a connected automobile on the same network as OT, IoT, and BMS assets can provide a pathway to access those sensitive devices. If the pathway is unknown, the risk is substantial.

Claorty noted that the problem for OT, commercial, and healthcare networks is that they may operate outside IT’s control, with individual sites maintaining autonomy over their operations. This decentralized approach has created an architecture characterized by perimeter-focused security, often relying on a single large boundary firewall with minimal internal controls; limited network segmentation, with few guest networks or isolated VLANs for non-critical devices; minimal access controls, allowing devices to connect to corporate OT networks with few barriers; and mixed-use infrastructure, where consumer devices share network space with human-machine interfaces (HMIs), programmable logic controllers (PLCs), magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) systems, and cooling equipment.

The report further detailed, “We not only found tens of thousands of consumer devices on OT networks, but also exploitable CVEs on the consoles, entertainment devices, and cars that could provide determined attackers with a foothold on the network.”

Claroty noted that segmentation has become the default answer for networks where downtime is simply not an option, whether that means a production line, a hospital ward or a commercial supply chain. Yet the gap it is meant to close keeps widening, and Claroty’s analysis points to three stubborn causes. The first is decentralized management. When individual sites can write, bend or ignore security rules on their own, a uniform posture dissolves into patchwork: patches land late, tool sets multiply, and the central security operations center loses sight of the very locations attackers find easiest to breach.

The second is legacy architecture. Networks built decades ago around a perimeter mindset no longer match how industrial environments actually behave. Digital transformation has pushed data sideways, across layers and out to cloud and partner systems, which cuts against the vertical logic of the Purdue Model and lets intruders move laterally with little resistance. Underneath it all sits a cultural divide, since OT prizes availability and safety while IT leans on confidentiality and tolerates planned outages, and that friction makes it hard to apply standard frameworks across converged environments.

The third cause, slow modernization, is where the practical pain lies. Many of these assets are out of vendor support yet remain woven into production or patient care, so ripping them out is costly and risky. Every change has to account for hidden dependencies, custom configurations and critical traffic that cannot be interrupted, because a clumsy fix can do as much damage as an attack.

Even routine remediation, from firmware updates to configuration fixes, demands scheduled downtime, regression testing and close monitoring once changes go live. Add the expense of new hardware, software and scarce specialist expertise, and the result is a long window of exposure to known exploited vulnerabilities, weak configurations and insecure remote access. In short, the problem is less a shortage of security tools than a collision between operational reality and the pace at which defenses can safely change.

Claroty argued that the security of hospitals, power plants, manufacturing facilities and water treatment systems depends directly on how rigorously organizations enforce network segmentation. Every Nintendo console, Tesla vehicle or Apple Watch connected to a production network expands the attack surface, creates a potential pivot point for lateral movement, introduces a blind spot in security monitoring and indicates a failure to enforce segmentation policies.

To strengthen critical infrastructure security, organizations should implement strict network segmentation by separating guest, IoT and production networks through enforced security policies. They should deploy continuous asset discovery to maintain real-time visibility into all connected devices and enforce secure access requirements to prevent unauthorized devices from accessing production networks. Claroty also recommended accelerating modernization efforts by replacing legacy systems and redesigning network architectures, while establishing governance frameworks that centralize security policies without undermining operational autonomy.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://industrialcyber.co/reports/claroty-finds-65000-consumer-devices-connected-to-critical-infrastructure-networks-exposing-ot-segmentation-gaps/, industrialcyber.co, 19.11.2026)

[75] Industrial Cyber - AttackIQ reports that Iranian hackers leverage contractors and shared access to expand attacks on critical infrastructure
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Thu, 19 Nov 2026 11:58:06 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/reports/attackiq-reports-that-iranian-hackers-leverage-contractors-and-shared-access-to-expand-attacks-on-critical-infrastructure/

[TAM METİN - 1821 kelime]
AttackIQ reports that Iranian hackers leverage contractors and shared access to expand attacks on critical infrastructure

New research from cybersecurity firm AttackIQ maps 20 Iranian state-sponsored threat groups and their ties to government institutions and contractors, showing how espionage groups hand off compromised network access to enable destructive attacks. The findings show Iranian espionage groups turn compromised networks into launchpads for other operators, transforming intelligence-gathering operations into destructive attacks. Threat reporting on Iran typically covers one campaign at a time, and vendors track the same groups under different names. Helix Kitten alone appears under eight other names, including OilRig, APT34, and Scarred Manticore.

In its threat research report, ‘Weighing Cyber Power: Evaluating the Strategic and Operational Significance of Iran’s State-Sponsored Cyber Capability,’ AttackIQ categorizes 20 Iranian adversaries into heavyweight, lightweight and featherweight tiers based on their organizational significance, operational relevance and demonstrated capabilities. Heavyweight groups conduct sustained espionage operations, develop proprietary tools and play central roles in Iran’s cyber strategy. Lightweight groups focus on specialized espionage, access facilitation and destructive operations, while featherweight groups have narrower or less consistently documented activities and may rely on support from more established actors. The framework highlights how specialized groups contribute to broader Iranian cyber campaigns alongside more established adversaries.

As global institutions grew increasingly dependent on interconnected digital systems, cyberspace emerged as an alternative operational domain that enabled Iran to conduct espionage, influence, and sabotage operations and compete with stronger opponents on more favorable terms without resorting to direct military conflict.

The report, which draws on more than 200 references and consolidates over 2,600 procedures, 1,300 file indicators and 2,300 network indicators, finds that Iranian operators frequently disguise destructive activity as ransomware or hacktivism and reach heavily defended targets, including in the defense industrial base through subsidiaries, contractors and suppliers.

“For this reason, cyber operations aligned naturally with Iran’s asymmetric approach to security. Rather than competing symmetrically across conventional domains, cyberspace offered a comparatively accessible instrument for generating disproportionate effects relative to the resources required,” AttackIQ reported. “Its affordability, scalability, and deniability made cyber capability particularly valuable to a regime seeking to compensate for conventional disadvantages.”

“The value of threat research is measured by the decisions it informs,” Paul Reid, vice president of adversary research at AttackIQ, said in a media statement. “Understanding which Iranian groups work together, and how confident we can be in each relationship, gives defenders a clear starting point for emulation and a realistic picture of what could follow an initial compromise. It turns a single detection into a forecast of what comes next.”

“Iran isn’t as technically sophisticated as China, Russia or North Korea, but what it lacks in sophistication it makes up for in cadence,” said Francis Guibernau, senior adversary research engineer at AttackIQ. “They target anything and everything, on every operating system and in industrial environments. That volume, combined with how freely these groups share access, is what makes the ecosystem dangerous.”

By the end of the 2000s, Iran had established the foundations necessary to sustain an increasingly advanced state-sponsored cyber capability. The combination of domestic and foreign pressures accelerated this development and consolidated importance of cyberspace as an internal and external domain of national security. Shortly after, the identification of one of the most consequential cyber operations in history would fundamentally alter the trajectory of this capability.

AttackIQ’s report identifies several Iranian state-sponsored cyber groups and institutions with direct or indirect links to critical infrastructure targeting. It describes how Iran’s National Passive Defense Organization (NPDO) focuses on preparedness, resilience and risk reduction across critical infrastructure, including measures intended to minimize the consequences of hostile operations. The Cyber Defense Command, established under the NPDO in November 2010 following the discovery of Stuxnet, is responsible for strengthening the detection, assessment and response to threats against government, military and critical infrastructure.

The report cites Stuxnet as an early example of malware designed to produce physical effects through attacks on industrial control systems. Discovered in 2010, the malware targeted industrial control systems associated with the Natanz uranium enrichment facility, manipulating centrifuge operations while concealing anomalies. The incident demonstrated how cyber operations could affect physical processes in critical infrastructure. From a defensive perspective, Stuxnet exposed the vulnerability of even isolated infrastructure to sophisticated malware and reinforced the need for stronger protection, resilience and operational preparedness.

“From a defensive perspective, Stuxnet exposed a qualitatively different type of vulnerability by revealing that sophisticated malware could even compromise isolated infrastructure,” according to the report. “This exposure accelerated measures to strengthen protection and resilience against similarly sophisticated operations, while reinforcing the importance of technological self-sufficiency and operational preparedness.”

From an offensive perspective, it noted that Stuxnet revealed that unconventional operations could impose meaningful consequences on a nation-state without requiring conventional engagement. This reinforced cyberspace relevance as an instrument of retaliation and competition, particularly for a state seeking to compensate for conventional disadvantages while preserving flexibility and deniability.

Hydro Kitten, also known as CyberAv3ngers, is identified as an Iranian state-sponsored sabotage-focused group whose targeting centers on internet-exposed industrial control systems, supervisory control and data acquisition systems, OT environments, human-machine interfaces, Internet of Things (IoT) devices and PLCs (programmable logic controllers). The group has compromised Unitronics Vision Series PLCs used across the energy, water and wastewater, healthcare, food and beverage, and manufacturing sectors. The report also identifies targeting of internet-facing PLCs manufactured by Rockwell Automation/Allen-Bradley, Schneider Electric and Siemens. Hydro Kitten has used leased third-party infrastructure and manufacturers’ programming software to connect to misconfigured devices.

The report links Hydro Kitten to the November 2023 incident at the Aliquippa water plant in Pennsylvania. The group compromised a Unitronics PLC-HMI system, resulting in the outage of a pressure-regulation pump on a municipal water supply line. The operators also defaced the device interface with a red screen displaying the CyberAv3ngers name and logo. AttackIQ describes the group’s operational approach as combining opportunistic targeting of exposed industrial systems with politically motivated defacement, disruption and the potential for destructive activity. Its use of IOCONTROL, a Linux-based modular backdoor, extends this concern to critical infrastructure worldwide. The report identifies ICS and SCADA equipment from manufacturers including Baicells, D-Link, Hikvision, Red Lion, Orpak, Phoenix Contact, Teltonika and Unitronics among the devices of interest.

Other groups in the report also have targeting portfolios that include critical infrastructure. Chrono Kitten primarily targets internet-exposed industrial control systems, particularly those operated by oil and gas companies, as well as technology firms and telecommunications providers across Africa, Central Asia and the Middle East. Static Kitten has targeted government and government-adjacent organizations, financial institutions, energy companies, technology and telecommunications providers, and organizations supporting critical infrastructure.

Helix Kitten has maintained access to organizations that provide visibility into sensitive communications and critical infrastructure, supporting sustained intelligence collection through stealth and persistence. The report also identifies Afkar System Yazd Company as a government-linked technology contractor associated with state-sponsored cyber operations. Its employees and associates have reportedly exploited known vulnerabilities to compromise critical infrastructure and support follow-on activity, including ransomware operations.

The report discusses Iran’s use of destructive malware against energy-sector organizations in the Middle East, particularly through Shamoon, also known as DistTrack. Volatile Kitten is associated with the development and deployment of the wiper, which corrupts files and overwrites the Master Boot Record, rendering affected systems unusable. AttackIQ identifies multiple Shamoon campaigns between 2012 and 2016 that caused large-scale disruption and system destruction in targeted environments, particularly in Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates.

Tracer Kitten was identified during investigations into the Shamoon attacks. Although the report does not establish that Tracer Kitten deployed the destructive malware, it notes that at least one administrator system within an organization later targeted by Shamoon had been compromised before the wiper was deployed. AttackIQ says this may indicate that the group played an access or reconnaissance role ahead of destructive activity. The report also identifies cases in which organizations previously compromised by espionage-focused groups were subsequently affected by Shamoon, suggesting that access established for intelligence collection could potentially be repurposed for destructive operations.

Pioneer Kitten has exploited known vulnerabilities in internet-facing infrastructure, particularly VPN appliances, to compromise organizations. Its initial targets included aerospace, energy and utility organizations, while its activities later extended to government, technology and telecommunications entities. The group has also provided access to compromised environments to ransomware affiliates, including BlackCat, NoEscape and Pay2Key.

The report further identifies Iranian-linked entities associated with activity against U.S. critical infrastructure. It connects the Shahid Kaveh Group and Kavosh Security Center with disruptive activity targeting internet-exposed operational technology across U.S. critical infrastructure, including the Water and Wastewater Systems sector. It also links the Shahid Hemmat Group and Mehrsam Andisheh Saz Nik, formerly Mahak Rayan Afraz, to malicious activity targeting U.S. critical infrastructure, including defense industrial base and transportation organizations. These associations should be presented as the report’s assessments rather than as independently confirmed operational responsibility.

Another interesting fact from the AttackIQ report is that contributing to the expansion of Iran’s operational capabilities are government contractors and external organizations that provide specialized expertise and operational support beyond the capacity of state-controlled institutions. “Their functions vary considerably, ranging from software procurement, infrastructure management, and information technology services to cybersecurity expertise, intelligence support, surveillance capabilities, and malware development. While the extent and formality of their relationships with government institutions differ, these organizations collectively supplement the regime’s internal capabilities by providing specialized competencies and organizational flexibility that can be leveraged in support of national objectives.”

It added that such a distributed contractor model increases scalability while creating institutional separation between state-controlled institutions and the specialized organizations supporting operational activity. By distributing technical and operational functions across affiliated organizations, the government can expand access to specialized capabilities without requiring equivalent growth within the state apparatus, while complicating attribution and reinforcing deniability. Rather than functioning as independent operators, these organizations should be understood as capability providers, extending flexibility and operational reach of the broader state-sponsored cyber ecosystem.

Decades of constraints and adaptation have shaped Iran’s state-sponsored cyber capability into a distributed ecosystem of intelligence institutions, military organizations, specialized contractors, and operational adversaries that translate national-security goals into cyber operations.

While this complexity reflects deep cyberspace integration into national security apparatus, it also blurs organizational boundaries and complicates attribution. Within this ecosystem, adversaries vary widely in their organizational significance, operational relevance, and capabilities—categorized into Heavyweight, Lightweight, and Featherweight tiers. Although some groups operate independently while others rely on supporting actors, collaboration allows dependent groups to participate in operations whose requirements and impact exceed their individual capabilities.

Beyond individual capabilities, the relationships, intermediaries, and operational interdependencies connecting these adversaries carry major implications for both retrospective attribution and prospective threat assessment, as they complicate attribution and enable operations beyond historical targeting patterns. Analysts must evaluate these connections based on attribution confidence and supporting evidence rather than assuming interconnected groups pose equal threat levels. Ultimately, assessing Iran’s cyber power requires looking beyond isolated adversaries to account for the sustaining institutions, supporting organizations, and relationships that link them together.
